# Stand Strong

## Stand Strong

Shubha Vilas

## Acknowledgments

Walking through life, I have always seen myself as a student, surrounded by teachers who have touched and enhanced my life immensely. I would like to express my heartfelt gratitude for all that I have been fortunate enough to learn from them.

To name all of them here would be impossible; however, some of the most prominent teachers in my life have been: H.D.G. A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Srila Prabhupada, the founder Acarya of the Hare Krsna Movement, and H.H. Radhanath Swami, the author of the international bestseller The Journey Home – Autobiography of an American Swami.

I am indebted to Valmiki muni, the author of the Ramayana. And Kamba muni, who further enriched the flavor of the original with his amazing poetry. And to Tulsidasji, whose analogies bring out deeper aspects of this epic. I have learnt a lot about the intricacies of the Ramayana from Sri Velukkudi Krishnan Swami and K.S. Narayanacharya, Shri Ramkumar Kinkarji, C. Sita Ramamurti and Sri S. Appalacharyulu. I would also like to appreciate all those who have worked on the website – www.valmikiramayan.net – to make the epic accessible to the world.

My special gratitude to Dr. Shrilekha Hada, for her tireless zeal and invaluable inputs in developing every aspect of the book. She has become an integral part of this series.

I personally owe a million thanks to Meera Menon, whose meticulousness shows through in every sentence of this book, whose thought-provoking enquiries have helped me go deeper into the Ramayana.

A monumental venture like this wouldn’t be possible without constant encouragement from my friends. Particularly, Shadbhuj, Radeshlal, Raas Gopal, Raja Gopal and Nitin Chavan who have supported me in more ways than can be penned. I offer my gratitude hugs to them celebrating the joy of true friendship.

There are few people who have been silently facilitating my writing in numerous ways. Without their constant help, I wouldn’t have been able to write this or any other book. Rekha and Deepak Bharwani Mehul and Neha Sanghvi, Vraj Devi Priya dasi and Shyamras das. Would like to thank them for their silent contributions.

Probably the greatest inspiration has come from the thousands of readers who have enthusiastically written and spoken to me about their appreciation for these books and the life-transformation it has brought in their lives. I consider their wishes to be my primary motivation to keep writing. And of course, my warm thanks to the entire team of Jaico, especially Mr. Akash Shah, Sandhya Iyer, Sanjana Parikh, Nita Satikuwar and Vijay Thakur – their intense efforts and sincere dedication is visible on every page of the book. This book has been a memorable journey with so much contemplation going into making the series more accessible to the world. This journey has been about not only working on a book but also bonding deeply with the Jaico family. I am truly inspired and encouraged to continue this venture in making Ramayana everyone’s life companion with their support.

## Author’s Note

Ramayana – The Game of Life presents an authentic rendition of the epic in an absolutely modern and riveting manner. This book promises to enlighten the loyalists and entertain the modernists. These books are sure to guarantee satisfaction, and will be your life-time companion, effectively interspersing the story of Ramayana with thought-provoking and contemporary life lessons. As you flip the pages, one thought is sure to cross your mind: never has the Ramayana been so applicable in my life!

This series, while holding Valmiki Muni’s epic as a lighthouse, sensitively weaves in analogies and poetic ornamentations from the Kamba Ramayana and Tulsi Das’s Ramacharitramanas. In addition to these, the series also brings in folktales which are called Loka Pramana that are in philosophical alignment with the Ramayana. These folktales bring in background stories that enhance the understanding of the main storyline as well as of the various characters and their moods. Without the knowledge of these tales, the original story would be much less dramatic and comprehensible. Moreover, the series also has deep insights from the great acharyas who have studied the Ramayana thoroughly. Even if it is filled with so many elements, I have made sure that a modern reader does not find it even a little inapplicable or irrelevant in their personal life. In fact, this series shows the reader how these epics have immediate relevance in our lives if presented properly and understood deeply.

Of course, I would like to state here that I have taken some creative liberties to keep the narration riveting and gripping. But rest assured, these creative liberties are extremely minimal and do not, in any way, impede or supersede the original author’s work, or the divinity of the epic and its characters. If anything, these liberties enhance the joy of reading this great work of literature. Below are two examples of the creative liberty I have taken in this series:

In the third book of the series, Adopt Patience, I have introduced a section in which Lakshmana is jotting down in his diary his memories of the day Ravana kidnapped Sita. I specifically chose to do so because I wanted to highlight Lakshmana’s state of mind when two people he loved the most insulted him the most. Though all the facts and details remain the same, just this angle of narration captivatingly brings out the clarity and the maturity with which Lakshmana deals with the situation, helping Rama sensitively throughout the whole episode.

In this book, Stand Strong, I have introduced a dialogue between Hanuman and the vanara Taru. After Rama abruptly walks out without killing Vali, everyone is disturbed by his action. In the original Ramayana, there is no specific clarification about why Rama does this. But the acharyas bring in a philosophical angle to understand Rama’s uncharacteristic behavior. In order to introduce this understanding into the narrative, I took the liberty of adding a dialogue between Hanuman and Taru, where Hanuman (who is considered to be a very evolved personality and a guru) clarifies Rama’s actions.

Stand Strong is, in essence, the Kishkinda Kanda of the Ramayana. This book is all about the events occurring in the monkey kingdom of Kishkinda. With this novel, the speed of the Ramayana – The Game of Life series increases tremendously. The action is constant throughout!

Stand Strong deals with one of the most misunderstood and controversial sections of the Ramayana, the killing of Vali. So many people, since so many years, have been accusing Rama of committing this heinous act. This book gives an in-depth understanding of Rama’s mindset during that time, and clarifies the episode through an active dialogue between Vali and Rama.

As I’ve mentioned in every book, each character is a hero in their own way as they have the power to grab the reader’s attention in different ways, at different levels and during different situations. In this book, the reader will be introduced to so many new characters that will enhance their understanding of life and human psychology. As the subtitle of the book aptly says, keeping faith and conquering fear, the characters in this book show us how to do exactly that during tough times and challenging circumstances.

Shubha Vilas

## Chapter 1

Fear on the Mountain

sugriva’s silent surrender

“This can’t be true!” Nala repeatedly slapped his chest to quieten his pounding heart. Panic-stricken by what he had just spotted from his perch atop the discreet one-man watchtower on the tallest tree on the Rishimukh mountains, Nala shed his caution and leaped on to the biggest branch of the nearest tree, blowing the warning bugle in the process. This was an emergency! He had to alert his master! His life didn’t matter, his master’s did. He wanted to give his master as much as time as possible to run – run for his life!1

The mighty Nala leaped from one tree to another, zipping through the foliage toward the peak of the Rishimukh mountains, and his powerful thrusts and swings left a trail of wrecked trees trembling to their very roots. As Nala darted uphill, his eyes remained transfixed on the fast approaching source of fear downhill.2

Nila, meanwhile, had barely noticed the same movements below when the unmistakable warning from Nala’s bugle pierced through from the other side of the Rishimukh mountains. From his watch position atop a rock jutting out of the steepest cliff of the mountain range, Nila lunged into the gorge. That was the only way to reach his master: down this deepest gorge and up the steepest hill in the mountain range. On a normal day, Nila would carefully head down the cliff, tasting fruits and berries along the way. But today wasn’t normal.

Despite that urgent jump, Nila landed with perfect accuracy on the back of a wild elephant ambling nearby. His fall was cushioned by the fatty girth, but his tremendous weight crushed the matriarch to instant death. The death of the leader sent the elephant herd into a tizzy, but Nila had no time to spare. He had to reach his master at any cost. He began sprinting uphill now, toward the peak. Nila zipped across and through the trees with the trampling herd in tow, causing mayhem among the ferocious jungle animals. Caught off guard by the chaos, some animals chose to flee the vicinity, whereas others chose to attack. Lion or tiger, Nila kicked any animal that crossed his path. When it comes to life and death, policies of peacetime need not be followed. The five of them – Sugriva, Nila, Nala, Taru and the silent one – had mutually agreed not to meddle around with the eco-system of the Rishimukh mountains and peacefully cohabit with the wild animals here. But today, nothing mattered but his service to his master.3

Stationed at the rear of the mountain was Taru. His job was to keep a watch on aerial attacks. From his spot, he had a clear view of the entire Rishimukh range but more than that, he had a clear view of anything or anyone that descended from the air. He looked upward: clear skies, no threat whatsoever. But the moment he heard Nala’s bugle, the crescendo of his heartbeat stiffened him to attention. Taru began to reassess his surroundings; he surveyed every nook and every bit of the firmament. Nothing, nothing yet! Then the sudden eruption of the sound of scrambling animals made him realize that Nila was running uphill. “This has to be an inland attack.” With that assuredness, Taru began to run to the front of the mountain toward his master.

From the corner of his eyes, Nila could see movements on either side of the hill. All three climbers reached the plateaued peak exactly at the same time. Their panic-stricken urgent eyes met and together Nala, Taru and Nila hastened onward up the hill.

The three exhausted friends pushed their limits and drew every bit of the strength they had to reach as fast as they could toward their master. There he was, their master, pensive and forlorn, seated on a rock right in the middle of the flat peak. Nala, Taru and Nila collapsed on their knees around their master. Together they spoke with their hands clasped, “Master, we are in trouble!”

Silence was all they got. The crisis cry was completely lost on their master. Sugriva’s face was buried into his enormous hands, his elbows resting on his strong knees. He stayed there motionless and expressionless, staring at the ground between his feet. He was shattered. The trio was feeling sorry for him now. After all the humiliation he had gone through over the past few years, how could he be expected to live with dignity and confidence? Sugriva was tired. Tired of running. Tired of hiding. Tired of his life!

“I am dead!” A weak moan escaped his parched throat. Nothing more needed conveying. All hope was lost now. The leader of the wonder-monkeys was at his lowest ebb now.4

Some very painful memories flooded back into Sugriva’s mind. Abuses, losses, blows and most of all, kicks… The memories were replete with repeated insults hurled at him by his elder brother Vali,5 and the worst were those relentless and perfectly timed kicks.

Normally, meditating at the feet of the elder brother would be auspicious, but for Sugriva this brought endless trauma and physical, emotional and mental pain.6

Vali had been plundering Sugriva’s abode aerially and had never dared venture from the base of the hill. But today, he had found not one, but two ways to attack from the foothills of the Rishimukh. Now Sugriva could see him everywhere, all around and above him. He could even see his brother in his absence!7

“Now I am surely dead!” Sugriva was now merely an angst-ridden, helpless wonder-monkey.

advisor lakshmana

After watching the pure-hearted Shabari consumed by her yogic powers only to return to her beloved guru Matanga rishi, the divine brothers, Rama and Lakshmana silently walked out of the hermitage. Conflicting thoughts ran through Lord Rama’s mind. He was experiencing such a contradiction for the first time in his life. He was both satisfied and dissatisfied with His interaction with this saintly woman – satisfied with her love and devotion and dissatisfied with its limited existence. At exactly the same time, as if by telepathy, Lakshmana thought that never before had he seen Rama eat so many fruits at one go, as if He had been hungry for ages. Even when He did stop, it seemed Rama had reluctantly forced Himself from eating more. For those moments Rama was with Shabari, Lakshmana realized that He had completely forgotten His agony and was experiencing a type of satisfaction that was impossible to comprehend.8

As they walked down the pathway that led to the celestial Pampa lake, realities began to return. Lakshmana considered this short interaction with Shabari a welcome and necessary break for Lord Rama who had been forlorn since Sita’s abduction. As they neared the lake, Lakshmana noticed a distinct change in Rama’s expressions. Something had triggered His distress.

Not one, but three things in the Pampa lake9 stirred Rama’s memories of Sita. The lotus reminded Him of Her face, the black tulip reminded Him of Her eyes and the fish reminded Him of Her side-glances.10

Lotus, Tulip, Fish and the Spiritual Aspirant

A lotus though born in water keeps gazing at the sun above. Water below indicates spiritual experiences and gazing at the sun above indicates contemplation on the Lord. Like a lotus, a spiritual aspirant can progress by staying amid a spiritual lifestyle while constantly contemplating on the Lord.

Black tulips derive their life-sustaining nourishment by drinking the mellow light of the moon and as they are nourished, they slowly open their petals. Like a black tulip, a spiritual aspirant hankers for the soothing grace and guidance of his or her guru to blossom spiritually.

Fishes sparkle only because of their connection with the element of water. Their sustenance and nourishment are both present in water. Like a fish, a spiritual aspirant derives sustenance (dharakam) from the guru’s grace and nourishment (poshakam) from God’s grace. Thus, a spiritual aspirant sparkles in devotion only because of complete surrender and submission to the element of grace.

As Rama confessed his feelings to Lakshmana, He noticed the fishes trying their best to hide from His vision as soon as they realized that they reminded Him of Sita’s glances. He threw a stone into the lake in anger. The stone landed in the middle of the pond, creating a series of concentric ripples. These ripples were akin to Rama’s pulsating heart, going outward in search of the missing Sita who was at the center of His heart.

When watching the serene lake became too painful for Rama, He looked around only to be encountered with more elements that aggravated His pain. All around the lake, as birds of different hues sang their songs, they seemed like the cacophonous gathering of people speaking different languages and none understanding the other. Rama felt that although they were so chirpy, none was willing to share with Him the whereabouts of His beloved Sita. They were so immersed in their own selfish lives, unempathetic toward other’s tribulations.11

The melodies emitting from the kokila birds that would invigorate Him when He was with Sita now only contributed to His agony. The dance of the peacocks that would be the highlight of a day if He were by Sita’s side now only increased his frustration.

Rama looked at the dancing peacocks and thought, “You can afford to dance in joy with your wings spread because no rakshasa has stolen your peahen.”

The cooing peacocks seemed to mock Rama. He stared at the colorful plumage marked with eyespots and thought, “How fortunate is the peacock! It has thousands of eyes to look at its beloved.” The next instant He became sad. “I cannot even see My beloved with both my eyes!” The next instant Rama became very angry that despite its many eyes, the peacock still wasn’t telling Him anything about Sita’s whereabouts.12

Rama looked around with his tear-filled eyes, almost as if searching for His lost Sita. Just then, a gentle breeze, pregnant with the sweet scent of the lotus and saugandika flowers, ruffled his thoughts. Rama winced in pain and tears welled up even more. He flopped onto the ground on His knees in anguish. “The vasanta ritu (spring season) and the god of love have conspired to kill me today. This cool, fresh breeze from the Malaya hills laden with heavenly fragrances is supposed to relieve distress and mitigate fatigue. But I find the cool wind extremely hot because it fans the heat of the fire of separation from my beloved. Thieves steal things that are visible and outside, but this breeze is like a subtle thief, expertly robbing Me of My peace of mind.” Unable to bear more pain, Rama broke down into uncontrollable sobs.13

On seeing his distraught brother, Lakshmana kneeled down and gently squeezed Rama’s broad shoulder, trying to ease His unbearable pain. Lakshmana’s touch had its desired effect. Rama’s sobbing decreased but his lamentation did not. “Lakshmana look at My life!

It is just a by-product of My bad karma. First I lost My kingdom – My greatest opportunity to serve. Then I lost My father – My greatest wealth. And now I’ve lost Sita – My life itself. There was a day when I had both rajyalakshmi and bhagyalakshmi. When I lost rajyalakshmi, I consoled myself saying I at least still have bhagyalakshmi by my side. And now that I have lost my bhagyalakshmi too, how do I pacify Myself? Today I have truly become a beggar. How will I explain this situation to my heartbroken father-in-law, Janak maharaj? How can I tell him that I won his daughter by performing the herculean task of breaking the Pinaka bow, but lost Her by failing to perform my duty of protecting Her? How can I return to Ayodhya and face My mother Kaushalya? How can I explain to the people of Ayodhya that I lost their daughter-in-law? If a king can’t protect his own wife, how can he possibly protect the kingdom? Lakshmana, I don’t want to return to Ayodhya, you go alone. I will remain in this wilderness forever! I can’t blame anyone or anything for all My losses except Myself and My past actions for My present condition. There is no one more unfortunate than me in the three worlds today.”14

Finding his brother completely broken down and terribly helpless, Lakshmana stepped up into a higher role for the second time; this time, from servant to advisor. The last time Lakshmana had made the transition to help Rama deal with anger, this time it was to see Him through His depression. Towering above Rama’s emotional challenge, Lakshmana began to speak.15

“Love is the basis of life, but excessive love is the cornerstone of self-destruction. Life is best lived when balanced. Every quality must stay within the upper and lower limits. Confidence over the upper limit morphs into arrogance and below the lower limit becomes skepticism. Ambition beyond the upper limit changes to greed and below the lower limit leads to indifference. Similarly, when love surpasses the upper limit, frustration takes over, and when it falls below the lower limit, insensitivity reigns. When a virtue transcends limits, it causes imbalance and effects negative consequences in life.

Too much oil in a lamp extinguishes a burning wick; similarly, excessive remembrance of past happiness results in a frustrated present. Right now, the most important attribute You need is composure. Reminiscence of Your past will only cause suffering and cloud Your mind and judgment. Attachment to an object of love leads to prudent action, but excessive attachment leads to frustrated inaction.16

O Rama, please try to recollect the three most important things to keep in mind before embarking on a tough mission. First, nothing can be achieved with a weak mind; second, nothing is impossible with intelligent enthusiasm and third, the courageous never accept defeat.

The magic formula that will help us recover Sita is earnest enthusiasm. Sorrow takes the throne when enthusiasm relinquishes it. Your sorrow has made You weak and forgetful of Your great selfcontrol. Your sorrow is actually distracting You from Your love for Sita. You can show the magnitude of Your love for Sita through the intensity of enthusiasm in Your endeavor to find her.17

O Rama, today I commit to You in Your endeavor to find Sita. Even if Ravana hides in the nether worlds, I will find him. His death will be by my hands. The one who has caused You so much agony cannot survive for long. Even if he re-enters his mother’s womb, I will trace him and slaughter him mercilessly.

O divine brother, set aside Your grief and summon Your sharp, disciplined intelligence at your command. Become aware of Your own greatness. We need every ounce of that on this monumental mission we are embarking upon.”

Lakshmana’s words inspired Rama to wipe off his tears and embrace His brother. Lakshmana encouraged Rama to bathe in the cool waters of the Pampa lake to drown his fatigue and rejuvenate himself. As Rama entered the cool waters of the lake, the whole lake warmed up, just as water warms up when a blacksmith drops a burning hot iron rod in it. Similarly, the waters of the Pampa lake warmed up with the heat of separation emanating from Rama’s body. He closed His eyes and let go of all His anxieties and fears in the waters. When He emerged from the lake, He was a changed person. Gone were his fears, worries, anxieties and frustrations – instead, there was hope in his eyes.

Just as He stepped out of the waters and readied Himself for the onward journey, a crow began to caw very enthusiastically. Rama gazed in the direction of the sound and noticed a crow cawing loudly from its perch atop a nearby tree. A smile flashed across Rama’s beautiful face after a long time. Lakshmana was happy to see the transformation in his brother. Rama walked up to His brother, placed one hand on his shoulder and pointed to the crow with the index finger of His other hand. “This is the good omen proclaiming that very soon we will find Sita!”

The brothers embraced each other with newfound hope.18

hope to the mountain

*Sambhramah tyajataam esa*

*Bhayam na iha asti*

“Do not worry! There is no fear on this mountain!”

A thunderous voice bellowed from somewhere high above and behind them. The three downcast monkeys sitting on the plateau along with their master Sugriva looked up toward the confident voice. A hint of a hopeful smile flickered on their faces despite fear pounding within their hearts.

With fists clenched and arms akimbo, standing behind them on a gigantic rock was a mammoth figure. Every inch of his chiseled body and rippling muscles indicated years and years of disciplined lifestyle. He wore a yellow silken garment around his waist. His torso was bare except for the gold ornaments that decorated his broad and deep chest and the set of armlets and bracelets clasping his arms. He looked powerful yet regal.

“Hanuman!” They exclaimed together.19

Smiling at them, Hanuman, the silent one, jumped off the steep rock and landed right amid them kneeling on one leg, supporting himself with one hand on the ground. Standing up, Hanuman offered his respects to Sugriva, his master.

“There is no fear of Vali on this mountain anymore.” Hanuman continued to assure his friends. “As long as we are by your side and the curse of Matanga rishi favors you, no danger can come to you on this mountain from Vali.”

Hanuman’s words triggered deep-seated emotions in Sugriva and sent him down memory lane. He began to recollect the most excruciatingly painful series of events he had ever experienced in his life, both physically and emotionally traumatizing.

From the moment he discovered Rishimukh mountain – the only place in the world Vali could not enter because of Matanga muni’s curse – Sugriva had settled down there in peace. But peace was short-lived as Vali had found a unique way of troubling him right from the next day. Vali’s daily routine was to chant the gayatrimantra at the confluence of the four oceans. He timed his daily prayers to such precision that anyone could know exactly where he would be at what time of the day. As he jumped from the southern to the northern ocean, he spotted something below by chance. A wicked idea came to his mind. His intent would no doubt delay his prayers by a few seconds every time he jumped, but Vali thought it was all worth it. After all, everything in his life had gone awry the previous day.

Vali zoomed down toward the Rishimukh mountain – the very mountain he dreaded approaching since he heard of Matanga muni’s curse on him. But this risk was worth taking. He would not trade it for all the pleasures in the world. Nothing would bring him more pleasure – sadistic pleasure!20

There he was! Vali planned his landing in such a way that he did not land on the mountain – no, he did not even touch a single pebble on the mountain. Instead, he landed straight on his brother Sugriva’s head with all his weight.

“WHAM!”

The impact of the kick was so severe that Sugriva toppled over and fell to the ground, rolling over several times. His eyesight was blurred, and his head was reeling. He had no clue about what had hit him. Just before being walloped, from the corner of his eyes he could see something that seemed like a pair of legs. By the time he gathered himself and stood up, his associates had congregated by his side with worried looks on their faces. It was obvious that they hadn’t noticed what had happened to their master. They helped Sugriva to his stone seat again. Ensuring that everything was all right with their master, they left to investigate the matter. Nila remained by his master’s side to ensure his safety. Hanuman hadn’t joined them by yet. Sugriva sat holding his throbbing head with his hands.

“WHAM!”

Sugriva toppled off his seat and rolled to the ground. Nila glanced up just in time to see Vali flying off with a smirk on his face. He ran up to his master and picked him up. This time Sugriva knew what had happened. Nila didn’t have to say a word. Sugriva had been thinking about the timing of the second kick, and he had realized that it was in alignment with Vali’s jumps during his rendition of the gayatrimantra. In exactly the same time, Vali landed on Sugriva’s head for a third time and yet again in the next frame for the fourth. All of Sugriva associates – Nala, Nila and Taru – helplessly watched as their master kept being pounded by his brother repeatedly eight more times.21

From that day forth, Vali jumped on Sugriva’s head a dozen times divided uniformly and predictably between the three gayatrimantra timings. Everyone knew the exact time Vali’s kicks would land on their master’s head and how often, but they knew nothing about how to stop it. What made the trauma even worse for Sugriva was that Vali abused him during the three times of the day that the influence of his father Surya, the sun god, was the highest. What made it even more agonizing was that Surya had to watch helplessly as his worshipper kicked his son repeatedly.

Sugriva continued feeling neglected and let down by his father, but Surya was in fact arranging for a solid cover for his weak son. The cover did appear to him in a very appropriate manner, but it was almost dramatic. One day, Sugriva was mentally preparing for his brother’s kicks. He sat very taut, his hands on his temples and eyes tightly closed. At exactly the stipulated time, instead of a powerful kick, what landed on Sugriva’s scalp was just the tip of Vali’s toe, which had frozen. Sugriva, meanwhile, bracing for the worst was on the verge of panic. He waited for a few seconds, and when nothing really happened, Sugriva was left confused. He reluctantly opened his eyes. It was the most comical thing he had ever seen. Vali was helplessly dangling in the air, his foot just above where his head was moments ago. He was frozen; he tried his best to pull away from whatever held him, but failed. This was the first time Sugriva had seen any sort of helplessness on the face of his arrogant and haughty brother.

Sugriva got up and stepped back to admire the hero who had dared to grab Vali’s ankles. Although a wonder-monkey like him, the look of confidence on his face and effulgence of his persona indicated that this was a monkey of a different genre.

Sugriva had heard stories about the valor and might he exhibited from the day he was born, but he had never had an opportunity to interact with him. Yet here he was, performing yet another herculean task: stalling Vali.22

A word escaped from Sugriva’s mouth: “Hanuman!”

Vali wriggled and wriggled, but Hanuman’s grip on his ankles was so strong that he could barely move an inch. Hanuman’s next actions caused Vali’s face to contort in panic.

“No don’t do this! Leave me, alone. What have I done to you?” Vali began to plead as he realized Hanuman was slowly dragging him down, forcing him to land on the Rishimukh Mountain and touch the ground. Vali realized that he had to act fast before he could fall victim to Matanga rishi’s curse. His ego did not permit him to say what was best under the circumstances. So he closed his eyes and channeled his entire energy toward his legs so that he could break free of Hanuman’s grip. By now, his legs were dangerously close to the ground. A mighty roar pierced through and shook the hearts of all the spectator monkeys as Vali made one final attempt to escape Hanuman’s grip. Nothing happened!

Now Vali’s mind was in turmoil. He had only one choice left: to beg!

With folded hands and tears coursing down his face, Vali implored. “I beg you, please leave me. I promise you that I will never return to this place or harm Sugriva. Please spare my life this time.” How Vali hated himself now! Those relentless tears could no longer do anything for his crushed ego. Then suddenly, he stopped weeping. Hanuman had stopped dragging him to the ground. Such relief: pride wounded but life saved!23

Hanuman stared at him menacingly. He said in a stern voice, “OK. I’m letting you go this once. But if you were to ever come back to harm my master, Sugriva, let this be known that you will never return. You might as well order your ministers to prepare your funeral pyre before heading this way.”

Hanuman released his grip and Vali soared into the sky. From up there, Vali took one last vengeful look at Hanuman and disappeared.

From that day forth, Sugriva was in complete awe of Hanuman and full of gratitude. Every time Hanuman spoke, he found his words inspiring, motivating and hope giving.24

“Vali will never come back to this mountain. He wouldn’t dare. Now it’s not just Matanga muni’s curse that he has to fear, it’s my presence too, O master. Remember that you are the son of Surya, the all-pervading sun god. There is great expectation from you as a future leader of the wonder-monkey army. By trembling in fear, you are displaying an ordinary, fickle, monkey-like nature not befitting a leader of your stature. The more cowardice you exhibit, the smaller you will remain in your own eyes, let alone how that will seem to your subordinates. Remember that the body language of a king reveals his mind-set. Keep your intellect firm, control your flickering mind, and ooze confidence. An unfocused king cannot expect dedicated followership.”

After these words of wisdom, Hanuman abruptly stopped speaking. He always tried his best to balance his multiple roles as Sugriva’s servant, minister and advisor. Although he found many faults in his master, he chose not to point them out as a humble servant. Only when he felt that a situation mandated that he take on the role of advisor for the safety and future stability of his master, did Hanuman speak up truthfully and yet cautiously.

Sugriva nodded, seeming to understand what Hanuman was trying to convey. But the next moment, he turned in the direction of the new problem that was approaching him steadily. That very moment, a gush of worries flooded Sugriva’s mind, ruthlessly breaking the dam of wisdom that Hanuman had just built. The wonder-monkey leader became paranoid all over again, and his hands began to tremble.25

“Look at their lengthy arms. Look at their broad eyes. Look at their divine bows and arrows. Just one look at these two beings instills fear in me. Who won’t be afraid of such formidable enemies? Look at their attire carefully. Note how much contradiction there is in their personalities, their attire and their weaponry. They look like princes, but are dressed as sages. Dreadlocks and bows and arrows; this unusual combination is a first. I have never seen anything like this before. If the combination is so incompatible, their thought process must also be highly controversial.26

“I am sure Vali has drafted these two divine, handsome men to kill me. Kings always have many mysterious allies to help them carry out their ingenious plans. Hanuman, if my premonition is true, then no one can save me now and I no longer have a place that’s safe enough.”

Following Sugriva’s gaze, Hanuman walked ahead toward the edge of the cliff and carefully gaped down at the two handsome men walking along the edges of the Pampa lake, heading toward the Rishimukh mountain. Hanuman immediately made some mental notes about these two stunning personalities. “They must be in a disturbed state of mind; their expressions and body language gives that away. They look tired from walking for too long, but they are pushing themselves ahead with a sense of urgency. They must be in search of something valuable that they seem to have lost, because their eyes are darting in all directions, carefully scrutinizing everything around them. Dharma is oozing from every pore of their bodies.” The pair was the most virtuous Hanuman had ever seen. In fact, their luster made them appear godly. “Could they be gods in human form? Divine beings surely cannot engage in iniquitous acts.” Hanuman had come to his conclusion about the two hermitlike men walking toward the mountain.

He walked back toward the disturbed Sugriva and laid forth his observations about the men. “O Sugriva, look at the two of them carefully. They seem to be karuna sagara, oceans of mercy. They don’t seem to be indulging in acts of violence for their own sadistic pleasure or to facilitate anyone else’s adharmic whims. Their weapons appear to be the kind meant for protecting the weak. O Sugriva, there is no need to fear them. Get a grip over your mind with steady intelligence. A king under constant panic attacks cannot gain the confidence of his subjects.”27

Gulping down his doubts and insecurities, Sugriva tried to speak logically rather than tragically. He saw merit in Hanuman’s words but was unwilling to believe all of it. Sugriva thought of a plan.

“Hanuman, I can’t rely on intuition anymore. When my life is at stake, I need clear proof before drawing any conclusions. I want you to use your guise changing ability to ascertain the intentions of these personalities. I specifically want you to take the form of a genuine wandering sanyasi. Any dharmatma cannot possibly hide his intentions from a saintly soul. Use your expertise in analyzing human psychology to decode their minds. Please utilize profusely that one tool that never fails to win hearts: praise.

“When used profusely, praise can melt even the hardest hearts as snow does when exposed to the warmth of the sun. A melted heart becomes vulnerable. Once influenced by your eulogies when they drop their guard, lead them hypnotically into the labyrinth of your words. Try to extract as much information as possible about their mission, their master and their mind-set. Closely study their facial expressions, their subtle gestures, their choice of words, their eye movements, levels of stiffness in their limbs and other subtle nuances. Hanuman, my father had told me that there wasn’t a greater expert than you in these deep mysterious human sciences.28

“An impeccable disguise is imperative, especially if these people are indeed Vali’s agents, of which there is a great probability. They will then naturally be scouting for wonder-monkeys in this area and obviously wouldn’t suspect a wandering sanyasi. In fact, they may decide to use you to gather information about my whereabouts. Use that as an opportunity to converse with them and win their hearts with your charm. Once you have their confidence, slowly transform their malice toward me into goodwill by pouring the message of my goodness into their ears. Just as the wind blows away dark clouds that cover the moon, O son of the wind god, dispel all doubts about me that Vali may have planted in their minds.

“While conversing with them, ensure that you are constantly facing in my direction. “Deftly make use of our secret sign language to give me hints about the information that you are gathering from them. Even if I receive a fraction of a sign from you that these two warriors are related to Vali, I will begin fleeing in the other direction. You engage them sufficiently so that I can head toward the mountains.

“Hanuman, I have full faith in your abilities and your sharp intelligence. My life is dependent on these. Please follow my instructions carefully and bring me good news soon.”

Hanuman heard every word Sugriva spoke, processed them carefully and understood their essence. He then closed his eyes, folded his palms and murmured an incantation. His four other associates looked at him with great respect. His divine qualities made him their natural hero. Right at that moment, the valorous monkeyhero disappeared and in his place stood an old and frail ascetic, with a gray, tattered quilt wrapped around him. He had a stick in his hand and a subtle smile as he walked away from the group with a reassuring wave of the hand.

The four wonder-monkeys looked on in awe and watched their now unrecognizable colleague disappear into the steep valley below.29

Master’s own disciple

“These mountains are considered so holy by the sages! People say that when Lord Brahma created this mountain, he predicted that if anyone were to sleep on this mountain and dream of gold, on waking up the person would actually find that gold. No sinful person is ever allowed to step on the rocks of these mountains,” Rama explained to Lakshmana as both stood staring in awe at the Rishimukh mountain peak that majestically soared before them.

Lakshmana held his hand to shield his eyes.“O brother, as we stand beneath this mountain, Kabandha’s prophetic words are ringing in my ears. Now I am very eager for our rendezvous with these wonder-monkeys. I am also curious to know why they are called wonder-monkeys and what must be so wonderful about them. I have been trying to decode the word vanara right from the moment he mentioned it. He said that the word vanara is a rhetoric question, ‘Are you human?’ What would that mean? Why would someone ask a monkey such a question?”

Lakshmana kept analyzing these words and the hidden meaning they held, and Rama just smiled. When Lakshmana saw Rama’s trademark smile, he knew that his brother knew more than He was going to share with him at this point. Rama always waited for the right time to reveal the right things. And whenever He did that, it always seemed to be the perfect time to understand that particular truth. Of course, that was always in retrospect. But at present, it was always irritating when He refused to reveal something He knew even though He could see the confusion in others’ minds. But of course, Lakshmana knew better than to probe his brother when He smiled that way. He resigned to the fact that he would have to wait for the right moment.

As Lakshmana got lost in his own thoughts, Rama walked a bit ahead on the trail that Shabari had shown them. He suddenly froze.

When Lakshmana raced up to Him, he overheard Rama whisper under His breath. “This doesn’t make any sense at all!”

Lakshmana followed Rama’s eyes. He was astounded by what he saw. Standing a little further away was an old, frail, diminutive sanyasi with a begging bowl in one hand and a walking stick in the other. He was slouching and had to brace himself with the help of the stick. Around his neck was a string of wooden beads that seemed rubbed to a shine through years of use.

Rama was right! This certainly didn’t make any sense. What was a sanyasi begging for in a dense and uninhabited forest? Their eyes were fixed on the elderly sanyasi as he wobbled straight toward them.30

Suddenly, the sanyasi’s knees began shaking. The shiver went up his spine and his body began to quake. Soon his arms were also trembling, then his head and eventually every bone of his body began to melt inside him. Tears welled up in his eyes and flowed like torrents. The stick and begging bowl had long fallen to the ground. He had no idea what was happening to him. From the moment his eyes met the stranger’s eyes, something had transpired that manifested as these uncontainable symptoms.

In a few moments, the quaking settled down, and he was able to think more objectively. Embarrassed, he picked up his stick and bowl and walked up to the strangers. As he observed them from close quarters, he began to notice things that startled him. Many wild animals lingered around the two warriors, and every now and then, one of them would walk up to them and nuzzle them with great affection. It appeared as if these wild beings were treating these humans as their cubs. It was not just the wild animals, even trees and plants followed suit. To the beggar, it seemed as if every tree or plant the two heroes were passing automatically bent toward them, as if offering their salutations to their guests. In fact, even pebbles and rocks seemed to be softening themselves under their touch. “Just who are these two?” The mendicant wondered. “Why did the forest creatures have so much reverence for them? Why am I so helplessly captivated by their beauty? Why do I feel such immense love overpowering my body?” Hanuman, the sanyasi in disguise, had no answers.31

He inched toward the handsome heroes and stopped right in front of them in total admiration of their divine beauty. The only way to allay the doubts that plagued him was to pose intelligent questions to these warrior-like sages or sage-like warriors. Holding his palms together, still holding on to his pot and stick, Hanuman bowed in respect.

As Hanuman bent his head, Rama had doubts about this sanyasi. “Why would such an exalted sanyasi offer his respects to us, young warriors? Something isn’t right here.”

As Rama wondered, Hanuman spoke up. “You look like kings, but are dressed as sages. You have the sharpness of courageous warriors, carry dazzling weapons that skilled heroes do and have the divine luster of evolved souls. You seem to be complete misfits in this impenetrable forest with your conflicting personality traits. Why do you tread these hard forest grounds with your soft tender feet?” Hanuman then turned toward Rama and spoke specifically to Him. “Your lengthy elephantine hands that end in Your spherical shoulders are worthy of being decorated with all types of priceless ornaments. But why are they undecorated? And why is Your right shoulder left open and exposed to the elements?”32

Silence – an eerie silence!

The strangers simply stared at him as if he was some specimen under observation. Their eyes seemed able to see through his farce, almost as if penetrating his external garb and staring right into his bare soul.

Even as Hanuman continued to analyze them, the brothers were cross-analyzing him. With his head slightly tilted toward Lakshmana, Rama whispered into his ears, while staring at the stranger in front of them. “Ascetics wouldn’t beg for alms in an uninhabited forest. They wouldn’t talk so much unless questioned. His probing questions definitely reveal a hidden motive. This is either a disguised demon or a clever messenger. This meeting is not coincidental, it is orchestrated.”

Noticing doubt in their eyes, Hanuman realized that the reason behind their silence was probably that they doubted his identity. Holding out his begging bowl, he said, “I am a sanyasi begging for alms. I subsist on alms offered by anyone who walks into this area. According to the status of the person, I request for alms. So may I know your identity please?”

On hearing this, Rama asked the sanyasi a question that left him stunned. “If you are really a beggar then what is a diamond necklace doing around your neck?

If you already have such a priceless jewel, what is the need for you to subsist on begging? You seem to be too strange to be a beggar!” Rama searched deeply for the mendicant’s reaction with his curious and alert eyes.33

His eyes bulged out! He felt the ground below his legs slipping away. He was bewildered. “You could actually see my necklace! I can’t believe this! How could you see my necklace?”

Now it was Rama’s turn to be surprised. Why was this sanyasi so flabbergasted about his necklace being spotted? He turned to Lakshmana, who shrugged shoulders and looked confused. Hanuman immediately turned to Lakshmana. “Can you see my diamond necklace?”

“What diamond necklace? I can only see a string of wooden beads. What are you talking about?” Lakshmana was completely clueless.

With tears streaming down his cheeks, Hanuman fell on his knees, dropped his stick and bowl, folded his hands in reverence and gazed intently at Rama through his moist eyes. “I have been waiting for this moment all my life! My mother was right when she said that I would never have to go looking for my master and that my master would come looking for me!”

The disguise faded, and in place of the frail old sanyasi beggar was, kneeling on one foot, a well-built wonder-monkey. Still weeping, the wonder-monkey gazed at Rama’s feet with great devotion. Bending his bulky body, he placed his head on Rama’s feet, wetting them with his tears.34

As his head remained on his divine master’s feet, his mind zoomed into an unforgettable event in the distant past that enabled him to recognize this beautiful milestone in his present life.

shower of blessings

“The sun has gone missing!” The heavenly realms were in utter chaos.

Everything was fine until a few minutes ago. For the first time, darkness, total blackout engulfed the universe. Except for the radiance of Agni, the fire god, there was absolute darkness everywhere. The celestial residents began scampering hither and thither, bumping into each other and escalating the confusion.

The king of the heavens, Indra, was extremely angry. He stood up from his throne and shouted everyone down. He demanded there be order in his court. He wanted details in chronological sequence before drawing conclusions. The celestials who had witnessed the recent happenings shared their observations. Indra’s eyes popped out of the sockets.

“Impossible! An infant monkey gobbled up the mighty sun!” Indra paced around the courtroom punching his fist into his palm. “You want me to believe that this infant monkey came searching for a fruit to quench his hunger and swallowed the sun because he thought it was a big fruit? And not just that, when Rahu came to attack the sun, the innocent infant monkey considering him a black fruit, gobbled him up too? These seem to be nothing but fantasy tales for toddlers to hear.”

Mounting on his white elephant carrier, Airavata, Indra reached the epicenter of the chaos. The scene that greeted him sent a shiver down his spine. As soon as the infant monkey saw Airavata, he jumped toward him to grab him. He thought it to be yet another white fruit. Seeing the infant monkey’s steely grip on Airavata’s trunk, Indra jumped off its back and simultaneously threw his celebrated vajra or thunderbolt weapon at the unsuspecting juvenile. The powerful weapon hit the infant monkey, Anjeneya, right on his jawbone.

“CRACCKK!”

Anjeneya swooned. His eyes rolled back, and he fell backward toward the earth. As he fell, his mouth opened and the trapped Surya and Rahu escaped. Once again, light returned to the universe and the all-pervading darkness abated.

All the celestial residents cheered and praised the might of their savior and leader, King Indra. No sooner did they begin to return toward their chores, than something unexpected happened again.

They were choking and gasping. Everyone began to collapse on their knees, holding their dry throats. There was no air! There was a total vacuum! Where had all the air gone now? Calamity after calamity today!

The confused celestials turned toward Indra. The equally confused Indra turned toward Brahma. The clear-minded Brahma turned toward the earth and looked intently toward a cave on a mountaintop. Instantly everyone realized that the cause of the problem, and naturally the solution to that problem lay right there. They proceeded along with Lord Brahma toward that desolate cave.35

As they rushed hastily toward the cave, the intelligent four-headed creator explained that the monkey child that Indra had recently attacked was actually Anjeneya, the son of Vayu, the wind god. He was born through the monkey queen Anjana and the monkey king Kesari. At the right time, Vayu intercepted the free fall of Anjeneya and took him into this cave. The sad father was disappointed that all his celestial colleagues had chosen to treat his innocent son so cruelly, so he protested by sucking out all the air from the universe.

Lord Brahma stood beside the unconscious monkey-child Anjeneya and caressed him with his healing touch. The innocent monkeychild slowly opened his eyes and gazed in wonder at the galaxy of celestials crowded around him. He had no idea what all of this meant. On seeing Indra and Surya beside him, it slowly began to dawn on him that all the most powerful celestials had gathered around him out of concern. A weak smile took shape on his broken chin. He folded his hands in reverence and gazed at all of them.

Lord Brahma spoke up first. “O Anjeneya! I bless you that you will live a fantastically long life. You will live as long as I live.”

Hearing Brahma’s magnanimous blessing, all the demigods showered Anjeneya with profuse blessings with an open heart.

“Immunity from any sort of fire hazard!” said Agni, the fire god.

“Speed!” said Vayu.

Yamaraj, the god of death offered “perfect health and everlasting youth!”

“Victory in every battle, small or big,” was what Kubera, the celestial treasurer, offered.

“Resilience to harm from any water bodies,” was the blessing of Varuna, the water god.

“Protection against any kind of dangerous weapons!” offered Vishwakarma, the celestial architect.

Surya, the sun god, offered him an interesting benediction, “Unsurpassed wisdom! You will be radiantly brilliant like me. I also offer you the ability to change your form at will.”

Dhanvantari, the physician of the heavens, offered him a very useful blessing. “Any medicine you offer with your hands to anyone will have a million times more effect than usual. You will have a healing touch!”

Brihaspati, the guru of the demigods, spoke up. “O heroic Anjeneya, I offer you the knowledge of Ayurveda by which you will know the nature of every plant, tree, fruit and flower in the world. You will not only know the intrinsic nature of every tree in creation but also its medicinal effects and properties.”

Finally, Indra, the king of the demigods came forward with a sheepish smile, well aware that he had permanently damaged this child’s jaw. “O valorous child, because your jaw or hanu is broken, you will be known from today as “Hanuman”. Henceforth, everything you do will be remembered, and paeans will be sung about you. Your fame will surpass mine. And because your body has been struck by my thunderbolt, I bless you that every limb of your body becomes as powerful as a thunderbolt. Thus you will also be known as ‘Vajranga, the one with resilient limbs’.”

With every benediction, Hanuman’s smile grew wider and his confidence in himself grew bigger. But the happiest person right now was his father, Vayu. Seeing his son receive such profuse blessings, Vayu beamed – a smile that only a satisfied father can radiate.

As a conclusion to the blessing session, Lord Brahma walked upto Hanuman with a brilliantly shining diamond necklace in his hands. He carefully placed it around his neck and peered into his eyes. “Listen carefully to what I am going to tell you now, O child, for what I am about to share with you will determine the shape of your future. This diamond necklace that I have given you will remain invisible to the world. None, including your parents, will be able to see it around your neck. The day you come across someone who does spot this necklace, understand that He is your eternal master.36

“The moment you see your master, you will have an ethereal experience. Every limb of your body will tremble in sublime bliss. Every single bone in your body will seem to melt in His presence. That very moment, surrender your life to Him and serve Him loyally life after life.”

The next moment, the cave was empty. Everyone vanished leaving behind a wonderful fragrance and a wonderstruck Hanuman with folded hands.

new beginnings

The brothers smiled at each other on seeing a radiant wondermonkey stand before them. They knew their search was headed the right direction.

“I am Hanuman, the servant of Sugriva!” the wonder-monkey beamed.

No sooner did Rama hear these words, than He took a step back. The ever-so-watchful Hanuman noted the change in Rama’s demeanor. He felt a tinge of worry. What have I done?

What was done, was done! He now had to undo whatever his mistake was. But then, not all mistakes can be undone instantly. Only time and sensitivity in the present and in the future will help undo the many mistakes of the nañve past.

Hanuman realized that what he had said was true up until this point in his life. But hereafter, that relationship was going to fade into the background and in its place would emerge this new one and take center stage. His previous relationship was imposed on him; this was awarded to him. That was temporary; this would be permanent. That was an obligation; this was a privilege.

That relationship was a by-product of another bond he fondly cherished – the relationship with his old master Surya, the sun god. As this relationship with his new master was taking birth, Hanuman recalled some fond memories with his old master.37

The first time Hanuman ventured toward the sun, he was an immature, frivolous child. This time, however, he was a sagacious, levelheaded youth. The first time, ignorance had provoked him, but this time the quest for knowledge had inspired him to embark on this arduous journey.

When Hanuman expressed his desire to be tutored by the best teacher in the universe, his father, the monkey-king Kesari, recommended the sun god Surya’s classes. Unfortunately, by the time Hanuman reached the sun, the seats were all full; admission was over. There were already six million sages seated on Surya’s orbiting chariot, all eager to receive high-level education. Although delighted to serve yet another eager student, Surya expressed his inability to accommodate Hanuman. His school was brimming with students; there wasn’t room for a single extra student.

Hanuman was very keen on being tutored only by the sun god. So he suggested an out-of-the-box solution that took Surya by surprise. Hanuman pointed out that the hearing process was the most important aspect of education, and as long as he was able to hear in on the lectures, the lack of a place to sit in class didn’t really matter. All he needed was the permission to listen to the teacher. Hanuman’s eagerness to acquire knowledge at any cost impressed Surya. He accepted him as a student, but on two preconditions: Hanuman had to always face him during the classes, and he should never expect any repetition of the lessons. This meant that the monkey-prince had to grasp it all the first time. Hanuman agreed right away, with absolutely no realization of the enormity of the stipulations.

The classes began soon, and all students sat listening to their teacher with rapt attention. Hanuman, on the other hand, floated around the massive orbiting school,38 making sure he always faced the teacher and absorbed every expression and word. All was well, until suddenly the chariot moved in the direction opposite to Hanuman’s line of flight. When that happened, a hint of a smile flashed across Surya’s face. He glanced at Hanuman even as he continued to teach. Ohh…now I get it! Hanuman knew instantly why those preconditions weren’t as simple as they seemed. This was a classroom in motion! Now, not only did he have to keep pace with the moving school by flying along with it, but he also had to fly backward to ensure that he always faced his teacher. Hanuman spent half his energy trying to keep his balance and focus on his reverse flying motion and half trying to catch every wave of wisdom emanating from his teacher.39

In just sixty orbits of Surya, Hanuman mastered the Rig, Sama, Atharva and Yajur Vedas and their unique applications. In addition, he also perfected the nine vyakaranas in just nine days – something that would take years and years for regular students to comprehend. Never in all his days of learning did Hanuman request his teacher to repeat a lesson or even a sentence. His attention was so intense that he absorbed everything instantly and retained it forever.

Soon, Hanuman was all set to leave the orbiting school and return home. Hanuman prostrated before his exalted teacher and thanked him for having taught him so patiently and so expertly. As a sign of gratitude, Hanuman wished to offer Surya an appropriate guru dakshina. In all humility, he asked the sun god what he would want as his guru dakshina and was willing to go to any lengths to get it for him. “O master, even if you desire Indra’s crown, I will get it for you instantly.” But Surya asked him for something more challenging – something Hanuman hardly expected, something that would change his life completely from that of a prince to… 40

“…the servant of Sugriva… I am Hanuman, the servant of Sugriva.” Every time Hanuman heard himself say those words, he remembered that this was his guru dakshina to the sun god – working under someone inferior to him in every way was not an easy task.

Spiritually, intellectually, physically, mentally and in every other way, Hanuman was far superior to Sugriva. He knew that and so did Sugriva. But Hanuman suppressed his ego deep enough to allow his gratitude toward his teacher to prevail. Yes, he was the servant of Sugriva! Not just servant, a messenger, minister, advisor, protector, companion and friend all rolled in one!

As he stood in front of his eternal master, Hanuman realized that this was a very delicate phase in his life. He had to balance the two most important commitments and relationships in his life. He knew that to be loyal to one, he could not be disloyal to the other. To be sensitive to one, he couldn’t be insensitive to the other. Hanuman closed his eyes and offered a small prayer, summoning all his strength to stay balanced in this clash of commitments.

new journey

“If this is the quality of the servant, what must the quality of the master be?” Rama whispered into Lakshmana’s ears, looking at Hanuman with great admiration. “Just by meeting Hanuman, I am convinced Sugriva is the right person to help us find Sita.

“Lakshmana, I am yet to meet someone as vaakya kushala41 as Hanuman. Surely, he must have spent a copious amount of time doing in-depth studies under exalted teachers. The quality of his speech reflects his education. From the moment we met him, I haven’t found a single flaw in either his speech or body language. He is eloquent, succinct and unambiguous. His speed and volume during speech are consistent from the start to finish. While talking, his body is so still that every bit of the listener’s focus rests on his speech alone. He knows the art of utilizing his stomach, palate and nasal passage to create the perfect pitch and pronunciation,” Rama’s eyes glistened as he poured effusive praise for Hanuman into Lakshmana’s ears.

“Surely he must have mastered the four Vedas. The natural humility or vinayata he is manifesting in his words and gestures is the result of mastering the Atharva Veda. From his vocabulary and choice of words, it is obvious that he has great retention power, which is the result of his mastery over Yajur Veda. The Yajur Veda texts have many deceivingly similar verses, which could cause confusion and are likely to be misquoted and used interchangeably. Anyone with mastery over accurately remembering such complex texts is blessed with tremendous memory. Although it is one thing to remember something, it is completely another to reproduce it all verbatim. Knowledge of Rig Veda has perhaps given him the power to reproduce what he has learned. Definitely, Hanuman has perfected Rig Veda too.

“Look at the suppleness and charm in his voice. Every word emanating from his mouth resembles a song. This is definitely the result of proficiency in Sama Veda, which is characterized by music. Expertise in Sama Veda is indicative of in-depth knowledge of all kinds of melodies. He makes absolutely no mistakes in the choice of words, no mistakes between sentences and no errors in sentence formation. I would even go to the extent of saying that if a cruel enemy with a raised sword were to hear Hanuman speak, he would drop his weapons and fall at his feet. He can easily win over anyone’s heart just by speaking. He can easily accomplish any mission assigned to him just by the dint of his wise speech. Lucky is that king who has such an expert ambassador. Hanuman will always be the harbinger of success in any venture of which he is a part.42

“O Lakshmana, judging by all the auspicious omens that we came across just before we met him, surely this pact with the wondermonkeys will turn out to be profitable to us. Converse with him and agree to strike the chord of friendship with their leader Sugriva.”43

When the brothers stepped aside for their private conversation, Hanuman took this opportunity to follow Sugriva’s instruction to scrutinize them carefully using his expertise in behavioral analysis. But as he looked at Rama’s charismatic face, all his analytical abilities vanished. Instead, a surcharge of deep love filled his heart and two words that best encapsulated the personality of Rama came to his blank mind: honey and sword.

Hanuman felt that Rama was a combination of the sweetness of honey and the sharpness of a sword. Rama’s face represented bhogya tattva or the honey-like sweetness that gives joy to everyone, and His shoulders represented raksha tattva or the swordlike sharpness that can protect everyone. Just as the sword isn’t sweet, honey isn’t sharp. Nothing and no one in this world can together exhibit these divergent qualities, none except one…

“O Hanuman!” Lakshmana’s clear voice shook Hanuman out of his musings. “We have been on the lookout for you and your master for quite some time now. It’s fortunate that you appeared before us yourself. I consider it my greatest fortune to introduce Rama, the prince of Ayodhya and the elder son of Dasaratha. The world considers me His younger brother, but I consider myself His menial servant in every situation of life. My name is Lakshmana.”

Painstakingly, Lakshmana went on to recount every incident that led to the brothers’ becoming forest-dwellers and about Sita’s abduction by Ravana during the last year of the exile period. Lakshmana concluded his narration with the story of their chance encounter with Kabandha. “In fact, it was he who advised us to ally with the wonder-monkey Sugriva, whose knowledge of the topography of the entire world is very precise.”

On hearing Lakshmana express the brothers’ eagerness to connect with Sugriva, Hanuman’s face lit up with great joy. It was hope more than joy, the hope of his master’s liberation from bondage.

“Now our only recourse is Sugriva. The mighty son of King Dasaratha, Ramachandra, is under the shelter of Sugriva. Please help him get back his lost wife…” Lakshmana’s eyes were wells of tears by now, and he could speak no further. Rama held His brother’s shoulders to steady him. Or was it to steady Himself?

Realizing the gravity of the situation and the pressure on the two brothers, Hanuman decided to light the gloomy environment with some hope.

“Intellectuals such as you who have conquered their senses and vices are actually God’s own people. Here, Sugriva is the one fortunate enough to have your refuge. In fact, he should have been the one seeking your shelter rather than you having to go to him. I am absolutely sure Sugriva will go all out to assist you in your quest.” Hanuman went down on one knee, with hands folded in front of the brothers. He made a request, “Please allow me the fortune of carrying the two of you on my back to meet Sugriva who is now atop the invincible Rishimukh mountains.”

With one glance at Rama, Lakshmana knew His exact desire and decision. Soon the brothers were perched on Hanuman’s broad shoulders, speeding up to the lofty mountain. Hanuman was as nimble and agile as any other monkey. Although he was scaling up the mountain with unbelievable speed, the precision and timing of his jumps were beyond perfect every step of the way. He took extra precaution to ensure that the brothers weren’t inconvenienced too much and were comfortably seated. As they climbed higher, the view below became just as majestic as it was scary.

To keep the minds of the brothers occupied, Hanuman decided to tell them about the intriguing circumstances surrounding the birth of Sugriva, something he thought might prove useful to them before they actually met him in person. The story was indeed so exciting, that at times, Hanuman had to stop climbing and just focus on the narration to do complete justice to the unusual twists and turns in the storyline.

brothers from different fathers

Today was the only break he had had in eons – the only nonworking day in the millions of years that he had been at this job. He was so high with excitement that he found himself going crazy with the newfound joy this very temporary freedom promised to bring.44

So what if there was a universal calamity? So what if every big and not-so-big demigod was in a state of panic? Each day until this lucky day, he had slogged hard to keep the massive chariot spinning nonstop around the center of the galaxy while all others had just had fun. But a while ago, the chariot had come to an abrupt halt. He had not been able to steer it, no matter how hard he tried. So he asked the sages on the chariot about the cause of this sudden holdup. It seemed luckily, that an angry woman on earth named Shilavatihas had cursed the sun god Surya and forced him to a standstill for some unacceptable injustice done to her husband, Ugrasravas.

So finally, today was his day of fun. And today, he wanted to immerse himself in every drop of pleasure he could possibly get out of this unexpected break. Now that Surya was still, his charioteer Aruna had nothing to do. He had been doing the thankless job of steering Surya’s chariot around-the-clock for ages. The sages had said that it would take at least a whole day to undo the curse. For what it was worth, Aruna wanted to make the most of this break and extract every bit of pleasure he could. So he began considering different avenues to have pleasure.

After eliminating countless options for gratification at various pleasure hotspots in the universe, Aruna froze at the thought of something he believed was the pinnacle of all pleasurable experiences: Meneka’s secret dance. It was Indra’s by-invite-only event, which was billed as the performance of the millennium. With only a single day to fulfil all his unfulfilled desires, Aruna gathered his wits and decided to gate-crash the party by adopting a smart and undetectable disguise.

Aruni, an extremely beautiful damsel, rushed toward the gates of the party. No sooner had she entered the dance room, than all eyes turned toward her. The dance was already in progress! In fact, the late entry caused a commotion and interrupted the performance. The moment Indra saw her, he stood up, and everyone in the assembly did the same. To everyone’s surprise, Indra staggered toward the new entrant. The most gorgeous Meneka felt slighted by this sudden shift of attention from her and the forcible break in her performance by a stranger’s entry.

“You are by far the most beautiful damsel I have ever seen!” Indra walked around Aruni staring at her with intensely lustful eyes. Those stares made her very uncomfortable; she tried to escape the prying eyes, but Indra caught her hands forcibly and held her in place. By now, Aruni was squirming and feeling mortified by his advances. She so badly wanted to tell him that she was a he, but the fear of the consequences stopped her from uttering anything.

Indra dispersed everyone with a wave of his hand. Once alone, he began to inch toward her. Unable to take it anymore, the damsel screamed, “I am not a woman, I am a man! I am not Aruni, I am Aruna, the charioteer of the sun god.”

Indra was least bothered. He said, “I don’t care who you are. Now that you are here with me, you will have to bear my child.”

In the next instant, a child was born through Aruni. Handing the child over to Indra, a very distressed Aruna reverted to his own form on the way and hopped right into Surya’s chariot, just on time. Looking at Aruna fume and sweat, Surya was nonplussed. “Where had you disappeared to for so long, Aruna?” he asked. Aruna did not at all intend to reveal his humiliating tale, but Surya cajoled him into a confession. When Surya heard the fascinatingly bewildering story, he wanted to see Aruna as Aruni.

Aruna pleaded with his master not to embarrass him yet again. But Surya was adamant, and soon Aruni was standing in front of the sun god in all her mesmerising glory. Surya was bewitched by Aruni’s beauty and stung by a passion so deep, that he fathered a child with her. After handing over the baby to Surya, Aruna was back to his own self.

The heavens resonated with the cries of these two newborns, making it very difficult for anyone in the heavens to focus on anything at all. A disconcerted Lord Brahma then advised Indra to find a suitable father to take good care of these children.45

Accordingly, Indra found out about a vanara king, Riksharaja, who was engrossed in long and ardent tapasya for children. The demigod king appeared to this wonder-monkey and offered him these two children. Riksharaja gratefully accepted the two babies and took them to his abode in Kishkinda where they grew up to become two of the most powerful vanaras in the universe.

The son born of Indra became Vali, and one born of Surya became Sugriva. They had the powers of the gods who fathered them and the appearance of the vanara king who raised them.

unlikely ally

The beggar-sanyasi they had seen traverse down the mountain a few hours earlier was now standing at the entrance of the cave inside which they had stationed themselves, safely ensconced from any calamity. When he left, he was confident of himself. Now upon his return, he seemed superconfident, as if replenished by some power beyond himself.

One look at him and they could easily tell that something had happened down there. None of them had the courage to ask what had happened. They felt that they did not have the right to do so. Beyond that, however, they had a feeling that even if they asked and he were to tell, they might never understand it.

There was a flash of light, and Hanuman was standing in front of Sugriva with folded hands and a smile on his face. That smile spoke a million words.

Would Sugriva understand that smile?

How did brothers who grew up together become such bloodthirsty enemies?

## Chapter 2

Anjana’s Blessing, Vali’s Nemesis

joined in devotion

A sudden quiver ran down her spine! Her cheeks glistened with tears. A pleasant smile appeared on her lips.

She was sitting in a yogic posture on the topmost cliff of a rocky mountain when this happened. She opened her eyes slowly, folded her palms and bowed in veneration to the divine phenomenon that she had clairvoyantly witnessed.

She had been waiting for this moment since the day the sages had blessed her husband and her for their extraordinary contribution to their lives. Oh what a day that was! The distance between life and death was just an inch away – the day she met her life companion in the most dramatic, unimaginable way. A day she still dreaded and cherished simultaneously.46

\*\*\*

Her eyes were shut in deep meditation, but she felt like she was being watched – intensely.

Her heart pounding hard, she opened her eyes. Horror of horrors! In front of her was a sight so grotesque, that she backed off with a start and hit the wall of the cave inside which she was seated. Standing a few meters away from her, blocking the entrance of the cave was this intensely ugly, extremely fearful-looking, gigantic rakshasa. There was no escape from the demon’s cruel intent to ravish her then and there.

She stood up, steadying herself for what was to come. Then taking the lusty rakshasa by surprise, she lunged toward him. Using all her strength, she rammed into the demon. The force of her thrust was far beyond what her frail body seemed capable of. This incongruity worked to her advantage, and the unprepared rakshasa lost his balance and staggered backward. She had found the escape she was seeking!

The few moments he took to recover from the shove were enough for the nimble girl to make her way toward the closest village. The rakshasa gathered himself and chased after her. If he had the size and extraordinarily long strides – each of which equaled a hundred of hers – she had skill, speed and flexibility. She ran, swung from branches of trees and hanging roots and jumped over boulders and bushes.

She could see a trail of smoke not far away. A settlement! She just had to push herself a few more steps to reach her destination. When she looked back to make sure that the danger was still some distance away, she realized that her destruction was closer than she had imagined. Not wanting to give up after reaching this close, she used every ounce of her energy to sprint ahead. Once she entered the village, she zigzagged through the narrow lanes between the different hermitages and houses to trick the rakshasa into losing sight of her. She could finally take a breather. In those fleeting moments of rest, she decided to crash into a hermitage, hoping and praying it belonged to the person to whom she assumed it did. She had heard of a wise sage who lived somewhere in this village. His fame rested on the fact that he had answers to the most complex of doubts that existed. Right now, what could be more complicated than this demon?47

She barged in, crashing down at the feet of the saint seated inside with his eyes closed in deep absorption. She was lucky! Indeed, she was seeking him. He opened his eyes with great compassion, undisturbed by her sudden intrusion and glanced at her lovingly. Through the tiny window of his hut, he could see the looming rakshasa searching for his prey. As lust and frustration burned in his eyes, he began plundering the village, flattening and uprooting homes looking for her.

The sage looked at the distraught girl, “This is the powerful asura, Sambasadhana. No one can do anything to stop him from achieving what he seeks to achieve. He is indestructible. It appears as if Lord Shiva himself has empowered him to destroy everything in this universe. As far as I know, barring the monkey-hero Kesari, no one has the power to destroy him. I have no idea where he could be at this time and how to even convince him to come to our aid.”

The moment she heard this, hope filled her heart and she immediately sat down and began a deep prayer to Lord Shiva. Suddenly, a tremor shook everything and tore through the hermitage, ripping apart the walls and the roof. Sambasadhana’s glaring eyes pierced into the beautiful form he was lusting after. His search had ended. She looked captivating with closed eyes and a serene look on her face. He could no longer resist his temptations.

As he took the next step toward her in an attempt to grab her, he was hit by something so hard that his ears began to ring very loudly, and he felt torturously deafened by a relentless hum. Unable to bear the pain any longer, he collapsed on his knees. What or who could have the ability and the audacity to hit him with such force! He turned around to see a strong wonder-monkey standing with his hands on his hips and a grin on his face.

“Kesari!” the sage exclaimed. The girl opened her eyes with a smile. Yet again Lord Shiva had answered her prayers.

Kesari spoke in a grave and deeply resonating voice. “Anyone can show might in front of weak people. If you have any strength, come and fight with me, you rascal.”48

Sambasadhana, unable to bear the insult, barged into the standing monkey. Although he was massive, Kesari’s power was formidable and matched his. The two of them pounded each other’s bodies ferociously. When fists were not enough, they uprooted trees to attack each other. The entire village including all the sages assembled to see this close-quarter fight. It was very difficult to predict the outcome at this point. Everyone prayed for Kesari to succeed in banishing Sambasadhana from their lives forever.

Just then, something unimaginable happened. Sambasadhana transformed into a giant elephant. He was now ten times his original size and appeared to be ten times stronger. He began demolishing everything within his vicinity, razing the entire village to the ground. Not one house was left standing. To the horror of the villagers and sages, even Kesari seemed like he was clueless.

But the next moment, Kesari did something that surprised all of the onlookers. He shrank himself down to a tiny size, jumped onto the elephant and began pounding him on the forehead with his tightly clenched fist. Although he was tiny, Kesari’s blows hurt Sambasadhana the elephant. Kesari was relentless with his punches, knowing well that the forehead was an elephant’s weakest spot. Sambasadhana began to writhe in acute pain from every blow to his forehead. He could neither reach Kesari with his trunk nor do anything to prevent the blows. Soon Sambasadhana was bleeding profusely, his blood dripping all over the village as he ran amok shaking his head violently trying to throw Kesari off.49
