All he ever wanted was a good fight and a better victory. His thirst for a challenge had taken him to the ocean. He had stirred the waters with all his might, but no challenger was forthcoming. His chaotic antics had begun to hurt the ocean god.

His ravaging rumblings in the waters finally forced the ocean god to appear in front of him. “O powerful, Dundubhi, what you want I can’t provide. You can already see that I am helpless in front of your prowess. If you really want a suitable competitor who would even dare to challenge your might, then I suggest that you leave me and proceed toward the mighty Himalayan mountain range. Himavan is the only person who can probably match your might.”

Dundubhi walked out of the ocean with a disappointed groan. He had come with so much expectation. An exhilarating fight was all he wanted. No matter how much he searched, no one even considered fighting with him. His body and limbs itched for a good battle. So he rushed toward the formidable Himalayan mountain ranges. He knew how to get instant appointments with the most important people. His method was very simple really – destruction!107

He began rummaging through mountain peaks, causing tremors throughout the area. The ruckus he created soon forced Himavan, the king of the Himalayan mountain ranges, to appear before him. Snorting at the handsome figure of the king of mountains clad in white flowing robes, Dundubhi spoke in a hoarse and heavy voice, “O king of the mighty mountains, if you have any strength at all in reality, I challenge you to fight with me. If you withstand me for more than a minute, I will accept that you are strong. Else, you should submit to my will.”

Himavan humbly folded his hands and beseeched the angry buffalo demon with a heavy voice that echoed as he spoke. “I am sorry. I am neither a warmonger nor a match for your prowess. I am just a humble shelter for the sages. I cannot get into a fight to prove my superiority and disturb the tapasya of these saints. My job is to assist them in their journey toward perfecting their lives.”108

The danava Dundubhi was so frustrated with yet another attempt at thwarting his urge to fight. Himavan noticed irritation and gave him an appropriate suggestion. “If you really are looking for an intense fight, I suggest you to challenge Lord Shiva. He is the most intense fighter when provoked sufficiently. So far, no one has been able to beat him in a fight.”

Very soon, the Kailash mountain was shuddering. Typical of his style, Dundubhi uprooted the mountain from its base. He had tired of exhibiting his power to oceans, mountains and trees. He wanted to fight a real person. Lord Shiva definitely seemed a promising candidate. Somehow, Shiva was in peaceful mood and didn’t want to lose that tranquility by indulging in a stupid fight with a puny demon. He simply accepted his inability to fight with Dundubhi and directed him to the king of heavens, Indra.

Within minutes, the heavens were in turmoil because of a crazy buffalo chasing the celestials around just for fun. Finally, Indra too appeared to admit his inability to match his strength. But he said something that caught Dundubhi’s attention.

“Don’t waste your time running around. Your death will be at the hands of my son Vali. He is the most powerful warrior the world has ever seen. Go and die at his feet.” On another day, Indra’swords would have enticed hate in Dundhubhi, but this time he felt happy. Finally, someone to match my valor, give me a real fight and satisfy my itchy limbs.109

At dusk, something shattered the stillness of the royal palace of Kishkinda. Mayhem followed. Soon the servants were running helter-skelter, unable to deal with the sudden catastrophe. The portico of the palace had collapsed. With just one smash, Dundubhi had dismantled a substantial portion of Vali’s palace. This buffalo had struck terror on Kishkinda. Dundubhi’s arrival was like that of the dark, monstrous, rain-filled cloud before the monsoon. By attacking the royal palace of the king directly, he had managed to strike right at the heart of Kishkinda’s security system.

On hearing the commotion, a drunk and tottering Vali came to the terrace of his palace supported by his queen Tara and numerous women from his harem. His sunken eyes were so intoxicated that he was barely able to pry them open. But what he saw bewildered him so much that the effect of the alcohol vanished instantly. Vali stared in horror at the favorite section of his beautiful palace, which was now a ruin. He looked around to see the amount of destruction in his kingdom. Thousands of uprooted trees, uneven craters dug out by the horns and hooves of the buffalo, blood splattered around the palace building and intense terror in the eyes of thousands of Kishkinda’s citizens.

Vali’s eyes glowered with anger as he directed them toward the culprit – the smiling buffalo demon, Dundubhi. Although affected by the demon’s deeds, Vali did not want to show that he had lost his cool. Continuing to place his hands over the shoulders of his women, Vali spoke to the offending demon, “Dundubhi, if you still want to save your life after destroying Vali’s palace, run away from here right now. If your stupid buffalo brain is able to comprehend the seriousness of my warning, then leave my kingdom and never come back again. King Vali will consider this folly of yours to be a child’s naughty prank and neglect it.”

Vali’s haughty words only ended up fueling Dundubhi’s desire to fight. He spat out in contempt, “Only cowards speak tall words while conveniently hiding behind ladies. Come down from your dream world and face the deadly reality that stands before you.”

Hurling more sarcastic slurs at Vali, Dundubhi said, “Oh, now I understand why you are still standing up there with your girlfriends. Surely, you want to experience one more night of sensual enjoyment before you finally die at my hands tomorrow morning, you swollenfaced monkey. Go ahead, continue to revel and gratify your senses, King Vali.

“Don’t I know that the one who kills a drunken man, an unvigilant man, a mad man, a defeated man, an unarmed man and a weak man gets the same sinful reaction as when committing feticide? The state of a person indulging in sensual pleasures is very vulnerable, much like a fetus in the womb. So don’t worry, enjoy your night of bliss in every possible way.

“When you come to fight me tomorrow morning, make sure that you line up all your monkey friends, relatives and well-wishers to bid them your last good-bye. Embrace each of them lovingly as you do so. Make sure you give away sufficient wealth to the needy, as that good deed will help you in the upcoming hellish journey I will be sending you on. If you like, before coming to meet your death, you can even go around your city taking in its sights and smells for one last time.

“And of course, while doing all this, make sure that at the back of your mind you mull over whom you will appoint as the future king of Kishkinda knowing well that the present king has already become past history.” The buffalo demon grinned after uttering his words of instigation. Seeing the rage building up on the monkey king’s face, Dundubhi knew his desire for a tough fight would soon be satisfied. He had indeed hit the right nerve of Vali.110

“HA! HA! HA! HA! HA!” Vali roared, as he pushed aside the women. He went closer to the railing of the terrace and stood gripping it tightly, glaring menacingly at Dundubhi. “You dullheaded buffalo, don’t assume that I am afraid of fighting due to my drunkenness. Great heroes take a pre-battle drink that exhilarates their nerves so they perform with greater intensity. Indulgence in pleasures only ends up inciting my deadly fighter instincts. Say your final prayers if you so wish. I am coming!”

Vali dashed into his inner chamber, grabbed his precious necklace and ran back to the terrace while wearing it on the go. Leaping up the parapet, Vali jumped down and landed on his legs in front of the grinning demon. Tara and the other women in the terrace looked on with concern as Vali strode boldly toward the humongous menace lurking in Kishkinda.

Walking straight up to the buffalo, Vali bent his body forward so that his eyes were barely less than an inch away from the eyes of Dundubhi. Staring eye to eye with intense hatred, the two hulks began to wrestle using the strength of their heads. They remained head-locked for a long period.

Then suddenly, using his horns, Dundubhi tossed Vali away to his left very vigorously. Recovering even before falling, Vali rushed back to attack his opponent. As Vali ran toward him, Dundubhi saw a brilliant shine reflecting off Vali’s pendant. Suddenly, he felt a bit weak. Did his sudden weakness have anything to do with the necklace around the monkey’s neck? As he shook off the unlikely notion from his mind, a powerful blow from Vali’s fist landed on his skull. Dundubhi’s forelegs grazed the ground. The blow shook his body and sent his head spinning.

But he recovered quickly and kicked Vali with his forelegs, flinging him backward into a thick wall, which shattered on impact. Shaking off all the debris from his body, Vali ran toward his formidable opponent. This time, he was determined to finish him off.

Dundubhi experienced the same draining sensation again. Something in that jewel was distracting him. This time, however, he was too preoccupied with trying to make sense of his loss of strength. Everything had happened too suddenly: Vali had reached him, seized him by his horns, lifted him off the ground and begun spinning him. Only when he felt himself being whirled around did Dundubhi realize that he was in big trouble. He began to kick his legs in a desperate attempt to release himself. But by now Vali was spinning him by his horns at breakneck speed. The centrifugal force was so intense that when Vali finally released him, he flew miles away and crashed at the boundary of Kishkinda. Dundubhi was now a gory mess, with blood oozing out of his nostrils and his body.

Not wanting to give up after being insulted like this, Dundubhi gathered his strength and darted back toward Vali. Anger surged within the buffalo demon when he saw Vali laughing loudly at his expense and displaying his strength to the women looking from the terrace. When Vali saw the demon head fast toward him, he began to run toward the demon as well. As this battle reached a peak, the two hulks let out deafening war cries. Just when they were a few feet away from each other, they leaped off the ground and their shoulders collided. When the giant bodies crashed into each other, fire sparks flew because of the friction caused. Every time they pounded each other, they damaged each other’s limbs.

Soon, everyone in Kishkinda was gaping with wonder, watching the king take on the mighty demon. The crashing sounds and boisterous bellows had drawn all the citizens to gather around and enjoy this nail-biting battle of strength. Although the buffalo demon was colossal, the citizens could clearly see that their king had an edge. In fact, it was evident now that the demon was only fighting with half his strength and that Vali was becoming stronger by the second. Vali’s seemingly nondescript pendant had somehow viciously sucked away half of Dundubhi’s energy and transferred it to the monkey king.111

Then Vali suddenly realized that something had gone wrong. Instead of becoming weaker by the minute, Dundubhi was growing stronger. If things were to go according to Vali’s usual experience with the powerful jewel, by now Dundubhi should have been whining on the floor, exhausted and conceding defeat. Instead, defeat did not seem to be on the buffalo demon’s mind.

That’s when Vali realized that he had calculated everything to perfection, but neglected one key thing: the time of day of the fight. Time played a crucial role, especially when it came to fighting demoniac or dark forces known to draw their strength from darkness. The jewel did make Dundubhi weaker, but the fast approaching night was offering him strength. Vali knew that if he didn’t act fast enough, the battle would get more complex and tricky.112

Determined to finish off the fight, Vali held Dundubhi by his horns and lifted him off the ground. As before, he twirled him again with great force and speed, despite the demon’s size. The spin velocity was so high that Dundubhi could neither breathe nor see anything. Everything was a blur. Soon his eyes began to close in sheer exhaustion. Everything was turning dark before his eyes. Very soon, Vali’s endless and superfast spins had sucked the life out of Dundubhi. The first round in the game of physical self-establishment had ended with a termination.

As Vali was rotating the demon’s body, blood began gushing out from all over the demon’s body. There was so much blood that all the buildings and even mountains in the vicinity seemed sprayed in red. When Vali realized that the buffalo demon was just mere pulp, he threw the carcass high into the sky.

When gravity eventually pulled down the demon’s body, it fell on the Rishimukh mountain. Not only was this hill a favorite of many rishis, as the name suggests, but it was also the abode of the holiest of them all, Matanga rishi. When Dundubhi’s cadaver fell, the impact of the contact with the earth smashed whatever was left of his body, splattering in all directions. The worst part was that Dundubhi’s pulpy body fell right at the entrance of Matanga rishi’s ashram, wetting the inmates of the ashram with blood and flesh. Matanga rishi, who was seated at the porch of the hermitage in meditation, was drenched with a huge blood glob.

A gore-soaked Matanga rishi’s blood boiled with rage, his eyes bloodshot. He looked around at the ruin that the fallen carcass had caused to his serene and pure hermitage. Every tree in the vicinity was dripping gore. The peaceful deer, peacocks, squirrels and cows that would wander around the hermitage were all so frightened that they ran a good half a mile away from the premises and hid behind trees. They had no time to comprehend what had fallen from the skies, but now from behind the safety of the trees, they watched patiently as the aftermath played out.

“Which stupid fool has attempted to play with fire?” Matanga rishi hollered. Seeing their master covered in blood, the scampering disciples froze. When they saw his rage, they knew that the troublemaker was now in deep trouble. When the rishi got no answers and saw no claimants owning up for the mess, he shut his eyes and decided to peek into his yogic vision.113

He could see clearly the power clash had occurred in Kishkinda between the dead demon and the monkey chief. This was the consequence of that battle. Vali was infamous for his arrogance, but this single act crossed the word ‘arrogance’ by a million miles. This was simply the most conceited act the sage had ever seen. He realized that Vali had become egotistical beyond comprehension because of the strength of his mystical jewel. “It is time to prick Vali’s bloated ego with the pin of a limitation. His ego makes him believe that he can do anything, anywhere, anytime and with anyone and get away with it.” Despite his fury, Matanga rishi was still thinking of the welfare of the evildoer.114

“The head of this evildoer will split into a million pieces if he dares to even venture within one yojana of this sacred Rishimukh mountain.” The impact of Matanga rishi’s curse was so strong that nature reaffirmed it. As soon as he uttered the curse, a huge bolt of lightning flashed across the sky and a thunderous sound echoed across the skies. So powerful was the phenomenon that Vali’s assistants who were strolling in that very mountain began to tremble. They immediately sensed that something terrible had taken place, something that spelled trouble for their land and master.

They knew that sage Matanga’s ashram was close by and a small walk to it would allay their doubts. With that hope, they reached the ashram only to find a blood-covered hermitage and the pulpy carcass of Dundubhi blocking the entrance. Their legs began to shake. They added everything up – the thunder and lightning, the battle between Vali and Dundubhi and this bloody mess at the hermitage – and soon realized what this meant for their master.. Vali had badly messed around with the wrong people.115

“Ignorant monkey disciples of an arrogant monkey leader! Your days of joy are numbered now. Your leader’s barbarous act signals the beginning of his end. From this moment forth, if any of you even so much as touches the trees, leaves, fruits or roots of this Rishimukh mountain, you will transform into lifeless stones that very instant.” That ominous threat from the powerful sage rattled the monkey assistants. They clung to each other in fear and began to search for a tree or shrub to hide.

Matanga had more to say. “Inform your arrogant leader that my curse will take effect exactly at sunrise tomorrow morning. After that if he even tries to venture within one yojana of this mountain, he will die instantly in the most painful of ways.”

The moment the monkey assistants conveyed the curse, Vali rushed to the holy sage and fell at his feet. “I beg you, please forgive me for my mistake.” The disgusted sage just brushed past him, right back into his hermitage.

Nothing within Vali’s power could influence the angry sage.116

reason to kill

Mere arrogance alone doesn’t warrant death.

Rama still wasn’t clear about why Sugriva hated Vali so much that he wanted him dead. His fear was obvious from the way he overglorified Vali’s herculean deeds. But unless such fear was justifiable, no action could be taken.

“I have heard a lot about Vali’s greatness and prowess, but now if you could get to the real point, we could draw some conclusions. Unless I know the complete truth, I won’t be able to decide on my next course of action. I want to know the exact magnitude of your mistake, which led him to banish you from your own kingdom. If indeed your offense is small and the magnitude of his hate is disproportionate, then I will take him to task. But if on hearing the whole story I realize that the greater mistake was yours, then I will endeavor to negotiate with him to either forgive you or punish you suitably but reinstate you to your position. Only immature people take stern action before weighing sufficiently and carefully the various causes that act as stimuli behind any event.

Yes, I am your friend and I want to help you in every possible way. But first, the cause has to be in alignment with the principles of dharma. Unless I ascertain the root cause of your enmity with Vali and confirm that your cause is indeed in alignment with principles of dharma, I won’t be able to help you with all my heart. For a kshatriya, anger surges forth like foam from a river during the rainy season, but kshatriya dharma expects that before exhibiting anger, a true kshatriya should ascertain if the cause is worth his anger. If need be, I am willing to destroy Vali and his entire army, but unless I know the exact nature of the crime, I would not be able to ascertain the magnitude of the punishment. So my dear friend, now I want you to focus on elaborating in great detail that part of your life story that has caused such a drift between loving brothers.”117

Rama’s words hurt Sugriva. What did Rama expect from him? Why didn’t Rama believe him? Tears of disappointment began to well up in his eyes. In a voice mixed with desperation and anger, Sugriva spoke up. “What is this, Rama? You want a good reason to punish him. Haven’t I given you enough reasons already? Isn’t driving a brother away from the kingdom a good enough reason? Isn’t usurping a brother’s wife good enough? Isn’t throwing all innocent citizens into prison for supporting me good enough? Isn’t sending so many hitmen to kill his own brother good enough? How many more good reasons do you need to punish an evildoer? Why don’t you please understand my predicament? My brother is trying to eliminate me. If he doesn’t die soon, I will perish. I cannot be happy as long as Vali breathes.”

Sugriva had completely broken down. Rama had to lift him up gently and explain to him that the only reason He said all that was because He needed to know the details of Vali’s errors as well as the episode that led to Sugriva’s banishment for greater clarity on the character and mind-set of Vali. Rama explained to Sugriva that a good kshatriya does not blindly take action but digs into details to ascertain facts before drawing conclusions.

Breathing deeply, Sugriva began his recollection of the most painful day of his life, a day he wished had never happened at all, a day he had to take the toughest, most erroneous decision of his life.

sugriva’s story

“That moonlit night by the dark unending burrow in the ground still haunts me. What happened at the burrow is why I stand in front of you today.

“Stubbornness had been Vali’s hallmark, women his weakness and a quest for fame his intoxicant. Now this mortifying burrow enticed him with the promise of fulfillment of all three. He entered into it with great overconfidence and arrogance. Vali made me swear that I would not follow him into the burrow and then leaped right in, with a desire to hog all the credit for killing the enemy who had disappeared into it.118

“The whole night passed in complete silence. At the break of dawn, I began to panic knowing well that the demon Vali had followed into the burrow was extremely dangerous. The earth and the sky were definitely Vali’s domains. I had never seen anyone defeat Vali in these zones. But the subterranean regions were foreign to Vali’s dominion. Not only was he alone, but he was also in unfamiliar territory.119

“Now that I look back, I realize that right from the day Vali had killed Dundubhi, his retribution day was round the corner. But I hadn’t expected it to happen this way or so fast. Mayavi had in fact timed his entry into the Kishkinda province to perfection, but at first glance, it seemed as if he had merely tottered into an erroneous zone in his drunken state. He made his entry exactly at dusk, knowing well that Vali would have long returned from his oceanic gayatri expeditions and slipped under the intoxicating spell of liquor and women. Somehow, both the brothers had chosen the same time to attack Vali. Had they communicated about it to each other? Impossible! As Dundubhi was dead before, he could not have communicated anything to anyone. But from the choice of time, it was obvious that both brothers thought alike. Both were convinced that to attack Vali, the best time would be the time of his greatest weakness and the time of their greatest strength – nightfall.

“But Mayavi’s attack on Kishkinda was not just to avenge his brother’s death, although it seemed that way at the onset. I later discovered that there were two other even more important reasons for this attack. The first had to do with the cold-blooded murder of their father Mayasura at his own palace in his own city of Hemapura. The second had to do with a woman whom both Vali and Mayavi had been wooing. It seemed that Mayavi was so in love with that girl that when he discovered that she had been two-timing him with Vali, instead of forgetting her, he decided it was better to eliminate Vali.

“When Mayavi roared that night challenging Vali to a duel, it was the painful bellow of a hurt lover. To make it worse for him, his girl was in the arms of Vali that night. An argument ensued, with the hulks hurling profane abuses at one another. Finally unable to resist himself, Vali ran out of his palace to pound the talkative demon. A voice within told me that this venture was going to ruin our lives. I ran after my angry brother, imploring him not to engage in this brawl as my intuition was warning me about dire consequences. My arrogant brother touched his magical pendant and told me not to worry. As he ran toward the howling Mayavi demon, I sprinted behind him to support him in the fight.

“When Mayavi saw us both darting toward him, he shed his daring and took to his heels. Now on hindsight, I wonder if he actually ran out of fear or if it was some strategy. In retrospect, it definitely was a master strategy.

“It was an intense chase after the demon, with only the moonlight guiding us. We were now running through a forest, but not once did he turn back to see how far behind or close we were. He seemed completely confident of our inability to catch him and that we weren’t going to give up the chase. Now that I think about it, Mayavi hadn’t come unprepared like his foolhardy brother Dundubhi had. He had researched Vali’s psyche and knew exactly what to say to trigger his anger. He was also wise enough not to engage in a fight with Vali.

“I kept having this feeling that this whole chase was a trap. I tried to, but I couldn’t decipher what the trap could be. But more than that, I couldn’t muster enough courage to warn my elder brother about it. Just as my mind was running with these wild thoughts and my legs were sprinting through the forest, both of us abruptly froze in our tracks. The demon had vanished!

“A moment ago he was running right ahead of us. Where and how could he disappear so fast? Suddenly the answer was right in front of us. The full moon night revealed this dangerous burrow in the ground.

“On observing carefully, we realized that the burrow had been camouflaged with grass. Had it been even darker, finding the cleverly concealed hole would have been impossible. It was obvious now that Mayavi had strategically lured us to this hole in the ground. What lay inside was unknown to us. But whatever it was, it was potentially dangerous and life threatening.

“I went berserk when my brother prepared himself to enter the burrow. Mayavi had indeed been shrewd enough to understand Vali’s psychology. He knew that Vali’s anger would compel him to accept any challenge posed to him. He also knew that Vali’s pride would force him to persist until victory. He knew that Vali’s selfishness would make him enter that burrow alone, not wanting to share any credit for the victory.

“Although I understood what Mayavi was trying, and because of my inability to explain all this to my brother, I fell at his feet desperately trying to dissuade him from entering the burrow all alone. I tried explaining that we could return the next morning with the Kishkinda army and rummage this seemingly unending underground tunnel. I tried to wrest him out of his suicidal determination. When I realized that the argument wasn’t working, I pleaded with him to at least take me along with him on this dangerous expedition. Ignoring my concerns, he made me swear not to leave the entrance of the cavity until he returned after annihilating Mayavi and his friends. This was the second and final round in the game of physical self-establishment. He had to win it at any cost!120

“Before jumping in, Vali turned back and gave me an instruction that seemed absurd to me at that time. But in hindsight, I realize it was a very intelligent move and just shows that his intuition was right about what was about to happen. But unfortunately, Mayavi either overheard the directive or he just turned out to be more intelligent than both of us put together.

“Knowing well that once he entered the abode of the demons, there would hardly be any way of communicating with me, Vali revealed a secret code. He said that he would make sure that the fight ended with blood splashing out of the burrow. If milky white fluid splashed out, I would know that the demon was slain; however, if red blood poured out, then I should assume that the mighty Vali had met his end. If that were to happen, then I would have to seal the cavity permanently with rocks and boulders so that the demons would have no way of coming out to harass the residents of Kishkinda ever again.

“With that last instruction, the determined Vali plunged into that hellhole. I squatted on my knees staring into that dark pit. The night was eerily silent –no sound or a flicker of movement. As the early rays of the sun penetrated the darkness of the burrow, all I could see was a winding dungeon. It seemed to be one of those unknown secret entries into the lower planetary systems known as the patalalokas. All day long I stared into that gaping hole, telling myself not to lose hope, reminding myself of my brother’s unfathomable prowess. By the end of the day, my hope began waning. I dared not move from that position, just in case my brother called out for help.

“I managed to pass on a message to the kingdom that we would soon be back. I managed to conceal our whereabouts, to prevent the citizens in the kingdom from panicking. Days passed as I continued staring helplessly into that hole. I had no idea how long this would continue. I had become a nomad, living in the forest so far away from the comforts of the kingdom. I never once returned to the kingdom to get even basic supplies, only because I wanted to be there if my brother called out for help. There was no information from inside, no secret message and no call for help for many, many months. I would sit on the edge of the hole all day long, with my feet dangling inside it, waiting for something to happen. All night long, I would lie down staring into the darkness of the cavity until sleep took over.

“Almost exactly a year after my brother pounced into the burrow, I heard a rumbling inside. When I heard it the first time, I couldn’t believe it. I thought I was hallucinating. Then I heard the rumble again.

AAAGGGRRHHH! AAAGGGRRHHH! AAAGGGRRHHH!

“Those cries were loud and clear. I could distinctly hear not one or two, but hundreds of voices. It sounded to me as if hundreds of voices were subduing the single distinct voice of my brother. Anyone else would have probably missed my brother’s cries in the cacophony of hundreds of other voices, but my alert and keen ears picked up Vali’s painful screams. Suddenly all tones changed to roars. Hundreds of demons were now roaring, and only my brother was screaming. Gradually my brother’s voice began to subside and all I heard was the roar of demons. Then, just as suddenly as it had begun, the demoniac roaring ceased. An eerie silence followed. “I almost jumped into the burrow to help my fallen brother when the most unbelievable thing happened. From within the deep cavity, a huge gush of hot blood spurted out with such force that I had to stagger back just in time to save my face from being splashed with it. I tripped and fell on my back just in time to see gushing blood take the shape of a stream and flow out of the burrow. When I went closer to the burrow, I was horrified to see that unbelievable amounts of blood had accumulated in the burrow in such a short time. What was once an empty, dark cavity had now literally become an overflowing blood tank!

“What was most disturbing was the color of the blood. For a moment, I thought my eyes had deceived me. I thought I saw whitish fluid turn red. But any instructions about the color of the blood disappeared from my mind when I saw the rapid gushing of blood right in front of me. The sight of the blood stream was horrible, and a disturbing thought ran through my head: my brother had been slain. The powerful King Vali was dead!

“As I fell on my knees next to that stream of blood, memories of how lovingly our father, the great Riksharaja, embracing both of us filled my mind. As I cried, lamenting my brother’s death, the teary face of my father flashed before me. He had always appreciated Vali’s determination to eliminate his enemy and never spare them. But today, that same determination had become the cause of his death. The quality that makes you can be the very quality that breaks you. “I wiped off my tears as I had bigger responsibilities now. I realized that my next duty was toward the kingdom and its citizens. If this demon had destroyed someone as powerful as Vali, he was definitely unstoppable. I decided to act fast before the demon crept out of the death hole. I looked around and decided to seal the burrow permanently as Vali had instructed so that no one could come out of it. I quickly gathered huge rocks and boulders. I placed them strategically and I covered the burrow in such a way that the exit point would be impossible to trace from within the burrow. In a matter of moments, I filled the burrow to the brim with stones. I first threw big boulders, then smaller ones that occupied the crevices and then finally finer gravel to block the hole completely. Either all that blood that filled the burrow flowed out or the stones and gravel just soaked it all up. Finally, I uprooted a huge hillock and placed it over the hole. Once done, I stepped back to gauge my handiwork: no one could tell that there was once a burrow where the hillock now stood.

“Satisfied that Kishkinda was safe from the attack of the nowtrapped demons, I staggered back to the city heartbroken at the loss of my elder brother. When the citizens, the ministers and the women of the palace saw me return alone after a year, they ran upto me with a hundred questions. For days I just kept mum, not uttering a word about what had transpired the past year. However, constant probing and encouragement from the ministers forced me to speak, and I told them everything I knew right up to the last detail.

“On seeing my concern for the kingdom and after carefully evaluating my qualifications, the ministers of Kishkinda decided that it would be apt to crown me as the king of the land. I hesitatingly accepted the honor and responsibility. I knew that the kingdom rightly belonged to my brother and then to his son Angad. But Angad was too young and not yet qualified to rule a kingdom like Kishkinda. I had to take over the reins of the kingdom to provide it the security it needed. Just as smoke keeps bloodsucking insects away, a king on the throne keeps bloodthirsty enemies at bay. With no other desire except to provide security to the kingdom, I sat on the throne. But unfortunately, I had not even sat on it long enough to even warm it, when the unbelievable happened.

Vali returned!

“Confusion took over Kishkinda as people saw their old king walk in alive and their new king agape in disbelief. Initially, people were numb with shock. Then as the reality sunk in, they began to hail their most powerful and trusted king. As Vali walked into the courtroom, I could see intense hatred in his bloodshot eyes. I tried to flash a weak smile, more to hide my incredulity than to display my happiness at his return. Seeing me seated on the royal throne with his crown on my head, Vali began to breathe heavily. He charged at me at great speed, and with a powerful flick of his backhand, he dislodged the crown from my head. As the crown clanked noisily over the marble floor, the onlookers were aghast.121

“As I had expected, my brother had completely misunderstood me. To avoid making things worse, I realized that I had to tell him my side of the story right away so that he could judge me better. As I fell at his feet, I could feel the heat of his body. He was boiling in rage, under the assumption that I had deceived him. I was trembling with fear inside, but I mustered a smile.

“Gathering all my goodwill, I began to say what I hoped would pacify him. ‘Brother, I am so happy to see you return after a triumphant victory against the enemies. In your absence, both the kingdom and I have felt so orphaned. Please accept both the kingdom and me back in your protective custody. The royal umbrella is apt only to shade your highness. I had taken on the responsibility of this throne only to protect Kishikanda from unscrupulous eyes. Please trust my integrity. What happened in the forest was a gross mistake triggered by my worst fears. Pardon your ignorant and foolish younger brother for letting you down. After waiting for a whole year, my hope of your return had begun to wane. When I heard the final war cries, I could not distinguish your voice as I had earlier. I could only hear them. I assumed the worst. The blood gushing out of the burrow just confirmed my worst fears. I panicked and my only thought was of the safety of your kingdom and the citizens. Seeing the kingdom vulnerable without a king, the ministers forced me to ascend the throne as custodian. Now that the rightful and most qualified king has returned, it is my honor to step down and return the kingdom to you.’

“As soon as I completed my plea, I received a powerful kick on my shoulder that sent me tumbling down the stairs that led up to the throne. Vali uttered the worst abuses and insulted me in front of the whole assembly. He shifted the entire blame on me and twisted the facts to seem as if I had conspired with the demons to murder him. He was so bitter about how I had so heinously betrayed his trust.122

“He then began to proclaim to the world how he had spent a whole year in that underground dungeon that led up to the patalaloka looking for Mayavi. He boasted about how he had murdered every relative of Mayavi who had dared to oppose him. After almost a year of searching, as he was about to give up and leave the burrow, he spotted Mayavi. On being seen, the demon attacked him along with hundreds of his associates. In the mass massacre that followed, Vali slaughtered hundreds of enemies single-handedly. He beat his chest about how he derived joy from the bloodbath that followed. He then spoke about his dilemma. Blood had filled up the burrow, and it had become difficult to traverse the underground tunnels and find a way to the exit without external help. Then he shot a cruel look at me and declared that I had let him down by sealing the burrow with boulders and stones and capping it with a huge mountain on top of it as well. He then went on to describe the trouble he had to go through not only to survive in that hellhole but also to smash open the exit and break free.123

“He went into a fit of anger and began smashing things. He threw all the ministers responsible for my coronation behind bars. He issued arrest orders against every friend dear to me. He banished me from Kishkinda and told me to leave with my breath and the clothes I was wearing as my only possessions. When I asked for a last meeting with my wife, he denied me even that. Although I could have fought for my rights with my brother, I did nothing out of respect for him and the hope that he would accept me and return my basic needs once his anger subsided.

“Days became weeks, but Vali’s antipathy toward me only increased. It seemed as if he was replaying the helpless horror in the hellhole in his mind and blaming it all on my betrayal.

Finally, a day came when Vali could just not contain his anger, so he came looking to assassinate me. Luckily, I got wind of it some time in advance and managed to escape from his clutches. Vali did not give up immediately. He chased me across the globe. I ran in great fear from one kingdom to another for asylum. But wherever I went, Vali managed to track me down.

“It seemed to me that the only place I would be safe from Vali’s wrath, was death. I had almost given up hope of living when I learned of a secret, a secret that Vali had concealed diligently. It was Matanga rishi’s curse on him. Vali and his confidants had managed to hide it from the rest of Kishkinda. The moment I came to know of this safe haven, I immediately relocated to the Rishimukh mountains, which was closer home and closer to my wife Ruma. Funny are the ways of the world. Vali could harm me anywhere except his own backyard. Of course it’s another matter that even after I shifted here, Vali continued to carry out his aerial attacks on me every single day. He would kick me a dozen times a day without touching the mountain and fly away swiftly. Thankfully that stopped when Hanuman came to my rescue after my father and his teacher, Suryadev, sent him into my life.

“O Rama, my existence now totally depends on the extermination of Vali. As long as he is alive, I can have no happiness or peace. Please help me get back my wife, my friends, my peace, my respect and my kingdom. Help me rid the world of this madness named Vali!”

When Sugriva finished narrating the story of his life, a wild and disturbing thought crossed his mind. It was a thought he just couldn’t dismiss, a thought that could change his decision of blindly depend on Rama as his savior. He voiced it loud and clear for all to hear. “When a buffalo demon with the strength of ten thousand elephants couldn’t kill him and an entire horde of demons headed by the mystical Mayavi demon couldn’t trap him, how can I expect an ordinary human being like Rama to terminate him? Thus far Vali has eluded death like it’s child’s play.”124

Four eyes glared at Sugriva when he said that. A monkey would always remain a monkey.

## Chapter 4

Redemption, Finally

sugriva’s tests

Bloodstains still marked the walls of the ancient, ruined ashram. Matanga rishi had shifted his new ashram to the foothills of the Pampa lake after that gruesome Dundubhi episode. When Rama saw the ruined ashram, He had mixed feelings of elation and annoyance – elation because He had the fortune of visiting the exalted sage’s original abode, and annoyance because of Vali’s insensitivity that had forced the sage to abandon this beautiful hermitage.125

But Rama had a purpose. So He pulled Himself out of the dual mood that the condition of the ashram had evoked in Him and decided to focus on the task at hand. The way He dealt with the challenge ahead of Him now would determine the rescue mission for Sita.

Sugriva had remained silent since he had narrated his story. Although his lips were silent, it was apparent from his face that his mind had been chattering away. It almost seemed as if he was reliving and replaying those painful memories in the theater of his disturbed mind. His frank expression of doubt regarding Rama’s abilities had caused a lot of disturbance among his associates, particularly Hanuman. But Rama had decided to take it in the right spirit. After all, Sugriva had never seen Him perform in reality. How could he be expected to risk his life based on stories of greatness? Lakshmana of course was most irritated at the mere thought that his brother was about to give tests of His talent and prowess to satisfy the curiosity of a stupid monkey.126

Sugriva could feel the negativity directed at him by Lakshmana and Hanuman from behind as they walked toward the Matanga ashram on the other side of the Rishimukh mountain. He tried to gauge how Rama, who was walking alongside, was feeling and what His emotional state conveyed. All Sugriva could feel was calmness and tranquility. There didn’t seem to be any agitation in Rama’s persona. Impressive! This itself was his first test, and Rama had cleared it impressively. Sugriva knew that to deal with Vali effectively, a warrior had to be able to think straight amid chaos. What greater agitation can you subject a talented person to than dealing with insult?127

Pointing to the heap of bones that lay near the entrance of the ashram, Sugriva looked at Rama. He didn’t say a word; he just raised his right eyebrow. Holding up two fingers, Sugriva indicated his expectation from Rama: two hundred bow lengths was the target set for this second challenge. Lakshmana winced at the thought that his divinely pure brother had to touch the inauspicious remains of this demon. Understanding the expectation from Sugriva, Rama nonchalantly walked up to the bone mountain. Rama appeared so small beside it. With a slight flick of His right foot, Rama kicked the bone mountain. The impact was so powerful that the entire skeletal remains of Dundubhi flew up in the air. What appeared so huge from so close a few moments ago was now just a speck in the air, ascending higher and higher into the sky. The five monkeys stared at the unbelievable phenomenon in complete awe.

In a few more moments, the bony flight began its descent and crashed, raising a huge dust storm a long distance away. From where they were situated on top of the Rishimukh mountain, the bones probably fell ten yojanas away. This was incredible! Sugriva felt like dancing. He felt like running to Vali and telling him that he was dead. He felt like shouting to the skies that he was free at last. Oh, he felt like doing a dozen and more things. Then suddenly, a thought hit him so hard that he sat down.

Rama had kicked only the skeleton the demon, but Vali had lifted the entire demon – flesh and bones. That seemed much more formidable than what Rama had just demonstrated. Sugriva’s hopes dipped again.

Lakshmana was becoming disgusted. Just by observing the changes in the contours of Sugriva’s facial expressions, Lakshmana could clearly make out the chain of thoughts churning in this monkey mind. Not wanting to appear too presumptive, he decided to wait for him to voice it out himself. But he had decided that one more test of Rama was all that he would allow Sugriva. As it is, kicking such a huge skeleton was no ordinary task. Despite his anger, Lakshmana thought it best to keep shut even when he knew what was going on was wrong.128

When Sugriva finally voiced his thoughts to the patient prince, Rama smiled at his nervousness. “Tell me unhesitatingly, what do I need to do to gain your complete confidence in My abilities?”129

Without saying a word, Sugriva got up and began to walk away. His entourage followed silently. Everyone knew intuitively that the next challenge was going to be far more intriguing and exciting than the previous one. Though it was painful to see Rama having to go through tests to prove His greatness in front of an insignificant monkey, it was also fascinating to watch the exhibition of powers He always chose to hide.130

saving grace

Soon the entourage was standing amid seven equidistant sala trees standing in a perfect circle. It almost seemed as if they were planted this way deliberately to create a feeling of being in captivity. The trees stood menacingly tall and wide, and gave one the impression of being watched. They could feel eyes digging into them. Sugriva’s words shattered the silent thoughts.

“Here is the third challenge. This is Vali’s favorite play area. He comes here once a year to test his prowess. I have watched him do something incredible that no warrior no matter how strong can ever think of doing. He considered this to be his topmost fitness test.” Lakshmana couldn’t help but smile when he heard Sugriva glorify his brother out of fear. Although it lasted only a moment, Sugriva caught that smile. He knew that what he was about to say would definitely make that smile and the overconfidence behind it disappear.

“Vali defoliates every single one of these seven huge trees at one go! I have seen him achieve this feat. From the center of this circle of trees, in one leap he jumps to the topmost branch of one tree. Then using all his weight, he bends the tree sideways to catch hold of the next tree. He then bends both the trees to grab the adjacent one. When he has three trees in his hand, he bends them forward to hold the forth one. He keeps bending and tilting the trees until he holds all the seven to form a canopy in the center. After grabbing all the seven trees, Vali hangs in the center of this circle, doing what he loves the most; revelling in the rush of bending all seven of these giant trees and holding them together.He has once told me later that the canopy felt like a royal umbrella declaring him the king of the universe.

“After enjoying the power game, he would let go off all the trees and jump down toward the center of the circle. When released suddenly, the trees would spring back to position but continue to shake before coming to a standstill. By then they would have shed all their leaves on Vali who would be standing below with his hands held high in the air, basking in the leaf shower. Within moments, all the seven trees would stand stark naked.”

This invigorating story gave the monkey soldiers goosebumps. What an exhibition of power! Sugriva continued, happy to see the effect his story had on his associates. He looked at Lakshmana expecting to see a forlorn face, but he was surprised to see that same grin of disinterest again. He clearly wasn’t at all impressed.

Sugriva decided to continue with his story. “No one, however strong they may be, can defoliate even one of these trees, let alone bend all the seven trees at once and rid them of their leaves, I know the exact might of my brother. I know exactly what he is capable of achieving as I have observed him exhibit his strength closely. But, O Raghava, I have never had a chance to see you exhibit your strength. I have had no chance to get the exact measure of your power. Then how do you expect me to have complete faith in you just by hearing mythical tales of your valor? Dear friend, do not think that I am examining you by subjecting you to these tests. Do not even think that I am demeaning you or intimidating you in anyway. Please understand that my brother’s exploits have induced cowardice in me. That cowardice is making me test you again and again before reposing complete faith in your abilities.”131

Rama patted Sugriva’s shoulders gently signaling his agreement to help him dissuade his fears. Rama then signaled him to proceed by clearly wording his next test. Taking a deep breath, Sugriva instructed Rama. “I would accept that you are strong enough to handle Vali if you could defoliate just one of these seven trees with a single arrow of yours. I don’t expect you to do what Vali did, but even if you can achieve one-seventh of what he could, I will accept that you are a worthy competitor to Vali and are worth relying on.”

Without a word, Rama began to walk away from the center of the circle of trees. The confused monkeys followed Him, looking at each other, unable to understand the importance of Rama’s actions. Rama turned around to face the center once He stood at what seemed to be the perimeter of the circle of trees. The monkeys along with Lakshmana stood a short distance away behind Rama, watching his every move with great interest. It was obvious that he was going to do something fascinating.

Rama raised His bow high in the air. Stretching the string to its limit, He released it. The twang of the bow reverberated throughout the entire forest scattering the birds and animals. Holding the bow parallel to the ground, Rama closed His eyes and murmured a silent prayer. Pulling out a golden shaft from His quiver, Rama held it in the other hand. Raising His leg and placing it on a small stony mound close to His feet, Rama swiftly knocked the arrow to His bow. Suddenly, instead of shooting the arrow, Rama stamped on the stone mound with great force causing it to be pushed into the ground.

As soon as Rama did that, Lakshmana immediately realized his brother’s intention. He remembered a story from two years ago that they had heard from Suteekshna, the disciple of the great supersage Agastya.

Suteekshna had told them about the background of these seven trees and their connection with sage Agastya. He recollected that these seven trees were in fact the seven sons of King Manibhadra of the lunar dynasty whom sage Agastya had cursed to become trees. Here too, Lakshmana realized that there was a connection with Mayasura. These seven boys had learned magic tricks under the tutelage of Mayasura. Intoxicated by the magical powers they had acquired, they fashioned a huge snake on whose back they would ride the world, causing mass fear and dread with their magic tricks. The last trick they tried cost them dearly. They had no inkling of the stupendous powers of sage Agastya when they chose him as their target. They buried their magical serpent into the ground in a circular fashion, morphed into sala trees and stood in a circle on the serpent at seven places equidistant from each other. Their idea was that as soon as the sage stepped into the circle, they would make the serpent tighten its coils and reduce the diameter of the circle. Thus, it would appear to Agastya that the seven trees were closing in on him in an attempt to crush him. But Agastya’s clairvoyance came to his rescue, and even before the boys could make their move, he cursed them to remain as trees forever. Of course, Lakshmana clearly recalled Suteekshna saying that after the boys made fervent pleas, sage Agastya had modified that curse to last until Rama came and redeemed them.132

Lakshmana now re-studied Rama’s actions and realized what his brother had done. The stony mound was in fact the jutting head of the magical serpent that lay buried deep below the ground. The seven cursed trees still stood on that serpent’s body. Something fascinating happened the moment Rama stamped the head of the serpent. Because He had pressed a vital point in the snake’s body, it jolted. The shock was so great to that magical creature that it straightened itself underground. Because the seven trees were standing on it, all the seven trees now stood in a straight line.

As soon as the trees were in a straight file, Rama released His arrow. The speed at which the arrow traveled stupefied Sugriva. He had never seen an arrow whiz past him so fast that he could hardly see its shaft. It appeared as if a streak of golden light had sped past him. The arrow pierced the first sala tree and bore into it like a drill. When it zipped out of it, it penetrated the second tree and the third and so on. The moment the arrow hit a tree, the core of the tree shook, which forced all the leaves to shed at once. Soon, Rama’s arrow had pierced and defoliated all the seven trees at once. But the deadly arrow still kept moving. It had lost neither its strength nor momentum even after penetrating through the girth of seven thick trees. The arrow then began boring into the mountain and entered it swiftly. Boring through the mountain from its peak to its core, the arrow penetrated the earth and entered underground planetary systems. The arrow then drilled through the seven lower planets Atala, Vitala, Sutala, Talatala, Mahatala, Rasatala and Patala and silently reentered Lord Rama’s quiver.133

Hanuman and Lakshmana were the only ones who understood the full meaning of what had just taken place. For Sugriva and the other monkeys, Rama’s arrow had traveled as fast as the mind and pierced through the seven trees, penetrated the mountain and somehow had mystically returned to Rama’s quiver. They could not wrap their heads around how far and deep the arrow had traveled before returning to the quiver. In fact, they had never seen trees fall into a straight file as when Rama pressed that mound. All this happened in a split second.

What was most amazing about the whole episode, was that from the moment the arrow left Rama’s bow, the bowstring vibrated violently until the time it returned to the quiver. So much had happened in such a short time.

Surgiva began to dance madly in sheer joy. “I am free! I am free!” he began chanting relentlessly as he continued dancing around his amused associates.

All of a sudden, he collapsed on his knees in front of Rama who held His formidable bow to the ground. Sugriva then placed his helmet at Rama’s delicate feet. Tears flowed freely from the wonder-monkey’s eyes as he looked up at his savior. “I have never seen an arrow move at the speed and force. What do I say of Vali? You have the power and ability to eliminate all the gods put together. My distress is gone. I am feeling joyous after years now. I humbly request you to kindly eliminate Vali today itself.”

On receiving a nod of approval from Rama, Sugriva began to whoop in great enthusiasm. “Kishkinda here I come!”

narrow escape

The idea was simple and clear.

Sugriva walked ahead with a spring in his step. The retinue trekked along gem-studded mountains that exuded red luster, walked under the canopy of cardamom trees whose fragrance permeated the mountain ranges attracting even the gods and even crossed streams that carried honey that dripped from the honeycombs hanging from trees that lined the banks. The team had traveled three yojanas from the Rishimukh mountain to reach the entrance to the kingdom of Kishkinda. From here on, only Sugriva walked ahead while the others chose to remain under the cover of the thick forest.

As Sugriva walked toward the gates of what was once his homeland, he began to appreciate the wisdom in Rama’s decision. His plan was based on the noble thought that innocents should not suffer for the mistakes of the tyrant king.

A Book on Leadership

Ramayana is a book about sensitive leadership. The way Rama deals with the bad leadership in Kishkinda and Lanka is very interesting. Rama realized that Kishkinda had only one tyrant, whereas Lanka had only one righteous person. In Kishkinda, Rama decided to destroy only Vali, and in Lanka, everyone but Vibhishan needed to be destroyed because it was a civilization of tyrants. Therefore, in Kishkinda he asked Sugriva to invite Vali for a duel so the innocent citizens remain unaffected, but in Lanka he declared war, which wiped out the entire civilization.

Sugriva watched the tall gates of the kingdom that he had once ruled, even if for a few days. Memories of the trauma and atrocities that he had suffered these past years rushed back all at once. The most painful was the way Vali took away his wife, Ruma, and kept her in his harem. The second most painful was when he fell at Vali’s feet, begging forgiveness for his mistake, but Vali cruelly kicked him around and verbally abused him in the courtroom. These were the very reasons why Rama concluded that Vali’s punishment had to be death.

As Sugriva stood in front of the gates of Kishkinda, his hatred for his brother reached a crescendo. From deep within his pained heart came out a shriek so loud that it left him surprised.

“VAAAAAAAAALLLLLLLLLLIIIIIIIIIIIIII!”

The powerful cry boomed throughout Kishkinda. By this time, the residents were used to demons exhorting Vali to a duel. As they had seen many come and go, no one really bothered about yet another shout of provocation. But when they realized it was not a demon’s call but their own banished ruler Sugriva’s, they began to speak in hushed whispers. During the few days that Sugriva had ruled Kishkinda, most citizens had taken a liking to him as their leader. They saw him as just and kind ruler unlike Vali. His violent banishment had come as a shock, and this had left a bad taste about Vali’s leadership style. They didn’t accept Vali’s accusations against Sugriva to be true. The sight of Sugriva made them emotional and groups began to discuss the possible consequences of this battle cry in huddles, careful not to make their sentiments evident to Vali or his supporters.134

When Sugriva received no response to his call, he tightened his waistcloth and yelled out yet again, challenging his elder brother.

“VAAAAAAAAALLLLLLLLLLIIIIIIIIIIIIII, COME OUT!”

Sugriva’s voice resonated across Vali’s courtroom like the clap of thunder that cracks open the sky. That very instant, Vali’s left limbs began to jerk uncontrollably. He instantly remembered the constant twitching of his left eye the previous night. Vali shrugged off the jerks and twitches as trivial and zoomed out of his palace as the blazing sun appears from behind the clouds. When he saw Sugriva stationed outside the farthest exit gate of Kishkinda, it fueled Vali’s rage. He blasted down the path to the gate to take on his betrayer.135

The sight of his traitor brother triggered an eruption of hatred in his heart. His eyes were glistening with intense loathing. As he positioned himself within attacking distance of Sugriva, Vali smacked his biceps with his palms. The smack was so loud and powerful that its vibration cracked the rocks nearby.

A little distance away from where the two monkey brothers’ had their eyes locked in bloodthirsty rage and resentment, two other brothers – hidden from the view of those in Kishkinda – stood philosophizing about the impending fight.

Rama pointed to the two charging brothers, telling Lakshmana, “It’s sad to see a kingdom that has such anomalies: a king who kidnaps his own brother’s wife, a leader that cannot forgive the shortcomings of a follower and a brother that banishes his sibling for the sake of a kingdom.”136

With rippling muscles, flowing hair, angry eyes, gnashing teeth, struggling arms and stony hearts, the two brothers collided with each other. They began to pound at each other’s chests and shoulders and threw in relentless punches, kicks, slaps, scratches and stabs.

On seeing these brothers fight, Rama was reminded of another brother back home. He shared his thoughts with Lakshmana. “In comparison to these siblings fighting for a kingdom and a woman, My younger brother Bharata is such a gentle soul. He was ready to sacrifice his life for his brother.” Lakshmana did not quite agree. With a hint of sarcasm in his words, he said, “It looks like younger brothers always behave in the same way. Sugriva, the younger one, is fighting so furiously to eliminate his older brother Vali, inspired by his quest for the kingdom..Watching them fight so mercilessly makes me feel like these two couldn’t have come from the womb of the same mother. I wonder why younger brothers behave so badly.”

Rama caught the sarcastic undertone in Lakshmana’s words right away. It was obvious that even after thirteen long years, Lakshmana hadn’t completely relinquished his anger toward Bharata and mother Keikeyi. Even as Rama appreciated Bharata, Lakshmana chose to vilify him. This was the one thing in Lakshmana Rama did not appreciate. Just because you love someone doesn’t mean you have to hate the remote instruments that inconvenience your loved one. Lakshmana’s anger could be justified as an offshoot of his love for Rama, but that was no reason to belittle Bharata’s sacrifice as greed. Rama’s quick and intelligent reply stunned Lakshmana into a shamed silence.

“What else can you expect from the monkey species but bickering? But even among humans, if all younger brothers behaved well, then how would the world know about the great glories of My younger brother Bharata?”137

Meanwhile, Vali had uprooted a huge tree, defoliated it and was repeatedly thrashing Sugriva with it. On each impact, Sugriva was flung around wildly. After some floggings, he would find himself sliding down from the topmost branch of some tree, wounded from the snapping of the branches all the way down to the ground. Yet sometimes, he would crash into the city wall denting it permanently. Although completely shaken up and battered by Vali’s powerful lashes, Sugriva managed to uproot a tree and counter Vali. Because of such aggressive smacking, both trees turned to mere sticks in their hands. Soon they began hurling massive boulders at each other that collided in midair and crumbled to gravel.

“It is interesting how love vanishes when a single difference comes in between relationships.” Rama had been conversing with Lakshmana throughout this bout. The brothers rapidly glided past the negative overtones with reference to Bharata. They never let any disagreement become a relationship barrier. Even during such a nail-biting fight, Rama shared a very interesting story with Lakshmana to make His point clearer. “The tragic love triangle in the lives of two asuras born to the Hiranyakashipu dynasty just explains this so aptly.”

The four monkeys and Lakshmana were all ears to hear this story. Their eyes were fixed to the grueling fight between Vali and Sugriva, but their ears were tuned to Rama’s equally exciting narration.

“The asura Nikumbha had two sons – Sunda and Upasunda – who were not only brothers but also the thickest of friends. Though they lived in Kurukshetra, they chose to perform severe austerities in the thick forests of the Vindhyas. Their goal was universal conquest. Although various devatas had tried their best to dissuade the brothers from their penances, they continued unfazed. When a pleased Brahma finally appeared before them, the brothers chose a very clever boon that would indirectly guarantee them deathlessness. ‘Please grant the boon that we may never be killed by anyone else but each other.’ Their presumptuous understanding that this single boon gave them a life warranty swelled their ego to disproportionate levels. They had immense faith in their mutual love and were convinced beyond doubt that they would never think of harming one another. With this conviction as their strength, they set out to conquer the universe. Thwarting powerful demigods with their prowess and subjugating planet after planet, they were soon ruling the three worlds.

“Meanwhile Lord Brahma was thinking of ways to atone for his mistake, as he understood that this universal catastrophe was the result of his benediction. He summoned Vishwakarma and orchestrated the creation of the most exquisitely beautiful damsel in the universe. The celestial architect fashioned this beautiful apsara using parts of the most beautiful objects. Because he chose the best (uttama) of small particles (tilamsa) of many objects to create this gorgeous damsel, Brahma named her Tilottama. She secretly walked into the lives of Sunda and Upasunda – separately – and won their hearts with her seductive charms. Once both brothers were totally under the influence of her charms, she confronted them together. The brothers were shocked when each realized that the other was equally mad about Tilotamma. The beautiful girl expressed her desire to marry the more powerful of the two. Their desire to win the hand of this girl was so strong that they forgot about Brahma’s boon about their deaths being at the hands of none but each other’s. Blinded by their love for a woman, they forsook their mutual love and fought until they killed one another.

“Love literally vanishes when a single cause of difference comes in between a relationship. Right now, we can see history repeat itself. Only this time, Vali and Sugriva are fighting over two things, Ruma and Kishkinda.”138

Rama then abruptly ended His narration and walked away from the scene of action. His associates were confused. Why was He walking away instead of saving Sugriva from Vali? Initially they thought that He was perhaps looking for a better place to take aim. But when they noticed that He neither looked back at them nor at the ongoing fight, they realized that Rama had changed His plan. Once that became clear, they were completely perplexed about their role. What were they expected to do now? Do they attempt to save Sugriva’s life or do they follow Rama? As the four monkeys wondered about their next action, Lakshmana began to follow his elder brother. Watching him, Hanuman followed suit. Soon all the other monkeys walked away, leaving Sugriva to take care of himself. By this time, Sugriva had nearly exhausted all his strength. His muscles were sore from the repeated thrashing, and his limbs were screaming for relief from the excruciating pain. His face had terrible bruises and his eyes were blurring. He had almost lost his ability to focus on the charging opponent, and his determination to keep up the fight began to wane like the pulse of a dying man. He desperately and repeatedly looked back for a reassuring glimpse of Rama holding His bow.

But no matter how much he searched, he couldn’t find Rama anywhere. The desperation in his eyes was like that of a beggar beseeching a rich man for mercy. Unfortunately, the one who had promised him aid was no more there. He has gone back on His word. With the appearance of this realization came the disappearance of his determination to fight. Sugriva realized that the only way he could save himself now was to flee. Vali was still pummelling him hard and mercilessly. Sugriva gathered all his strength, and with a desperate lunge shoved Vali away, completely throwing him offguard. In the split second that Vali took to recover, Sugriva made his dash.139

With limbs dripping blood, Sugriva ran as fast he could. His goal was clear; he had to reach there at any cost before Vali caught up with him. He ran as fast as his legs could carry him. Vali had power, but Sugriva had speed. So far, Vali’s power had been in charge, but now Sugriva’s speed seemed more in control. The younger monkey ran nimbly through the forest path. Jumping over boulders and fallen trees, swinging on low-hanging branches and running in rapid strides, Sugriva finally reached the precincts of his sacred space and Vali’s prohibited zone. When Vali realized that Sugriva was about to escape his wrath, he made a final dash to stop him from stepping into the restricted area. But the brisk Sugriva outmaneuvered him.

As soon as Sugriva entered Rishimukh, beyond which Vali couldn’t enter, he fell on his knees puffing and panting. A wave of relief flushed his face as he looked up at his disappointed brother. Vali also dropped on his knees equally fatigued, but his mouth was frothing with anger. When Sugriva identified that look of disappointment in Vali’s eyes, he let out a shrill laugh that held pain and irony. Sitting in front of each other, not more than ten arms’ distance, the brothers sneered at one another in violent hatred. Vali stood up and pointed his index finger at Sugriva in trembling rage. He said, “I am letting you go this time, but I will be back soon for the final termination.”

With Vali gone and off his back, Sugriva dragged his tired body into the forest and staggered up the mountain to reach his hideout. As he stumbled onto the top plateau of the mountain near the cave hideout, he collapsed on his knees.

Sugriva bent over and began to vomit profuse amounts of blood. Blood also began to drip in torrents from his eyes, nose and ears. His body was wearing out. With bloody eyes and the most disheartened look, Sugriva stared questioningly at his betrayer. What was the need for all this drama if He wanted to betray me?140

new light

“Do you mean to say there is a deeper meaning to this whole episode than what meets the eye? There better be. Right now I am so confused by Rama’s actions that my confidence in Him is degenerating into doubt,” the disturbed Taru finally decided to confide in the wise Hanuman after spending some intense moments unsuccessfully trying to decipher the crazy series of events that had taken place in the last few hours. “How could Rama do what He did? How could Rama say what He said? None of it makes sense to me. Sugriva may be convinced, but I need a more logical explanation. I can’t accept whatever happened just at face value.”141

Hanuman smiled at Taru’s puzzled state. He knew that to understand the importance of Rama’s actions required keen scrutiny of the subtle nuances of life and relationships. When Rama abruptly walked away from the fight scene instead of saving Sugriva, even Hanuman was confused. But when he observed what Rama was trying to imply through His actions, he realized the deeper meaning behind it.

When Sugriva came back all battered and browbeaten, he asked Rama a blunt question. “If You were not really planning to eliminate Vali, then why did You encourage me with an impressive display of Your prowess and build up My hope? If You hadn’t encouraged me to challenge Vali to a duel, I wouldn’t have ever dared to.” Sugriva felt deceived by someone he had begun to consider as a true friend and well-wisher.

A calm Rama told Sugriva he shouldn’t house anger in his heart without a clear understanding. Before allowing rage to explode, one should give a chance for doubts to be allayed. Without proper clarity of the motive, doubting the intention of people you love is not right. Rama then explained that the brothers were undistinguishably similar in appearance, bodily movements, ornamentation, attire and even voice that it was difficult for anyone to differentiate between them on a normal day, let alone when they were in such close proximity and fighting. Rama then patiently explained to Sugriva that if He had mistakenly hit the wrong target, then both life and the mission would be ruined. He would become infamous for killing a friend who had sought His shelter and at the same time He would thwart His hope of finding Sita. Since so many lives revolved around Sugriva being victorious, Rama chose not to take a risk. He concluded that when in confusion, the best policy was to wait for the next opportunity.142

This explanation pacified the dejected and disoriented Sugriva. However, what was very obvious to the others was that Vali and Sugriva had stark differences that could be easily noted. The most important of those was the presence of the special pendant around Vali’s neck, which had the power to grab the enemy’s energy. This was exactly what Taru was hinting at when he walked up to Hanuman requesting him to clear his doubts about Rama’s actions. While Taru’s focus was on what Rama said, Hanuman’s was on what He did after He spoke.

Rama suggested that Sugriva wear a special identification on his persona that would clearly differentiate him from Vali. Walking along with Taru, Hanuman offered him deeper insights into Rama’s actions.

“Rama is the most sensitive and brilliant teacher I have ever seen. His teachings are so subtle that an inattentive student may just miss it. What similarity between Vali and Sugriva was Rama talking about? Was it just the physical resemblance? If he was talking about that, then as you said there are enough dissimilarities like the one you pointed out. But Rama wasn’t talking about the physical likeness between the brothers but about their parallel mentalities. Sugriva was just as disrespectful and arrogant as Vali in his behavior. If Sugriva has to be a dharmic king, his disrespectful nature toward superiors had to be treated. The thrashing he received at the hands of Vali was a karmic reaction at a physical level and ego purification at a spiritual level for the disrespect the showed toward Lakshmana during their first meeting. Respect is a prime quality that binds all levels of relationships from Rama’s perspective. Rama did not take the disrespect Sugriva showed him seriously, but He took the offense toward Lakshmana very sternly. Lakshmana was not only respectable by virtue of being the prince of the reigning Raghu dynasty, but compared to Sugriva, he was also superior in every way: physically, intellectually and even spiritually. Even before awarding the responsibility of leadership to Sugriva, Rama wanted to offer him a lesson on developing the right mind-set for leadership. A leader who sits on a throne after disrespecting his superior is actually sitting on his own tomb. Blessings of superiors act like a protective shield that ensures sustained prosperity, growth and stability. Without the grace of great personalities like Lakshmana, Sugriva looked exactly like Vali.143

“Why else do you think Rama asked Lakshmana to garland Sugriva? To accept the garland, Sugriva would have to bend his head in front of Lakshmana. Even that according to Rama is the same as seeking forgiveness for his disrespectful mistake. Of course, the thrashing he received from Vali was the punishment for his mistake. As soon as Lakshmana placed that beautiful garland that he had fashioned from forest creepers and fragrant flowers on Sugriva’s neck, the wonder-monkey became distinctly different from Vali mentally by showing symptoms of humility and respectfulness. In fact, did you hear what Rama said after he was garlanded? He commented that Sugriva was effulgent now. He went on to say that it appeared as if the shining sun was wearing a string of stars. Do you know the meaning of that statement? He was hinting at a Vedic saying that whenever and wherever stars can be seen with the sun, it heralds the death of the land’s current leader. Rama was implying that Vali’s death was certain this time.”144

Taru looked at the entire gamut of events in a new light, and everything made perfect sense. He now understood that each of Rama’s actions was justified, and every word that He spoke was apt. With his doubting mind put to rest, Taru viewed Rama with even greater respect now. His appreciation for Hanuman’s wisdom escalated a million fold.

In the distance, Taru could see Sugriva walk alongside Lakshmana with that beautiful garland around his neck. Even from where he was, Taru could see a glint of newfound confidence in Sugriva. But of course, he now knew that it was not merely confidence, it was actually the result of blessings. Rama happily walked behind them, smiling. As Taru walked with the rest of the monkey warriors, one thought ran through his mind very powerfully.

It felt strange walking back to the same place a second time the same day to do the same thing. This fight was definitely going to be an interesting one, but hopefully this time, the last one.

seven luminous sages

Nila noticed it first.

He was so excited when he saw the phenomenon that he grabbed his associates’ shoulders and pointed it out to them. Everyone’s jaws dropped when they saw what they did.

Right ahead of them, as Rama walked, flowering trees stooped as if to offer their flowers to Him. To an ordinary eye, it might seem as if the heavy flowers weighed the branches down, but to the love-filled eyes of these associates, it was something remarkable.

Suddenly Rama stopped and turned around to face west. Something there had caught His attention, something fascinatingly different. He instantly knew another secret in the forest awaited exploration. When Sugriva and Lakshmana noticed that Rama had stopped walking to stare at something on the sides, they walked back and joined him. By this time, the four monkeys following them also gathered around. When Sugriva carefully followed the trail of Rama’s vision, he broke into a broad smile.145

A thick cluster of trees formed some sort of a protective wall. Surrounding the cluster was another circle of plantain trees that cleverly sealed almost any gap that remained between the trees. It seemed like a fortress no human, bird or animal could penetrate. But the most amazing thing was that in spite of such secrecy, a pleasant, soft, celestial music accompanied by a divine fragrance emanated from within the cluster.

To quench Rama’s thirst for knowledge, Sugriva told him all he knew about that mysterious place. “What you see in front of you is the most inaccessible place in the forest. Even the greatest of the gods are forbidden entry. Whoever has tried to enter that restricted area has never returned. So much so, that even birds and animals don’t dare venture into the vicinity. Within the thick compound wall of trees is an ashram that belonged to the Seven Sages. These Seven Sages, or saptajanas, as they were more popularly known, were the most severely austere sages history has ever known. They stood on their heads in a yogic posture called the sirshasana for hundreds of years. What’s intriguing is that they did so underwater. That’s not all. When performing these severe penances, they take no breaks to eat or even drink anything. In fact, they did not even breathe for long durations. Once in seven days, they would breathe in a little air. These saptajanas performed their austerities continuously for seven hundred years. These supremely intense austerities earned them the merit of ascending to the heavens in their own bodies.

These saptajanas left for their heavenly destinations eons ago, but this place continues to glow because of their austerities and purity. In fact, even now one can see the flames of the ritual fire that those pure sages had ignited.
