The thick smoke from that holy fire ascends even today through the top of the canopy of trees and forms thick black clouds that bring auspicious rains to Kishkinda.

O Rama, there is a saying among us that anyone who offers deep, heartfelt salutations to the saptajanas here in their divine abode will never have to endure bodily trauma in their lives. Let us beseech them to bless us in our missions to help each other.”146

The saga of the greatness of these mysterious sages enthralled Rama and Lakshmana. It was intriguing and inspiring stories of dedication and sincere commitment such as these had sustained their enthusiasm through these thirteen long years of hardship in the forest. This was yet another story that created a lasting impact on the minds of the brothers. Standing reverentially with bowed heads and folded hands, Rama and Lakshmana offered their deepest respects to these sages for such meritorious sacrifices that would continue to inspire thousands in the future. On seeing the brothers offer their prayers with such deep reverence, the monkeys followed suit and prayed intensely.

As the team continued their trek to Kishkinda, Sugriva felt stronger now. This time, his strength was riding on the grace he received from higher powers.147

tara’s futile plea

“VAAAAAAAAALLLLLLLLLLIIIIIIIIIIIIII!”

With renewed vigor and fresh confidence, Sugriva incited Vali to come out for the conclusive duel. Even before Rama, Lakshmana and the monkeys had taken their positions, Sugriva yelled out in excitement. Once again, memories of the thrashing he had received earlier that day, came flooding back to him. He turned around and ran toward where he knew Rama was hiding. Rama was surprised to seeing Sugriva retreat instead of marching ahead.

Sugriva rushed and fell on his knees, holding Rama’s feet. He began to cry. “My Lord, I hope You will keep Your promise to me this time. I am as dependent on You as a creeper is on a tree to sustain itself and flourish. Please don’t let me down this time.”

Rama smiled and lifted Sugriva up, holding his broad shoulders. “Don’t worry, Sugriva my friend. The goddess of fortune marked you the moment Lakshmana adorned you with this beautiful garland of flowers. Be bold and walk into Vali’s dark city to bring hope to the people, as the radiance of the rainbow brightens the cloudy sky.148

“O Sugriva, I assure you that with a single arrow of Mine, I will release you from the causeless fear of Vali forever. Very soon, this ground will taste the blood of Vali.. Go on, my friend, go and challenge that arrogant monkey to a duel with great confidence. When challenged in front of their women, arrogant people never reject the provocation. And when he comes out to fight, he will meet his end at the tip of My arrow.”

With that reassurance from Rama, Sugriva quickly sprinted back to the spot from where he had just blared the first shout. With a great passion that shook the city of Kishkinda, Sugriva screamed:

“VAAAAAAAAALLLLLLLLLLIIIIIIIIIIIIII!

The cry was so intense that the cows in the city began to run amok at the speed of horses, sprightly deer began to limp in panic and flying birds crashed into walls. The sound waves caused a tremor inside Vali’s palace. Things began to shake and rattle for a few moments after the shout. After returning from the mad chase to Rishimukh mountain, Vali had only just settled down to his usual kingdom-managing tasks, when his defeated and battered brother’s super-confident roar took him by surprise. How and where that did that self-assuredness come from? Sugriva sounds so much more assertive than the first time.149

Brushing aside all his thoughts, Vali gnashed his teeth in great anger. Such audacity to return the same day even after the thrashing he got at my hands! As he imagined his brother’s face, the bitterness began to spread all over his body. His asymmetrical teeth jutted out as he chewed and frothed in rage. His breath became more and more forceful, and his eyes were balls of fire. This was the angriest any of his followers had ever seen their master. Everyone stepped aside as Vali ran out of his courtroom. As he thumped his feet, the ground began to shatter under his weight. Just as he was exiting the courtroom building, Tara interceded, trying to dissuade him. Without considering the surroundings, Tara embraced him tightly. Even Vali was stumped; his wife had never done this before in public. When she released the embrace, Vali could see tears flowing out of her eyes. She kept shaking her head, pleading him not to go for this fight. He had never seen his queen so overcome by fear. Even when he wandered out to fight against the most powerful of asuras like Dundubhi and Mayavi, Tara never wept or obstructed him this way.

“Please don’t go in such a hurry. I beg you to fight with him in the morning. It’s already very late now. Soon the sun will set, and darkness will prevail. I can sense ill omens, and my heart is beating wildly. I sense a catastrophe. Right now, the time is disagreeable for us. Can’t you see the obvious? Can’t you see that even though you had battered him and nearly defeated him earlier today, even though he had scampered away like a rat into his rathole, your brother has developed the mettle to challenge you with so much confidence? The conviction in his scream is a borrowed one. This can’t be Sugriva’s voice of confidence. This time, he is definitely not alone. Please don’t consider my doubts trivial and unworthy of consideration.

I insist someone is with him. I have known Sugriva well enough to know that although resourceful, he never trusts anyone unless he has verified and tested his authenticity and trustworthiness. He never enters into a friendship without personal benefit. The one he is depending on has most definitely assured him of unflinching support, which is why he has returned so soon.” Tara was sobbing uncontrollably by now. Sugriva’s abusive challenges were escalating in their tenor, volume and frequency. The patience Vali had to hear his wife out was wearing thin. These discussions never really mattered to Vali. He only knew one language well, the only language he felt people actually understood: the language of the mighty fist.150

Vali pushed aside his weeping wife and began to run toward the hollering Sugriva. Just as he began running, Tara said something that completely caught him offguard. He actually stopped and turned around to face her yet again. When Tara saw the effect her one-liner had on Vali, she smiled weakly between her sobs. She had said, “I am certain that Rama is here!”

When she saw Vali looking at her intently, she knew this was her last chance to stop him. She continued, “What I am saying is not based on speculation but on solid, verified facts. I got this information from our son Angad. Spies have reported to him that two brothers born to the celebrated Ikshvaku dynasty have joined hands with Sugriva recently. Lord Rama is a legend! His arrow moves at the speed of thought. His anger burns enemies like the fire at the end of an era. His power shatters the pride of His enemies as Indra’s thunderbolt shatters the mountains. Although He is tough in warfare, He is also the abode of compassion. He easily offers shelter to the weary like a tree.151

For those who are polite, Rama is the ultimate recourse. For the agonized, Rama is the ultimate hospice. For the needy, Rama is the ultimate abode of grace. Rama is an ocean of knowledge about both matter and antimatter, mundane as well as supramundane subjects. Rama is like the Himalayas, an abode of natural good qualities.152

Rama even gave up His kingdom to follow a dharma-centric life. His mission is to remove any trace of adharma from this world. Confrontation with the prince of Kosala dynasty is foolhardy. His dynamism exceeds Indra by a million fold. My dear husband, I am telling you all this so that you reconsider your decision of fighting with Sugriva now. Do not become overcritical of him. After all, he is your blood brother. Give up this animosity with your own kin. No one is more trustworthy than a kindred spirit. Return Sugriva’s honor by crowning him the prince regent. My dear Lord, if you have any love for me, please take my words seriously and act on them.”

For a moment, Vali began to consider the implications of all that Tara said. But the very next moment, he dismissed everything as unimportant and unnecessary. “Go to the woman’s chamber and wait for me. I have accepted your words as your love for me and nothing else. But do not attempt to stop me from smashing the skull of my treacherous brother. I have reached the limit of my tolerance. I have been mild enough on him, and he has taken that too casually. Now he will get to see my sternness. Soon he will lie annihilated. As far as Raghava is concerned, I am not bothered. His very dharma will prevent him from interfering in this matter. He will never commit the sin of going against the codes of kshatriya warfare.”153

Realizing that her husband was slipping away from her life, Tara made yet another attempt to dissuade him from his suicidal venture. “When someone acts against his nature, you should know that there is something seriously wrong. It is definitely a cause for worry. Have you ever seen your brother confident enough to challenge you so blatantly? The basic rule of security is to be careful if something is out of place. Sugriva is behaving against his core nature, and it’s of serious concern. Rama is his strength. We cannot underestimate him anymore.”154

With a flick of his hand, Vali dismissed everything Tara said. This wasn’t something he was going to consider at all. He was beyond such petty worries. He said, “Even if all the three worlds combine together to war against me, they will fail miserably. As far as Rama is concerned, He is the most unattached person. He will surely keep both sides in mind while making decisions. How can you doubt that a person who has forsaken material pleasures would take sides? When He gave away His own kingdom, not wanting to take sides to abide by the principles of dharma, why would He do it here? The whole world respects Him as dharmatma. Why would He waste His valuable time on petty monkey squabbles? Rest assured, I will eliminate my brother and return victorious soon. You wait for me.”155

Tara gave up when she realized that all argument was useless as Vali was too full of himself to listen to what anyone else was saying. All she could do now was cry, knowing well the result of this duel. Defeated and broken, she hugged him closely and circled around him thrice as a mark of respect to her parting husband. Irrespective of how he was, he was still her husband, and she loved him beyond words could ever describe. Everyone had seen the cruel king, but she had seen a side of him that none had. Deep within that ambitious and ferocious leader was a very gentle soul that Vali had pushed into the deepest corners of his heart and mind. Tara then chanted hymns for the protection of her husband and invoked higher blessings. Sprinkling some grains on his head, she invoked auspiciousness and prosperity. Finally, applying a vermillion paste on his forehead in a straight vertical smudge, Tara stepped aside to allow Vali to proceed toward his destiny.

Unable to retain her balance, she ran into her chambers to cry in isolation. There was nothing she could do now; there was nothing she wanted to see now.

vali vanquished

The gigantic brothers stood facing each other, their eyes overflowing with hatred. Scars and bruises from the previous encounter were still visible on Sugriva’s face and body. With signs of previous defeat was extreme determination this time. Both the brothers tightened their waist girdles simultaneously to prepare for the do-or-die battle. In spite of all the tension, Vali was amused to see the garland around Sugriva’s neck. Huh! Who ever wore a garland to a battle? Shaking off the flippant thought, Vali clenched his fists and gnashed his teeth, determined to finish him off.156

“This fist will unclench only after your death, Sugriva!” He looked ferocious and fearsome. Even Sugriva had never seen him this angry. Vali leaped high into the air and pounced on his brother, delivering a deadly punch, and simultaneously tearing off his brother’s garland. His fist collided with Sugriva’s head with such power, that blood squirted in all directions. Sugriva rolled on the ground in pain, blood gushing from his skull. The physical pain of the wound notwithstanding, Sugriva winced at the sight of the crushed and shredded flowers from the garland. He had no time to think about its implications, for Vali had already begun advancing toward him for his next attack.

Sugriva quickly pulled himself up, ran toward a nearby sala tree, and uprooted it. As Vali raised his fist to land another punch, Sugriva turned around and whacked him hard with the uprooted tree. The power of the blow was so much that Vali began to stagger like a loaded ship in turbulent waters. Taking advantage of his momentary disorientation, Sugriva walloped him with the tree repeatedly. As Vali reeled in pain, Sugriva began to laugh madly. There was such joy in causing his brother pain. With blood and gore splattered all over his face, Sugriva kept laughing like a madman, whacking Vali with all his might.

Suddenly, Vali held up his arm just in time to shield himself from yet another blow. With his other hand, Vali landed a mighty blow on Sugriva’s elbow that made him drop the tree. Now both of them were weaponless and heavily battered and bruised. An intense tussle followed. They looked like two lions trying to tear each other apart. At times, they stood on their feet, at other times they whirled around or crashed into each other. Often their limbs collided, and sparks of fire flew in all directions. They almost seemed like two planets ramming into each other, ending all life on them.

Even the gods had assembled to watch this gruesome fight. The citizens dropped everything they were doing and rushed to witness their king in action. Of course, no one dared venture anywhere near these hulks who were at each other’s throats. When the brothers’ iron-like chests smashed together, the reverberations reached the end of the universe.

The battleground was a blood pool. Innumerable cuts, bruises and wounds meshed their battered bodies. But that did not stop them from gouging out each other’s flesh with their claws repeatedly. Sometimes they would uproot trees and whack each other with all their might. When their hands became tired, they used their legs, kicking each other hard, one leg at a time or both legs together. When their legs began to tire, they used their tails to wrap around each other’s bodies and tighten the grip until bones cracked from pressure. When even their tails became tired, they began to whirl around each other like kites in the wind.

In a while when both were badly battered, Sugriva’s strength began to wane, but Vali’s was still as it was in the beginning. The pendant had done its job: it had neatly discharged Sugriva and loaded Vali with half his brother’s strength. Sugriva understood what had happened and began to moan in pain. He was squirming on the ground, trying to stand on his wobbly legs. With blood dripping from his forehead, Sugriva began to look around for his savior. He couldn’t find Rama anywhere. Has he let me down again? The thought made him feel weaker. This time he had no energy to even run away.

Vali walked to the struggling Sugriva with a devilish grin on his face. This is it! Vali wanted to put an end to this business. He gripped his brother’s neck with one hand, placed his other hand on Sugriva’s hip and with a single heave lifted him off the ground and high over his head. With the struggling Sugriva in his tight grip, Vali walked up to a huge pointed rock nearby. Sugriva stole a glance at the diabolical rock and began to panic, well aware of his brother’s barbaric thought process. He began to wriggle intensely in an attempt to escape from Vali’s clutches. Vali began to laugh out loud; the scores of citizens of Kishkinda standing around began to cry on seeing their prince on the brink of his death.

Meanwhile, great tension had been building up among the monkeys standing next to Rama from the start of the war since they watched Vali rip away the “identification” garland within the first second of the fight. Their anxiety reached a climax when they saw their struggling master being lifted in the air. But even greater than that worry was the bewilderment about Rama’s casual approach during such a tense situation.157

Suddenly, everyone heard a deafening thunderclap and a loud whiz accompanied by the sight of thousands of birds fluttering away in different directions. Vali’s shrill laughter ceased abruptly. What happened? A collective gasp rended the air. He staggered, dropped Sugriva weakly and fell backward on the ground. No one knew what had happened so suddenly to the king. Even Sugriva couldn’t figure out what led to such a drastic change until he, and everyone standing around, noticed a golden arrow embedded deep into Vali’s chest.

Even the monkeys standing next to Rama did not notice Him choose the arrow, nock it onto his bow and release it. They were flabbergasted by the speed of Rama’s actions.158

The arrow continued to drill into his chest although he tried everything he could to prevent it from penetrating his flesh further. After piercing through and through, the arrow stopped moving. King Vali fell, covered in sweat and blood. He looked like a felled Ashoka tree. He looked like the flagstaff of Indra’s chariot that had been broken and mowed down.

Vali was writhing in pain, but his splendor and grandeur remained. In fact, he seemed even more effulgent now with three sources of luster: his glowing body, his precious pendent hanging around his neck and the shiny golden arrow embedded in his chest.159

Vali realized that everything was over now. All reasons for struggle were over forever. He closed his eyes and made his mind calm and peaceful. All the anger and hatred suddenly disappeared. All forms of attachment and desire were gone suddenly. There was only one thought in his mind at this point of time: Who is this person who is able to penetrate my impenetrable chest with a single arrow? Knowing well that the answer to his question lay in the arrow itself, he began to pull at the arrow. He knew that great warriors always engraved their name on their arrows to let their dying enemies know who killed them. As the arrow began to emerge out of his flesh, the pain became excruciating. But he was determined to know who the mighty warrior was. This desire to know was less out of vengeance and more out of respect for the skill and power of that warrior.

“RAMA”

Vali had tears in his eyes as soon as he uttered the name that was engraved on the arrow. As soon as Vali uttered the name of Rama, he could see a shimmering movement in the dark woods a little distance away from where he lay. He glanced in that direction, and though his vision was blurring, he saw something that brought a smile to his face. His hands automatically folded themselves respectfully.

A shining golden bow in hand, dressed in bark and deerskin, with matted hair and a quiver full of shining arrows strapped around His body, an effulgent golden-green personality stepped out from behind the trees with the gait of a lion!

“Rama!”160

## Chapter 5

Kishkinda’s Upheavals

shock and awe

His green, blood-rimmed eyes had never known or felt so much pain, anger or frustration. His enormous chest had never heaved as much either. His two mighty arms were never as desperate as they were now. Everything had changed. As his frantic hands gripped the tail of the golden shimmering arrow embedded deep in his heaving chest, his battered body smothered in battleground dust, he seemed like a dark cloud lined with sunset gold. It was his life’s most gruesome stretch, but the golden luster on his face still remained.

As the mighty Yayati’s pious deeds and dedication had lifted him up to Indra’s abode and Indra’s vanity and boastfulness had dropped him down to earth, Vali’s pride and glory had taken him to great heights and his arrogance and egotism left him broken and frayed like the dismantled flag of Indra.161

Apart from noticing Rama’s name on the golden shaft that was stuck in his chest, Vali discovered something else that shook him to the core. He couldn’t believe it could be true. The golden arrow, the sole cause of his imminent death, belonged to his father, Indra. How could a father do this to his son?

From Indra to Indra: Vali Goes Full Circle

When Rama put his hand inside the quiver, He hadn’t chosen a specific arrow to liberate Vali. In fact, it was a random pick. Sage Agastya had presented the arrow (which was ordained for the destruction of demons)to Rama in Dandakaranya, and was itself bestowed upon Sage Agastya by Indra. Providence had arranged for Indra’s son to be slaughtered by that very same arrow, indicating that no one with a demoniac mindset would be spared in this battle to reestablish dharma, not even the son of Indra.

Anyone shot down by that arrow given by Indra was destined to reach the heavens.The arrow impaled Vali’s chest just as effortlessly as a needle pierces a ripe banana; thus sending him back home to the heavens. Time had orchestrated the events so that it turned full circle.

History is a series of cyclic events written by the historian Time.

A painful question circled Vali’s mind. Is my father so disgusted with my actions on earth that he has arranged my journey back to the heavens?

Hurtful as it was, Vali shrugged off these disturbing thoughts and focused his livid eyes on the deceitful killer who was steadily approaching him. He had some tough questions to ask the socalled dharmatma. But when Rama came and stood in front of him, the charm of the enigmatic Ikshvaku prince made Vali smile and unconsciously fold his hands in reverence.

Noticing his sudden inadvertent submissiveness, Vali regained control over himself and gnashed his teeth in pain and disgust. Spewing venom and sarcasm, he said, “We have always heard that the moon is filled with flaws. But today, through Your despicable actions You have proved that the sun’s dynasty is more flawed. With this single careless act of Yours, You have set afire heaps of pious deeds accumulated by Your ancestors. I can’t believe that the brother of dharmatma King Bharata could commit such a heinous act. Your father sacrificed his life to abide by the principles of truthfulness. You have sacrificed truthfulness itself for the sake of Your selfishness. You are like a well-concealed double-edged sword. Your goodness tactfully covers the evil within You, just as smoke conceals fire. You gave up the opulent kingdom in style, winning thousands of hearts by playing the most-virtuous-man card. But in the forest where no one is watching You, You have flashed Your most-atrocious-man card. Your virtues are all false piety. You give others lofty advice, but follow nothing Yourself.”

Vali’s mouth had filled with blood by now. He took a moment to cough it out and continue his hateful tirade. “Looks like Your mind has been disoriented since You lost Sita. Like a crazed elephant in heat, You have trampled upon the codes of dharma and acted irresponsibly. You have eliminated an innocent person just as a snake kills a sleeping man. Why did You direct Your anger for Ravana toward the faultless leader of the monkeys? I am no enemy of Yours! Sugriva and I are strangers to You. Why did You choose to be partial and favor him to eliminate me? You are the secret of the universe. But what dark secrets do You hide within the core of Your selfish heart? You are so beautiful that no painting can ever do justice to Your beauty, but how ugly is Your character that makes You hurt others by concealing yourself? Your soul is surely dead. You are obsessed with the power of Your bow, and You run around shooting people just out of delight. You appear most gentle in Your demeanor, but You are the greatest flagbearer of unrighteousness in this world.”162

Vali’s spiteful words struck Lakshmana and Hanuman. They were pained to hear the insults hurled at their worship-worthy flawless Lord. Had anyone else been speaking these words, they would have slit his throat by now. But these were Vali’s last few moments. No point flogging a dead monkey. They chose to ignore his vindictive words and tried not to take them to heart.

Vali continued to lay bare his resentment in the harshest possible way. “You know what the world will say about the death of mighty Vali? It will say that he was killed by a mad elephant named Rama, who snapped off his girdle chord (tradition), discarded his goad (virtue), and infringed upon the conventions of the righteous Vali.” Vali’s bitterness was just as prominent as his hateful grimace. He knew that he had spoken enough vengeful words to incite Rama’s wrath and compel Him to speak. He had to know the thought process that triggered this unusual style of killing.163

Although He appeared unfazed, His crimson eyes revealed the rage building up within Him. He knew better than to react to Vali’s provocation. But He also knew that He was answerable to Vali, not only to enable his calm death pacified by replies to his burning questions but also for posterity’s sake. Many people in the future would surely raise these same questions. Rama decided to plunge headlong into His response.164

“An ill-mannered monkey with a flippant mind rebukes Me in a childish manner. Without understanding the intricacies of dharma, artha, kama and moksha, which are the guiding foundations of life, you find fault with My actions like an ignorant youngster. Your accusations clearly show your misguidedness and lack of training or upgradation under qualified teachers. If you had had the fortune of being trained under genuine superiors, you wouldn’t have spoken this way. When you analyze other’s actions and your intentions without ever serving or having faith in self-realized teachers, your conclusions will always be defective.” Rama’s words fueled Vali further. They hurt his ego much more than the arrow pierced into his body. Thus began a series of rapid-fire question-and-answer sessions that riveted everyone standing around. Probably for the first time and probably for the last time in the history of civilization was the victim arguing his own case with his killer.165

Vali presented his first argument quite logically. “What dharma are You talking about? On the basis of which principle of dharma did You shoot a stranger? I had no dispute with You – no land dispute, no clash of countries, borders, or women. And You are not even my brother to fight with me. I haven’t committed any misdeeds in Your country, nor have I ever taunted You in any way. If there was no previous incident that incited our enmity, then why did You shoot me down?”

Rama held up his right hand pointing it toward the setting sun in the horizon. “The entire earth with all its cities, mountains and woods comes under the absolute purview and authority of the solar race. King Bharata is the ruler of the cities and towns. He has kindly appointed Me to handle the forests and mountains. The day you claimed this kingdom of Kishkinda to be yours without the sanction of King Bharata, the fight for property began. I am simply meting out the punishment on behalf of the king to all those who are deviators from the path of dharma.”

“We don’t even know each other! How can You punish a stranger?” Vali wanted to know.

“A king may not know a citizen, but that doesn’t mean he cannot punish him for his misdeeds.” Rama replied.

As the discussion progressed, numerous heads turned from one side to the other trying to understand and absorb every word. This was no ordinary street talk. There was some deeper learning embedded here.

Vali guessed the direction to which Rama was guiding the conversation. To avoid being on the losing side, Vali decided to take an intelligent detour. “I am a mere monkey, and you are a regal human. I am a simple forest dweller, and You are an aristocratic city inhabitant. I am from a lower animal species, and you are a highly civilized human being. When we have absolutely nothing in common and are unrelated, why did You shoot me down?”

Rama understood Vali’s plan and decided to corner him with His own argument. “I came to the forest to hunt down an unruly animal. Hunting doesn’t mandate that the hunter and the animal be related. When animals become wild and disruptive, it is the duty of the kshatriyas to venture into the forest and hunt them down. Hunting is mostly done to save the lives of thousands of innocent citizens from the clutches of troublesome prowling animals.”166

Vali immediately came up with another argument. “Even if You wanted to hunt me down, why were you hiding? Why didn’t You come and face me? Were You scared that if You faced me, I would have sent You to the abode of death in a moment? What glory have You earned by shooting like a coward?”

Rama smiled. “When did hunting become a face-to-face game? The hunter hunts according to his readiness and not according to the convenience of the game. Whether the animal is standing or sprinting, dismayed or vigilant, or even facing away is not the hunter’s concern. The rules of hunting are not set by the hunted animals but by the hunting humans. You are not a human to set rules, you are simply a disturbing animal. How I killed such a disruptive animal is not important, the fact that I eradicated him is.”167

Now it was Vali’s turn to smile. Surely, Rama was caught in his cleverly laid snare of words. “If You wanted to hunt me down, there has to be a good reason, something I am unable to fathom. Perhaps You could help me understand this. A monkey has nothing to offer a human. If You desired skin, then a deer or tiger would be the ideal choice. If You desired nails or teeth, then a tiger or elephants would have been the right choice. If you desired hair, then elephants and rodents would have sufficed. And if You desired meat, then rodents or deer would be fine. A monkey can offer You none of these. In fact, a monkey would be the most useless hunt. Considering this, I am really confused about why You would want to hunt down a useless monkey?”168

The monkeys standing around were baffled. On the one hand was their dying king, and on the other hand was his handsome assassin. They didn’t know whom to back or what to do in this situation. Were they supposed to cry? Carry out a revenge attack? Seal the kingdom from invasion? They did none of these. Instead, they just stood by as witnesses to the strangest, most complex conversation ever between the killer and the victim.

Vali’s question made Rama serious and a tad impatient. He spoke in a resounding voice – a voice that almost sounded like a verdict from the high skies. “I hunted you down not desiring anything for Myself. As you said now, what can a mere monkey give the descendant of the prosperous Ikshvaku clan? I hunted you down as punishment for your misdeeds. Hunting down a sinner is a highly meritorious task for a king. King Bharata is definitely a kind king, but he can never tolerate devious citizens who exploit people for their own enjoyment. You may be a good king in your kingdom, but you are a bad citizen in Bharata’s empire. In fact, when a king punishes a sinner, it cleanses him of all traces of sin makes him eligible for entry into the heavens like a man of virtue.”169

Rama’s smart answers frustrated Vali, so he cross-questioned Him. “And what sin are we talking about? Did I kill a brahmana, a king, a friend, a woman or a cow? What kind of aggression did I do to deserve death as punishment? If You can’t answer this question, then I accuse You of committing the grave sin of killing an innocent. If You can’t answer to my satisfaction, then how will You face the criticism of future scholars who question your propriety?”170

“Your sin is that you have violated rajadharma and kuladharma. These two violations are enough for a death penalty.” Rama spoke with a tinge of anger. The disciplinarian in Rama was upset by Vali’s attitude. The monkey seemed so blissfully unaware of his own faults. “A king who has no idea about what conduct is right and wrong has no right to be a king at all. Misbehavior with one’s own daughter, sister and brother’s wife is considered a great sin. The moment you laid your hands on Ruma, is the very moment you became eligible for death penalty without any discussion. By forcing yourself onto your sister-in-law, you broke your kuladharma or sacred duties toward your dynasty.

A younger brother and a disciple with good character are to be showered with affection like one’s own son. When your younger brother Sugriva sought your shelter repeatedly, begging forgiveness for his foolish mistake, you tried to exterminate him rather than giving him the benefit of doubt and being kind to him. A leader who cannot give shelter to the submissive is a misleader. By abusing your dependent brother, you broke your rajadharma or sacred duties toward your kingship.

A violator of kuladharma and rajadharma is definitely a sinner of the highest order. In fact, if a king does not punish such a sinner, he himself becomes a sinner. I have not acted on My whims as per your allegation. Even a king is not independent; he has to abide by the laws of Manu and the codes of dharma. Punishment of a sinner by a king is not a whim but a scriptural injunction that has to be followed. So killing a sinner like you is no sin, but not killing you is. I would have incurred the sin of nonperformance of My duties as a kshatriya.”171

Once Rama broached this subject, there was very little Vali could do to defend himself. But not wanting to give up even at this stage and accept his fault, he played the animal card once again, hoping to get away. “All these lofty rules you talk about don’t apply to the animal kingdom. We animals enjoy the way we want to, unrestricted by these human laws.”172

“O Vali, according to your convenience sometimes you call yourself an animal and other times a civilized follower of dharma. On the one hand, you perform dharmic rituals like chanting the gayatri mantra in different oceans, but on the other hand, you imitate animals by seeking sensual pleasure. With great confidence, you exhibit your erudition by imparting lessons to Me on following the path of dharma, but you chose to follow the convenient path of instant gratification for yourself. You hurl accusations of deviation at Me, and you reward yourself with the gift of double standards. Vali, everyone knows that you are not an animal as you claim yourself to be. You are a vanara. The son of Indra cannot be an ordinary monkey. Great connections mean great responsibilities. Greater responsibilities mean greater scrutiny of actions.”173

Vali had closed his eyes by then. His faults lay bare. He could neither deny nor justify anything now. He was left with no more arguments to defend himself. Rama had shred every argument of his with precise logic. As Vali mulled over the entire discussion once again, he thought to himself that Rama was justified in punishing him. I deserve punishment for what I am, for what I have become.174

As he lay there drowning in his blood, he gazed at the serene face of Rama. His purity made him reflect about his own life. From the time he had befriended Ravana, the taste of power and domination had rubbed off on him. The desire for carnal pleasures had escalated and the consideration of stepping over lines of ethics began to wane. A wave of repentance surged through Vali.175

Now when he looked at Rama, he had no hatred, only gratitude. He began to appreciate the beauty of Rama in a different light. When he first saw him, an instant burst of awe and respect had forced him to fold his palms in veneration. But the next moment, Vali had pushed aside any thoughts of respect toward Rama and had chosen to argue heedlessly. Now as he gazed at Rama, his original spontaneous reverence for Him returned, and he automatically folded his hands in gratitude.

Four Reasons Why Rama Killed Vali Covertly

To respect Indra’s boon. The pendent Indra gave Vali had the power to transfer half his opponents’ strength to him. But if Vali had tried to acquire half the strength of Rama, his body couldn’t have handled that much power and blown up.

To punish and purify a sinner. Vali was nefarious, but he was fully aware of Rama’s prowess. He would have surrendered. In that case, Rama would have to give shelter to a sinner and not punish and purify him. If on the other hand, He didn’t offer him shelter, it would be unbefitting of Rama’s magnanimous personality. Rama could have taken advantage of Matanga rishi’s curse and driven Vali to Rishimukh mountain, where he would have met his death anyway but He chose to punish the Vali Himself and not wait for a curse to do the job so that Vali could be purified.

To keep a promise. Rama had promised Sugriva that He would bring back his wife. So He had to punish Vali who had abused her. If Vali was warned, he could have hidden Ruma in a secret place.

To protect the innocent. If He attacked Vali openly, the monkey king would have forced millions of innocent monkeys to die on his behalf; therefore, Rama wanted the attack on Vali to remain confidential to prevent the needless blood bath. In Lanka, all were as bad as Ravana, but in Kishkinda only Vali was corrupt. Ravana was born to destroy dharma, but Vali was born to protect it. However, because he became friends with Ravana, he brought the culture of Lanka to Kishkinda.

tara’s tears

The moment she heard the news, she became hysterical. She rushed out of her room through the corridors and hallways to reach the gateway of the palace, to find a city drowning in confusion. The citizens of Kishkinda were running amok, and everything was chaotic. No one seemed to know everything, but everyone seemed to know something. She even heard rumors of an invasion. She had to find out herself, for she couldn’t trust anyone to give her the right news. She wasn’t worried about any invasion or about losing the kingdom to enemies, all she cared about was the one piece of news that had rattled the core of her being. Vali is dead.

Could it be true? Tara didn’t want to believe it until she actually saw it. No matter what others said, she knew of her husband’s might. She had seen him return repeatedly from the jaws of death. But this time, an unnatural fear gripped her heart as her feet scurried toward the gates of the city. She hadn’t felt such fear even when Sugriva informed her about Vali’s death inside the mystical cave at the hands of Mayavi. More than the news, she was terrified of fear itself gnawing at her heart with every step she took toward the inevitable.176 Tara was still running, when she heard shouts.

“Shut the city gates! Seal the palace doors! Ready the army.” She saw the most prominent of Vali’s ministers barking orders at everyone. Of course, no one seemed to pay any heed. Sweat poured down their brows, and they kept looking back toward the city gates as if someone or something was chasing them. Along with shouting out orders, they were running amok too, not knowing what to do first. Tara reached out to them and tried to calm them down so they could speak intelligibly.

“Tell me exactly what happened,” The queen commanded authoritatively. “Why is there such mayhem in Vali’s capital city?”

Between breaths, one of the ministers began to explain the situation beyond the walls of the city. “Respected queen, safeguard prince Angad! The terminator Rama is soon going to seize the city. After a gruesome fight with our king, Rama hurled arrows like thunderbolts and eliminated him. Vali lies lifeless beyond the walls, Rama’s arrows drilled into him.”177

The same message again! How could Vali be dead? Tara’s mind began to race as fast as her feet, which began to take rushed steps toward the city gates, with these ministers flanking her. They tried to convince her not to venture out of the city in this dangerous condition.

“O queen, crown Angad as the new king right away, and all of us will stand by him safe-guarding the vulnerable and unprotected kingdom to the best of our abilities. If we don’t secure the kingdom right now, Sugriva and his men will seize it! They will first target the ministers closest to Vali and those who had boldly followed his example.”

Tara instantly understood what these perverted men were referring to. Soon after Vali had captured Ruma and kept her in his harem, many of his prominent ministers who had powerful positions had followed suit; they had forcefully taken away the wives of others. Now with Vali’s death, the ministers’ feared that those victimized would seek revenge once Sugriva took charge.178

Tara abruptly stopped walking!

“What use is a kingdom or even a son to a woman whose husband is no more? The only thing I want now is to confine myself to the feet of my husband.” Saying this, Tara rushed out of the city gates, with her tears streaming down and moistening the dry grounds of Kishkinda city.

forgiveness and heaven

Vali was sincere in his plea for forgiveness. With folded hands, he asked forgiveness for his arrogance and ignorance. He regretted using harsh words to malign Rama’s spotless character. He confessed to his mistakes and craved Rama’s pardon.

O Rama, every word you spoke is in perfect alignment with the codes of righteousness. Your intelligence is flawless and Your knowledge comprehensive because it covers both the cause and the effect. Layers and layers of my sins obscured my understanding. The gratitude of a lowly being like me knows no bounds because You patiently and painstakingly clarified every single one of my doubts. I was born ignorant and lived ignorant, but I am glad I am dying enlightened, only by your grace.

O Rama, the only thing I feel bad about is that You chose to approach Sugriva instead of giving me the good fortune of rescuing Your wife. Had You approached me instead, by now I would have brought Ravana coiling in my tail and thrown him at Your feet, thus making a fine display of my competence to You. Of course, I guess my past misdeeds made it impossible for me to earn the opportunity to execute that meritorious act. But now, it doesn’t matter. As long as you have Hanuman who can do any job entrusted to him perfectly, there is no need to worry.”

Why Vali Could Not Be Rama’s Ally

Had Rama sought Vali’s help to capture Ravana, He would have been following double standards. Ravana would have been punished for kidnapping Sita, but Vali wouldn’t have been punished for abducting Ruma.

Vali wanted to show the world that he was more powerful than Ravana, so he wrapped him around his tail and flew around with him. (See Adopt Patience to know more about this legend.) He didn’t want to serve Rama’s cause, for which he was originally sent. Instead, he wanted all the credit for killing Ravana. All other vanaras who were also demigods were silently assisting Rama. But Vali, instead of assisting Rama, indulged in a power game. If such a person were not killed, God would need another incarnation.

“O Lord of the world, when Tara warned me not to accept Sugriva’s challenge, something within me snapped at her. Apparently, some invisible force goaded me toward this destination. Delusion under the control of time completely obscured my reasoning power. But my instincts told me that I was going to die at Your hands today. In fact, I secretly desired the fortune of dying at Your hands.

“When You return to Ayodhya with Janaki at the end of fourteen years of exile, Your guru Vasistha will want to know the details of Your exile period. When You narrate about this episode of my death, surely Your brothers Bharata and Shatrughna, who don’t know what exactly happened, may blame Sugriva for my death. My dear Lord, I beg You not to allow them to blame my brother Sugriva for my death. In fact, I consider Sugriva to be the cause of my liberation. He is the one who actually helped me become detached from the earthly kingdom and enter into the eternal spiritual kingdom of Your grace. I no more consider Sugriva my greatest enemy. Instead, he is my ultimate well-wisher.179

O Rama, kindly treat my younger brother as You would Your own from here on. Out of ignorance, he may commit many follies, but I request You to always forgive him for his misdemeanors and never use Your disciplinary arrow on him. I am convinced that anyone whose head Your hand touches becomes empowered to not only effectively govern a small kingdom, but also the entire planet earth. Why only the earth, a receiver of Your grace can rule the three worlds.”

Vali held out his blood-filled hands toward Sugriva with great compassion in his eyes. In so many years, the two brothers hadn’t so much as looked at each other without intense hatred. This was an awkward moment for both of them. For a moment, Sugriva hesitated. The next moment he let go of his ego and burst into tears. He fell on his knees and tightly gripped the raised hand of his elder brother.180

As tears rolled down his eyes, Vali patted Sugriva’s head. He had ruthlessly tortured his younger brother for so many months. The least he could give him now was his final advice. “Shun the path of the monkeys, my brother! Only ruin lies there. Take the shelter of Rama and be trained by him for developing impeccable character. Even if you have to lay down your life to remove Rama’s misfortune, do it with a proud smile. Obedience to the will of Rama is the path to perfection.”

Seeing the unbelievable transformation in his brother, Sugriva embraced him as he sobbed inconsolably.

Rama Showers More on Vali

From a superficial reading of Ramayana, it seems that Rama is partial to Sugriva and is unkind to Vali. But a deeper understanding shows that in the spiritual perspective, Vali received much more than Sugriva.

Rama gave Sugriva access to the step-by-step path of selfdevelopment. But He gave Vali access to the path of leaps and bounds. Sugriva would take an entire lifetime to slowly develop and purify his consciousness and grow spiritually, whereas Vali, by the grace of Rama, made so much progress with just one leap that he reached eons ahead of Sugriva.

Rama gave a kingdom on earth to Sugriva, while he handed over His own kingdom in the spiritual world to Vali.

Wailing Wives

Tara’s heart-rending wails tore through the kingdom of Kishkinda. Seeing her husband bathed in blood with a golden shaft pierced into his mountainous body, Tara fell to her knees beside Vali’s body. She began to beat her chest and bang her head hysterically. Sobbing uncontrollably, she shook Vali’s motionless body. As his life was slowly ebbing, Vali drifted out of consciousness.

As Tara embraced her husband’s listless body, she resembled a female bird whose mate had been shot down by a hunter. It was agonizing to watch her charismatic husband lie there like an uprooted tree.

Old Lesson, New Situation

Tara’s sorrow is compared to that of a bird whose mate has been killed by a hunter. This hints at the first sloka of Ramayana where Valmiki curses a hunter for killing a bird. (See Roar with Courage for details.) In this case, the hunter was Rama and the victim was Vali, and it also hints at Mandodari’s cry after Ravana’s death.

From the beginning itself, Ramayana indicates that we have to learn to change our perception of situations in our lives. From the curse of Valmiki was born the beautiful composition of Ramayana. From the killing of Vali was born a beautiful era in Kishkinda. Not all cruelty is wrong, and not all kindness right.

Tara’s words pained and impressed every heart that heard her. She simultaneously expressed the sorrow of losing her dearest treasure and the highest truths of life with great eloquence.

“O my dear husband, never in my life have I ever blamed you for your actions, neither in your presence nor absence. Why then do you not talk to me now? O most heroic monkey, you were used to the highest comforts and the best of beds, why are you now lying lifelessly on the dirty soil? Why are you not enjoying your night time pleasure trips with your numerous consorts today? Is this what happens to someone who snatches away his brother’s wife? Is this what happens to someone who expels his brother in an unrighteous manner?

I am drowning in a sea of sorrow, not so much because you are leaving me, but because when you were alive you ignored my advice. Often I had warned you for your welfare, but you had always brushed me off. Time, the uncontrollable force, eventually controls every controller. Time has finally brought you under the control of Sugriva, the one you had always mercilessly controlled all your life. The pendant that you were so confident of has let you down. You cannot completely rely on anything material. Your story is a testimony to this for all time to come. As you depart happily to higher realms, you have left behind a lifetime of misery for me and your dear son Angad. Won’t Sugriva release his pent-up fury on your docile son who has always been accustomed to pampering? At least touch your son’s head and bless him so that he may handle well his life of woe before you depart on yet another long-term pleasure trip.181

“Please open your eyes; please look at me with love for one last time before you push me into the valley of sorrow forever. I am holding your feet, seeking pardon for any mistake I may have committed while serving you. Look around me. So many of your wives are here crying piteously seeing you in this sad state.”

As Vali remained unconscious, multitudes of women gathered around the fallen body, breaking into a collective wail. The wailing waxed and waned, only to wax again like the tides of a rough ocean.182

final goodbye

Tears of blood dripped from the young one’s eyes as he kneeled close to his dying father. He held his hand tightly, as if trying to dissuade his father from embarking on this long, eternal journey. He was still sobbing silently when his father opened his eyes weakly.

Hope surged in the lad. He is alive! Everyone gasped when they saw their mighty, unconquerable king struggle to keep his eyes open. The powerful Vali was now in a pitiable condition. When he saw his son Angad shed tears of blood, Vali pulled him closer and stroked his cheeks lovingly. Wanting to impart his death-time wisdom to his son, Vali spoke. “My dear son, whatever has happened to me is for my benefit. Understand that both birth and death are destined. Though I have spent my life in vain pursuits, I am fortunate to die at the hands of Lord Rama. It’s surely a reflection of some meritorious deed of my past. Always remember that Rama is the remedy for all ailments in this world. As long as you remain at His feet, you will be safe externally and pure internally. Dedicate your life to serving Him. He is the path of dharma. Serving Him is following dharma in its highest form.”

Vali then raised Angad’s hand toward Rama and pleaded. “My dear Lord, I have never been as worried about myself or Tara as I have been about my gentle son Angad. I have raised him like a delicate flower in luxury and comfort. Seeing my deplorable state has withered him. He needs your compassion and protection. Angad is very polite and strong. Politeness is often the characteristic of those who lack strength, whereas those who are strong lack politeness. The balance between strength and politeness is very rare. From now on, this rare Angad I place in Your care. Kindly hold his hand, O Lord. If he holds Your hand, he may let go, but if You hold him, I have no fear of You ever leaving him. Consider him as Your son as You have considered Sugriva Your brother.”

He then slowly nudged Angad toward Rama. Understanding the directive of his father, the youngster got up and walked toward Rama. Bending low, he touched Rama’s feet with great respect and veneration. Rama held him by his shoulders and very gently picked him up. He then drew out a golden sword sheathed in a silken casing held by his waist belt and offered it to Angad as a token of His love and acceptance. With great respect and humility, Angad received the valuable gift, as his father smiled on.

As he had no more worries about his son’s protection, Vali turned his attention to his younger brother. He pulled Sugriva closer to him and whispered his parting message. “My dear brother, once I have parted, I request you to not think about my lapses anymore. My perverted, unruly mind prompted me do all those nefarious activities. We are unfortunately not destined to experience happy times simultaneously. I hereby solemnly hand over the reins of the kingdom of Kishkinda to you. Along with attachment to the kingdom, I forgo my attachment to my ego, which made me believe I was invincible. Relinquishing my ego was far more difficult than giving up my attachment to the pendant.”

Vali then did something unexpected, something that touched Sugriva deeply. He took off his mystical necklace and held it out for his brother. With shivering hands, Sugriva received the priceless gift with tears of gratitude. He realized the enormity of what it meant to Vali. Although Sugriva knew that he wouldn’t inherit the mystical powers of the jewel, but the fact that Vali chose to give him the jewel rather than to his own son meant so much. Tears welled up in his eyes as he stared at the magical pendant in his palms. This one act of kindness melted away all the years of hatred frozen in his mind.183

Vali then made a sincere request. “I want you to promise me that you will henceforth care for Angad as a loving father.”

As Sugriva nodded his head in affirmation, Vali signaled for Angad to come beside him. Vali held the hands of Angad and Sugriva, seated on either side of him. He then said to his son, “From now on, Angad, you will be under the watchful eyes of Sugriva. You have to tolerate the pains and pleasures you will undergo during your training under him. Whether his orders are pleasant or unpleasant, abide by them. Never befriend his enemies. Don’t keep yourself too far or too close to him. Both extremes cause a rift in relationships. Respect him and always support him in every way.”184

Vali then looked toward his feet where Tara’s face was wet with tears. Lifting his fingers weakly, he signaled her to come closer to him. Tara controlled her sobs, wiped her tears and inched closer to her dying husband. Holding Tara’s hand, Vali looked at Sugriva and said, “This beautiful daughter of Sushena is the most insightful person in the world. Although soft-spoken and reserved, she knows the subtle meaning of everything. Whatever she says, take it very seriously and as priority. Whenever she gives an opinion, I have never seen anything going against her predictions. She is brilliant in the analysis of critical situations and in suggesting effective solutions. Look up to her for guidance for your own good. She is a wise and farsighted counselor. My dear brother, please do not mistreat her because she is the wife of your rival. Please look after her with care, respect and affection.”

Tara’s Father

Tara is considered the daughter of Sushena because of a very interesting tale. Tara was an outcome of the churning of the milk ocean by the gods and demons. On seeing her and knowing that she was the daughter of Brihaspati, both Vali and Sushena held her hands. Vali held her right hand, whereas Sushena held her left. Intervening in their quarrel about who held her first, a sage said that the one who has her right hand is her husband, and the other is her father. Thus Sushena is often called her father. Vali especially chose to remember this fact quite often.

Because she was the daughter of Brihaspati, the wisest being in the heavens and the guru of the gods, Tara’s intuitive and cognitive abilities were phenomenal.

By this time, Vali’s eyes began to shut. He began gasping for breath as the arrow drilled deeper into his body, edging him toward death. His strength had ebbed away. He folded his hands once again and looked toward Rama with immense love and gratitude. Vali thanked Him from the core of his heart, taking one long and last look at the beautiful form of Lord Rama. Slowly his hands slumped to his sides, his eyes rolled up and the valiant Vali was no more.

As Vali’s soul lifted to the heavens, something unbelievable happened. The arrow drilled through his body and entered the earth. After several moments, the arrow emerged from elsewhere and reentered Rama’s quiver after having washed itself in a nearby pond.

A chorus of sobs, sighs and screams erupted from all directions of Kishkinda as soon as their king was proclaimed dead. Thousands of loyal citizens gathered around their departed king to offer their last homage. Tara remained stuck to Vali’s body as if that would prevent him from leaving her. The other women tried to pull her back, but she shrugged them off and fell on her husband’s body again. It seemed as if all grief in the world had descended on this small kingdom.

Lessons from the Vali–Sugriva Episode

For Vali, the son of Indra, mental pleasures were of paramount importance. For Sugriva, the son of Surya, intellectual pursuits are of paramount importance, and he abides by illumining wisdom.

The story is about how the mind (Vali) eliminates wisdom (Sugriva) and dominates life. The mind is not just satisfied with kicking away wisdom from life, it tries repeatedly to eliminate it, as Vali tried to kill Sugriva. The mind cannot accept any rebellion against its dominion.

Sugriva befriends Rama who is the fountainhead of all spiritual wisdom. Rama strikes Vali from behind the tree called samsara or laws of nature or more accurately called the law of karma. To Vali, Rama is invisible since He doesn’t show any inclination to spiritual pursuits and neglects Rama. But to the virtuous Sugriva, Rama is not only visible but is also the friend he uses to establish himself in the hearts of others.

Tara literally means “pupil of the eye.” She represents the power of sight. She is capable of seeing the unseen. But the corruption of the mind is so high that despite her wisdom, she was unable to influence Vali to perceive the obvious truth.

Finally, the mind wills a confrontation with the Supreme Lord and welcomes punishment at his hands. In reality, however, the Lord does not punish the unruly mind but purifies it completely. Then vision is restored, and Rama becomes visible.

sorrow’s solutions

“Life is like a water bubble. Sooner than later, it will burst. When the bubble pops with time, don’t lament. It is an inevitable natural phenomenon. Rather than crying over the past, prepare for the future. Don’t worry about your husband, he has gone to a higher destination. But Vali has left a huge responsibility on your shoulders: Angad. Focus your energies on fulfilling that now.” On seeing Tara completely broken down, Hanuman felt compassionate for her and tried to counsel her with these words of wisdom.

“Nothing is my responsibility now. Everything worth living for is over. Angad and this kingdom are Sugriva’s concern now. I will follow my husband,” replied Tara.

As Tara intensified her desire to enter her husband’s funeral pyre, the monkeys began scurrying around to arrange for the royal funeral procession. Just then, Sugriva broke down and fell at Rama’s feet, clutching them very tightly.

“I don’t want the kingdom anymore!” As soon as they heard Sugriva’s refusal, everyone froze. What is he saying? How can he be saying this?

Sugriva continued crying out his heart as others looked on. “My heart has lost interest in the kingdom that is tainted with my brother’s blood. My intolerance provoked me to desire his death, and my ignorance fanned that desire. My only desire now is to spend the rest of my days in the company of saintly people on the Rishimukh mountain so that I may gain knowledge to vanquish my ignorance. How can I now sit on the throne that will constantly remind me of my wrongdoing? Indra acquired the sin of killing Vritrasura, the creation of Tvastha. Similarly, I will acquire the sin of killing my own brother. The earth, water, trees and women shared the burden of Indra’s sin, but who will share the burden of my sin of eliminating my brother? I can’t rule a kingdom that I have just ruined. I feel utterly miserable now. The sin of killing my brother is affecting my mental peace as a mad elephant tramples trees. O Rama, I can assure You that Your mission of finding Sita will be accomplished even in my absence. Please permit me to die along with my brother. I can’t live anymore.”185

Rama’s eyes moistened on seeing the intense emotions of Tara and Sugriva. Just then, the fawn-eyed Tara picked herself up and walked toward Rama in extreme anger. She wanted answers. This was the first time Tara noticed Rama. All along, her focus on her dying husband was so intense that she was blind to her surroundings. As she glared at Him, she began to think deeply. So He is the one Angad had warned me about, He is the one who has killed Vali and He is the one saints rave about. She rushed toward Rama with a mouthful of swear words. But as she neared the pure Rama, the two weak humanly traits – anger and folly – abandoned her, and she began to eulogize Him instead. Just seeing Rama’s beauty awakened her pious, loving nature. Perfectly composed poetry began to flow from her mouth that expounded the qualities of Rama.

*tvam apremeyasya durasadasya*

*jitendriyah uttama dharmikasya*

*akshaya kirtisya vichaksanasya*

*kshitiksamavan satajopamaksha*

“Tvam –You resemble a pillar that holds the burden of so many divine attributes. Just as all minerals are found in the heart of the mountain, all good qualities rest in You as comfortably as a walnut in a hand.

“You are apremeyasya. You are beyond the perception of even those who understand every nook and corner of the Vedas. Then how can a mere monkey like me even think of understanding You. You are so durasadasya –subtle – that You are unreachable, untraceable and forever remain hidden within the Vedas.

“You are jitendriyah. Not only are Your senses under Your complete control, but You also control the senses of those who see You. You could have easily claimed the kingdom of Kishkinda as Yours as You dethroned the king, but You chose to hand it over to Sugriva. People consider me one of the most beautiful women in creation. One who sees me just once has to steal another glance. But I have observed carefully that You haven’t even glanced at me once from the moment I have come here. More than that, I came here to abuse You in anger, but look at what I have ended up doing. I am eulogizing the killer of my husband. You have completely captivated me.

“Perhaps Your greatest quality is that You are uttama dharmikasya. Most people in this world are adhama – highly selfish. They can trouble others to any extent for their benefits. Very few people are madhyama – mature. They allow others to prosper, too, while taking care of their own benefits. ButYou are the only rare personality who is uttama – evolved. For the benefit of others, You are ready to suffer even personal losses. For the welfare of society, You have taken a blemish on Yourself by killing Vali from hiding.

“You are akshaya kirtisya – even if people slander You, You can never be defamed. Actually, no one can really blame You, because You are vichakshanaya, one with a high caliber. Of all the wrong that my husband did, he at least did one good while dying. He questioned Your actions in detail and allowed You the opportunity to clarify. He was so impressed with Your answers that he surrendered his life to You before dying.

“Although You are dealing with restless monkeys like us, You are kshitiksamavan – very patient. That’s because You are satajopamaksha – one who has reddish eyes like a lotus. Eyes turn reddish due to anger, grief or fondness. But Your eyes are red due to none of these reasons. Your eyes are red because of lack of sleep. You are so concerned for the welfare of the whole world, that You never sleep and are vigilant and absorbed in thoughts of protecting ungrateful people like us.”186

When Tara stopped speaking, everyone around was stunned by the nectarine flow of words and subtle depth in their meaning. In fact, Tara stunned herself the most. She had never composed exquisite poetry with such deep inner meaning. She folded her hands, realizing that Rama’s mere presence had extracted the best out of her. Within a few minutes, He had impacted her in a way noSacrifice is the adventure of giving up one good for the sake of a greater good. one ever had. She knelt at Rama’s feet beside Sugriva and gazed at Him with veneration.187

Tara made an unusual appeal. “Vali has gone to a higher destination after being shot by an arrow from Your bow. I beg you to kill me with the same arrow and let me join him wherever he is. By uniting the soul of a wife with her husband’s, You will incur neither any sin nor any blemish for killing a woman. The sages of yore have declared that there is no greater charity than bestowing a wife to her husband.”

Smiling with great kindness, Rama looked at both Tara and Sugriva who were at his feet and who were eager to die. “The creation of the creator is designed to bring both misery and joy. One should learn to embrace the will of the Lord that is spoken through the agency of destiny. Time has no kinship, and therefore is impartial. No instrument in this world can override the influence of time. Instead of mourning for the past, which is no longer in your control, you should prepare for the future by listening to the silent whisperings of nature. Kishkinda needs a king, Angad needs the security of able parents, Tara needs a husband’s protection and Sugriva needs to fulfill his promises to his dying brother. O Sugriva, it would be best for you to accept Tara as your wife and Angad as your son. Angad should be appointed as the crown prince of Kishkinda and the heir to the throne.”188

This suggestion hit Sugriva and Tara like a thunderbolt. But in a few moments, together they realized that this was the most practical and correct thing to do. Rama helped them to their feet and reminded them that life was not for enjoyment but an opportunity to serve society and God. “When you take life as an opportunity to enjoy, it leads to exploitation. When you seize it as an opportunity to serve, it leads to sacrifices. Your continuing to live is now not to enjoy life, but is a sacrifice to serve all life.”

Then Rama instructed Lakshmana to organize the entire funeral ceremony of the late king Vali with the highest degree of respect and honor.

Very soon, a royal procession followed monkey chiefs who shouldered the departed king in an ornate palanquin. A prolonged wail arose from every corner of Kishkinda as smoke from the sandalwood pyre rose up into the air.

The most powerful king that ever ruled Kishkinda was now fragrant smoke.189

hope and despair

Sugriva was dripping wet as he fell on his knees in front of Rama. Rama, seated on a flat rock, looked on. The eminent ministers of Kishkinda stood around in a semicircle like a backdrop. Hanuman was on one side of Rama and Lakshmana on the other.

“No, I can’t come,” Rama said bluntly.

Why is He so insistent on not wanting to come for the coronation? Sugriva could not think of any reason because he couldn’t imagine being coronated king of Kishkinda without the person responsible for his becoming the king. But Rama’s bluntness stunned him. After repeated requests, Rama said, “I have vowed not to enter a city or town during the fourteen years of exile and have decided to restrict Myself to the forest. I depute Lakshmana to grace the ceremony on My behalf.”

Hanuman requested Rama to allow him to remain by His side from now on. “With this change in the king, the security of Kishkinda is at its weakest. You first strengthen the security systems and correct all lapses. Once you do all that, you may come to Me. I will stay on this Malyavan hill190 for the next four months of the rainy season. Any kind of exploration is impossible during the monsoons. O Sugriva, at the advent of Kartik month, post the rains, you have to ready the monkey army for the grueling search mission. Go! Establish yourself as the king of Kishkinda and rule justly.”

Thousands and thousands of monkeys entered the city of Kishkinda for the celebrations. This was the beginning of a new era in their lives – an era of hope, an era of righteousness and an era of celebrations.

Meanwhile, far away, the two brothers stood on the Malyavan hill watching the dust raised by the rejoicing monkeys. As the celebration reached its conclusion, sadness gripped them again.

Just then, raindrops began to fall, as if teasing them, saying The countdown to the mission has begun, but there was a long way to go. Not before four full months of pure misery.191

Ponderings about the Happenings at Kishkinda

Rama tells Sugriva to challenge and fight Vali. Sugriva asks Rama, “Who will kill him?” Rama says,“I will kill him, but you fight.” This is the essence of Bhagavad Gita also, where God says, you do your duty, and I will give the result.

Vali tells Tara that he is not scared of Rama because he considered God as being equal to all. This actually means he expected God to be equal to both the sinner and the pious. This thinking of God being equal made Vali cruel.

Vali was proud of his body and attached to his son. He frees himself from pride by falling at Rama’s feet, and he frees himself from his attachment by handing over his son to Rama.

Rama chose to befriend Sugriva instead of Vali. An interesting reason why Rama chose Sugriva was that He knew that although that Vali was stronger, Sugriva was a faster sprinter. This guaranteed that he couldn’t be caught. It’s important to run so fast that Pride cannot catch you.

All demigods, on Lord Brahma’s instructions, had taken the form of monkeys waiting for an opportunity to serve Rama. But Vali, instead of waiting for Rama, decided to go ahead and take the credit of defeating Ravana.

Hanuman went to Rama by himself, but Rama came to Sugriva. Rama asks Hanuman, “Why is Sugriva not coming down to meet me? Hanuman replies, “He is tired of running all his life and is in no position to take another step.” One who is capable goes to God, and God goes to the one incapable. Because Sugriva shared all his weaknesses with Hanuman, he recommended that Rama go to Sugriva. When you open your heart to a saintly person, you bring God into your life.

## Chapter 6

Rewards of Patience

raining sorrow

“Sita! Sita! Sita!” With eyes closed and mind absorbed in rapt attention, seated with an erect back in the lotus posture of an advanced yogi, He fingered a string of beads as He constantly chanted the single word mantra over and over again. The entire spacious cave reverberated with the chants of the name that had become the center of their lives.

Seated in one corner of the cave, Lakshmana quietly observed his divine brother seated at the rear end of the cave, completely absorbed in thoughts of His wife. As incessant, pouring rain soaked the outside, the pure vibrations of Sita’s name drenched the inside of the cave. More than a month had passed since Vali’s elimination and Sugriva’s coronation as the king of Kishkinda. Occasionally, when the intensity of the downpour waned, they could hear the sounds of the revelries in Kishkinda. The sounds of music and celebration there reminded them of their lamentable condition. While the monkey king celebrated his victory, the brothers helplessly grieved on top of the Malyavan hill in the precincts of the Rishimukh mountain range. With the departure of summer, monsoon had converted the mountain into a beautiful carpet of colorful flowers and it appeared to rejoice as Sugriva after the departure of his sorrow.192

It pained Lakshmana to see his brother in this state. It pained him even more when he realized that he was helpless for as long as the rainy season lasted. They simply had to endure the pain of separation from Sita, patiently waiting for the change of season. The chatur masa (the four months of monsoon) was considered the time of sleep for the gods. It almost seemed as if no prayers would be heard until the season changed.193

The cave’s location was ideal in that there was hardly an inrush of rainwater and they could get a bird’s eye view of the flora and fauna of the region. The constant showers gave birth to scenic waterfalls, rapids and cascades. A lake filled with lotuses close by took care of their needs. Inside the cave itself was a broad and even-surfaced massive black stone, which served as an ideal place for repose. Not that the brothers slept at all – while Lakshmana was sleepless out of concern for his brother’s safety, Rama had no sleep out of His concern for Sita’s.194

Rama spent most of His day in deep meditation and chanting of His beloved’s name. Those were the relatively more peaceful times of the day for Him, but the times He spent gazing out of the cave at nature or speaking to Lakshmana were the more painful ones. Everything Rama saw invariably reminded Him of His separation from Sita. He used analogies to compare everything He saw in nature to everything He was going through.

Rama compared the formation of black rain clouds with pregnancy, which is nine months long. He analyzed that after the sun’s rays impregnates the clouds, they absorb water from the oceans and take nine months to form and deliver the rains. Nothing in this world happens instantly. Everything in nature tests one’s patience before offering a reward.195

The earth that becomes unbearably hot in the scorching summers vents out hot water vapours when the first rain hits the ground to rid itself of the agony of heat. Similarly, Rama felt Sita must be shedding humid tears amid the parched heat of separation.

There seemed to be a celebration everywhere in the forest, the myriad sounds of the forest resembling a musical symphony. The croaking of the frogs, the humming of the bees, the rumbling of the clouds, the gurgling of the rivers, the songs of the multicolored birds and the rhythm of the raindrops all combined to provide a musical platform for the dancing pride of peacocks to perform. As if to decorate this stage, the colorful flowers and luscious fruits hung from trees or fell all over the forest floor forming beautiful patterns. The earth wrapped itself in parrot-green grass that had red insects crawling over it, resembling a woman wearing a green sari with embroidered red spots. The waterfalls were like a pearly necklace on the mountain and resembled a well-to-do nobleman.196

Amid all this joy, only two people were most miserable. The source of joy for the forest was the source of misery for them. Although at most times, the rain was the harbinger of fond memories of Sita, there were times when Rama donned a more philosophical approach and shared with Lakshmana some profound realizations that the rains had brought into His mind. Lakshmana silently waited for these gems of wisdom to fall into his lap. He had decided to remember each of these nuggets and write them down whenever he got a chance later. Rama would share yet another wisdom-nugget with Lakshmana all of a sudden and then return to either chanting Sita’s names or to his reverential observation of nature’s downpour.

Once Rama saw streams of water coming from various directions from the mountaintop and forming a water pool, and He thought of an interesting analogy. He said, “This is exactly how commendable virtues find their way from all directions into the heart of a noble soul, and he need not go searching for them.” When He saw new leaves on the trees, He said, “They look fresh and green like the cheerful mind of a struggling soul who has attained spiritual wisdom.” After watching the rain batter the mountain for a long period, Rama commented, “Just as the mountain stands unmoved in spite of all the battering by the rains, a saintly person endures the harsh words of the wicked and is totally unaffected.” When He saw heavy clouds filled with water hang low near the earth, Rama compared it to an intelligent person who grows in humility with the growth of his knowledge.

One still and silent night, a sudden rush of hundreds of fireflies began to gleam in the darkness of the cave. Rama said, “These fireflies are like a band of hypocrites who emit light not to decrease darkness but to announce and show off their presence.”197

For Lakshmana, days began to roll along in complete servitude to his brother. For Rama, each passing day served as a grim reminder of His lost treasure. After three months, one day as he sat on the outer precincts of the cave, He saw repeated flashes of lightning that lit up the entire sky for a few seconds each. Tears began to roll down His cheeks and turning toward His brother, he said, “The flashes of lightning that brighten everything and then disappear in a moment remind me of the fickle friendship of wicked people, who show a lot of affection one moment and are not to be seen later. Every person I trusted and who had made big promises have all stealthily disappeared from My life. I hope the monkey king Sugriva hasn’t forgotten his promise of friendship while being absorbed in his own revelries. This rainy season is unbearable for Me. There seems to be no end to My misery. After the coronation, Sugriva wanted to send a search party for Sita and to prepare for war against Ravana immediately. But I dissuaded him from this project because this wet season leaves the paths unfit for travel or invasion. Further, Sugriva suffered greatly in many ways and for a long time and got back his wife only now. So I advised him to enjoy his good fortune until the end of rainy season. I am hoping that after the rains, he will come to Me on his own and not forget the good I have done for him. I have to wait until this season is over, and I can do nothing beyond hoping for his goodwill.”

Lakshmana reassured Rama of Sugriva’s integrity. He urged Rama to keep the fire of anger under control for a month longer. “As soon as autumn comes,” he assured his brother, “I will light the fire that will burn down Ravana’s kingdom.” Seeing no other recourse at the time, Rama resigned to chanting Sita’s name, waiting for the season of hope to come.198

power-drunk ingratitude

It seemed that the mood of the kingdom changed with the king: from living a life in fear to living a life of revelries, from tension to fun and frolic. Sugriva, the new king, seldom held a court. He handed over charge of the kingdom to his ministers and preferred to immerse himself in sensual pleasures – pleasures he had been deprived of for eons. It was quite natural for subjects to follow the example set by their king, and they did it diligently. The ministers, too, chose a life of pomp and luxury, which encouraged the citizens even more to seek and indulge in extravagance and merrymaking.199

Different as he was from the other vanaras, Hanuman had only wanted to be by Rama’s side at all times. So when Rama pushed him to assist Sugriva in Kishkinda and help the new king stabilize the kingdom and its systems, he felt extremely hurt. But now, after the way things were in the kingdom, Hanuman began to appreciate the visionary in Rama. Rama had rightly expected this to happen.

Over the last four months, as instructed by his master, Hanuman silently and diligently carried out his duties.
