Rama smiled at that last comment.250

## Chapter 8

So Far, Yet Sonear

futile search

As they sat on the rocks of the Sudarshan island, the monkey chiefs discussed their next move with Vinata, the chief of the vanara troop directed to search in the eastern regions. “There is nothing beyond this.” “We cannot venture any farther.” They said. They had no result to share with Sugriva. Or, could not having a result itself be a result?

The vanara chiefs recollected how their easterly adventure had begun with a search of the Videha kingdom or Mithila (Sita’s birthplace) as instructed by Sugriva. Then swinging from tree to tree, the vanaras had thoroughly checked the river beds, the river banks and areas adjacent to the Bhagirathi, Sarayu, Kaushiki, Yamuna, Saraswati, Sindhu, Shona, Mahi and Kaalamahi rivers. They looked through every forest and mountain near these rivers including Mount Kalinda.

Probably the most expansive range that they had to scout were the kingdoms of Brahmamaala, Maalva, Kaashi, Kosala, Magadha, Pundra, Anga and the provinces of the Kosakaara kings packed with silver mines. The most dangerous terrains were the Mandara mountain ranges where they spent a good chunk of their time and energy putting everything they had at stake, including life itself. These terrains had some of the oddest and fiercest creatures they had ever come across. With odd physical features or lack thereof, they looked ghastly. Some had no ears, while others had their lips as their ears. Some of their faces seemed to be made of some ironlike substance. Most were single-legged but could move at speeds greater than that of the four-legged animals. The vanaras also came across the scariest half-human and half-tiger beings. The vanaras agreed that this was the most dangerous and life-threatening episode.

Beyond the Mandara mountain, they searched the whole of Jambudvipa and went further eastward. They had to climb to reach some places, hop to some, travel through treetops to some others, swim to reach some and take the boat to reach the island of Yava. They explored seven kingdoms in and around the Yava islands and then reached the Shishira mountain, which had heaven-kissing peaks. Then they had to ferry across the reddish speedy waters of the Shoha river to reach the Plaksha island, which was home to vast mountains with many pleasure gardens. The island was amid the Ikshu samudra or the salt ocean, which had massive waves. Crossing this ocean was very dangerous because it housed demons that captured their prey with their shadows. Beyond the Plaksha island, the vanarasena had to navigate through the Lohita or the Red sea and reach the Shalmi islands. Crossing several mega oceans, they reached the Jaatarupa shila, which was a massive stone spread over thirteen yojanas and resembled a golden python. Beyond this was the place known popularly as Mount Sunrise or the Udaya mountain. Every morning the sun first emerged here, seemingly rising from within the mountain. Just adjacent to the Udaya mountain was a small island known as the Sudarshan island, which is where the monkeys were now stationed and discussing about how Sita was nowhere to be found even after the massive search.

They had searched high and low, scouting every tiny space possible. Time was ticking away, and their deadline was approaching fast. Sugriva had warned them not to venture beyond the Sudarshan island, but, at the same time, he had also warned them of the dire consequences of making a mistake in their mission.251

Concluding that there was no other choice, the chief Vinata decided to order an exit. Soon the entire troupe was at Sugriva’s feet. Vinata presented an elaborate search report and concluded: Sita not found!

failure again

Led by Shushena and directed by Archisman and his brothers, the mighty Archismalayaas, the group that went to the west reached just after the eastern group. The two hundred thousand monkeys led by them returned a day before the thirty-day deadline set by Sugriva. They explained their journey in brief to their master.

Sushena said, “As instructed, we began by exploring the Saurashtra, Bashlika and Chandrachitra provinces. We explored the rural areas and woods filled with exquisite trees and entered the western quarters. We then searched through and through and reached the western ocean, which had dangerous sharks in the deep waters and crocodiles near the shallow shores. We went on to search through the beach cities like Murachi, Jaatapura, Avanti and Angalepa. We even thoroughly explored the forests around Angalepa town. We then came across the golden mountain named Hemagiri, which is the habitat of flying lions and mammoths. The sea at the foothills of this mountain is filled with bloodthirsty sharks and dangerous elephant seals.

“As you had warned us, we came across the waterlogged Paariyaatra mountain with its peak that rose hundred yojanas high. Millions of gandharvas live on this mountain. As you had instructed, we made sure that we did not provoke these gandharvas by plucking the fruits or misbehaving. We then came across the hundred yojana Mount Vajra, which was shining like lapis lazuli. Probably the most fascinating part of the mission was the search through Mount Chakravana on which the celestial architect Vishwakarma had constructed a thousand-spoked wheel that Lord Vishnu had once used to kill the horse-faced demon named Hayagriva. The wheel weapon was under the possession of that demon until it became the permanent property of Lord Vishnu. In fact, we discovered that Lord Vishnu had also killed the Panchajanya demon on that very same mountain and taken away his conch-shaped backbone. We explored that mountain with great joy and experienced a sense of happiness for being in such an historic place. Right in the middle of the ocean was our next destination: Mount Varaaha with its golden peaks. On that mountain is situated the city Praagjyotishpur, the city of Demon Naraka. Without disturbing him, we scouted every nook and corner of his city.252

Venturing farther west, we arrived at Mount Meghavanta, which had massive waterfalls. Searching this mountain was indeed a task as it was filled with thousands of lions, elephants and wild boars that were always hungry for flesh. As we left the base of Mount Meghavanta, we reached a very interesting place that looked like a range of small mountains. There were sixty thousand mountains in all. We divided ourselves into small teams and explored each mountain simultaneously. From there, we went to Mount Meru. Everything and everyone residing on that mountain emits golden rays like the sun. From the peaks of Mount Meru, celestials like the Vishvedevas, Vasavas and Maruts worship the setting sun. Ahead of Mount Meru is Mount Astagiri, also known as Mount Dusk. Each day, the sun sets into the Astagiri mountain. Because you had warned us from venturing farther than this, we returned from there itself.

“Although we searched completely, meticulously, painstakingly, cautiously, consciously and attentively, we are sorry to give you this report: Sita not found!”

still nothing

“We have experienced everything that Sugriva had told us as it is. I wonder how our master knows so much, and with such accuracy. If we hadn’t been forewarned by him, we would never have armed ourselves with so many supplies.” One of the monkeys remarked on the predicament the vanaras found themselves in as they ventured toward the north. They were stranded on barren wasteland with nothing on it for about a hundred yojanas – no trees, no mountains, no living beings!

From the time they left Kishkinda, the hundred thousand monkeys under Shatabali traversed constantly for fifteen days through the provinces of Mlechcha, Pulinda, Shurashena, Prasthala, Bharata, Kuru, Madraka, Kaambhoja, Yavana, Shaka, Darada. They passed through the Soma hermitages and mounts Kala, Sudarshana and Devasakha before eventually reaching this painfully long stretch of starkness.

Eventually they managed to complete the barren stretch, which led them to the base of Mount Kailash, which was the location of the magnificent mansion of Kubera, the treasurer of the gods. From there, they ventured through Mount Krouncha, which had many tunnels; the treeless mountain; Mount Manasa, which was home to numerous varieties of birds and Mount Mainaaka on which the mansion of Mayasura was situated. To their shock and dismay, the female residents of that place were horse-faced and were called kimpurushas. Tiding across deep-flowing rivers, trekking across mountains and traversing the forests, they reached North Kuru where the rivers have lotuses with golden petals. After scouting North Kuru extensively, they reached the golden Mount Soma, in which was situated the gateway to heaven.

Shatabali reminded the monkeys about the boundary set by Sugriva beyond which they could not venture. Quietly, the monkey army retraced their steps to Sugriva and accepted their defeat. They had failed in their mission. They chorused: Sita not found!

hope wanes

Sugriva was very upset with the three divisions returning with negative reports. Turning to Rama, he said, “As long as Hanuman’s division doesn’t return, I won’t lose hope.”

Rama was sitting inside the cave in a lotus posture with His eyes closed, meditating like a sage. Every time Sugriva entered the cave to give news of the return of one of the divisions of the vanarasena, Rama would open his eyes for a moment. As soon as Sugriva gave a negative answer, He closed His eyes again and returned to His meditative trance.253

Only half a day was left now for the thirty-day deadline. Yet there was no sign whatsoever of the fourth division of the monkey army. For Sugriva, the last twenty-nine days were a breeze, but the tension and anxiety the last day brought made this one day seem longer than the twenty-nine days that had gone by. Every second moment, he gazed up at the sun to see if it was moving faster. Something in his heart told him that the monkeys headed by Angad that went to the south would return with some positive news.

The only thing left to do was wait patiently.

He was dying to hear those three golden words: I found Sita! But they seemed to elude him. Instead, all he had heard repeatedly thus far were three painful words: Sita not found!254

dark cave to golden city

Meanwhile, in the south, the monkeys had been searching for twenty days now. Yet no trace of Sita; not even the minutest hint.

The vanarasena led by Angad had minutely rummaged through the entire southern region as suggested by Sugriva. The first few days were very hopeful and intensely exciting for the monkeys. But as the days passed and the terrain got tougher, the excitement began to wane. The excitement was at its lowest as they trudged through an excruciatingly painful wasteland. They had already been crusading through this terrain painstakingly for the past two days and yet it seemed endless. Throughout their movements in this land, they had not spotted a single living thing, not even a living tree! This ghastly place seemed to be totally devoid of life. Once their water reserves were exhausted, they realized this land did not even possess a source of water. The lakes were just dry pits and the rivers dry pathways.

The powerful vanaras that had shoulders akin to thunderbolts and were powerful enough to destroy any army were very distraught. The heat caused them to perspire profusely. Their minds were very agitated. Their limbs began to ache and tremble. Their tongues began to hang out. Wherever their feet touched the dry scalding earth, boils began to appear on their soles. They unanimously felt that they were traversing through hell. When they inquired from Jambavan about the reason behind this land being so uninhabited, he told them a story.

“There was a reasonably powerful sage named Kandu whose young son died here, at a time when this place was a forest. As his son was dying, Sage Kandu couldn’t find anyone who could help him. Everyone and everything in that forest seemed indifferent to his need. Even the plants that grew there didn’t yield the right medicinal herbs to save the boy’s life. The sage became distraught with the loss and did not know whom to blame for his son’s death. So he cursed the entire forest to become uninhabitable and bereft of all birds and animals.”

The vanaras were saddened that such a wise sage decided to transfer the pain of his heart on to so many people for so many generations. Was it so difficult to handle pain personally? Yes it was. The vanaras were living the pain themselves. Although they could empathize with the pain of sage Kandu, unlike him they decided not to transfer their pain on to someone else.255

Although the journey through the dry land was agonizing, the vanaras continued to look for Sita. After a couple of days of tough trekking, they reached another area exactly the opposite to what they had just experienced. This was a thick forest encompassed by tall trees and thick shrubs. The monkeys were glad to see some greenery after such a treacherous patch of dryness. Knowing well that there had to be a source of water somewhere in the vicinity, they started searching the area rapidly. Relieved to find a lake in the precincts of the forest, they rushed toward it. As they bent low to drink the clear water, the reflection of a huge figure appeared on the surface of the lake. On the opposite bank of the lake stood a huge fiendish demon with shoulders that resembled the boulders of a mountain. On seeing him standing there and menacingly observing them, the vanaras stood up, tightened their waist belts and steadied themselves for a good fight. The sheer size of the demon was overwhelming.

When the demon saw thousands of vanaras spread around the lake, he let out a thunderous cry, which sounded like a cry of joy. Clenching his fists, he began to scream at the top of his voice. “Stay where you are! All of you are dead now! Don’t try to escape your death.”

He began running toward them right through the lake. The lake was very deep, but when he stepped into it, the water was just knee deep. With every step, he splashed huge quantities of water. The lake began to flood its banks as the stomping of the demon’s legs created wave after wave. He held his hands up high in the air as he ran toward them. It almost seemed as if he was tossing the clouds around with his hands. Such was his size! Just as he reached the middle of the lake, torrential rain began pouring all of a sudden. At this point, he resembled a mountain with streams of water flowing from it. His long hair, sharp fangs and reddish fiery eyes left all the monkey soldiers mortified.

Realizing that if this demon wasn’t handled quickly, he had the potential to wipe out the entire army, Angad decided to act. With a wave of his hand, he signalled the monkeys to step back. This would be his fight. As the monkeys took a step backward, Angad stepped forward. “This must be Ravana.” Angad muttered under his breath and rushed toward the approaching demon.

The titans clashed right in the middle of the lake. Angad had leaped off the edge of the lake and landed straight on the chest of the advancing demon. Mounting with his knee on its chest, Angad delivered a heavy blow to the demon’s chest. The power of the punch was so enormous that blood splattered from the demon’s body all over as he collapsed backward. He fell right in the middle of the lake with such force that all the water from the lake splashed out. Angad’s punch resembled that of a mountain battered by lightning. Seeing the demon dead, Angad jumped back. The fallen demon looked like an inverted mountain. The monkeys jumped in joy, celebrating the victory of their hero.

Just as Angad was rejoicing that he had killed Ravana, Jambavan stepped forward and revealed to him that the demon that he had just slayed wasn’t Ravana, but rather Dhumra. The celebrations abruptly stopped as soon as they heard this new information and the search resumed. The vanarasena searched high and low. They climbed every tree in the forest, trekked every mountain and ran through every cave in that forest. Still nothing!

After an intense search of the entire area, all the monkeys gathered at the base of a huge tree. They all sat dejected, staring at the ground. Their energy was low and their hope even lower. Seeing the distraught condition of his army, Angad tried to fuel them with courage and optimism.

“We are all together here for a single cause. A long time has elapsed since we left our homes and yet we haven’t found a single clue. Know for sure that our master Sugriva is a strict disciplinarian and will not accept exhaustion as a reason for our failure. We have to continue our journey until we reach the tip of the southern regions as Sugriva has instructed us. Let not submit to fatigue, surrender to remorse or slip into laziness. These lethargies may come upon us frequently, but let us utilize the power of our collective mental strengths. Alone we may give up, but together we never will. Let us all focus our thoughts on only one thing: Finding Mother Sita.

“Results don’t come only by wishing for them. Success is a result of persevering genuinely with a determined heart in the right direction. Even with the greatest positive thinking, our mission will not get any easier, but our determination not to accept defeat will get stronger. Drop your lethargy and let us continue on our mission with renewed zeal. We will stop only when we have attained our goal. If inspiration doesn’t inspire us, let us wake up to pursue our goal and know for sure that the wrath of Sugriva is the second option. Know for sure that he is a ruthless prosecutor. In your interest, I command all of you to rise to your feet. Come let us continue with our mission.”256

The first to rise to his feet was Gandhamadana. Although his lips were parched with thirst, and he was famished with the travels, he realized Angad was right. “I totally agree with what Prince Angad says. Let us carry out our duty diligently. We have already covered so much distance. Mahendra mountain is just a little ahead of us. That’s the last destination before the southern ocean. Let us make one last leap of effort to search all the land masses until we reach the southern ocean.”

Intellect and Inspiration

In the journey to find Sita, Angad is in the front and Hanuman the tail end of the troop. Angad represents the power of thought and therefore has to be in the front to give direction to everyone through powerful thoughts. Hanuman is pavanatanay or son of the wind god and represents the power of inspiration. When wind propels from the front, it creates hurdles, but when it propels from behind it drives everyone ahead faster.

One by one, the monkeys began to rise. Soon the search resumed in full swing with Angad leading the army from the front and Hanuman watching the tail end. The leaders made sure they did not press too close to one another or too far apart. This way they ensured that they didn’t lose anyone in the deepest of forests and yet at the same time guaranteed an extensive search of the entire area.

As they scouted the whole mountain range, they came across a massive cavity. The cavity was unusually dark and dingy. They all concluded that this cavity had some secret that could help them in their mission. As they peered into the dark cavity curiously, a sudden flutter startled them. Suddenly a flock of swans and krouncha birds flew out of the cavity. The unexpected flutter frightened the peering monkeys, who stumbled backward to avoid the birds crashing into their faces. As the birds whizzed past them, droplets of water from their wings sprinkled on their faces. The smell of the drops hinted at a hidden source of water filled with fragrant lotus flowers. Initially, from the look of the cavity, some monkeys suggested that this could be the pathway to naraka or hellish lower planets. But now, with the possibility of a water source inside, the same monkeys deduced that this was perhaps a secret pathway to the heavens.257

Seeing the hesitation of the monkeys to walk into the darkness, Hanuman stepped ahead. Probably for the first time in their journey from Kishkinda, Hanuman chose to speak. “Don’t worry, there can be nothing too dangerous in this cave. Whatever is most dangerous, we have already faced it. I assure you that this cave will lead us to our goal. My advice is that we should enter the cave in a single file. Each one will hold the tail of the vanara in front. I will lead the way.”

As Hanuman stepped into the dark cavity, he realized that it was darker than the darkest thing he had ever seen. It was pitch black. So intense was the darkness that beyond a certain point they couldn’t even see their own hands. Each monkey held onto the tail of the monkey before him, not wanting to let go and risk getting lost in this dark wilderness. Hanuman groped around with his arms outstretched, trying to find his way into the cavity. He was also as directionless as the others were, but he had great hope in the mission of Lord Rama. Right from the beginning, Hanuman was convinced that the entire mission was guided by the will of Lord Rama. He knew that Rama’s will would prevail. That single realization inspired him to continue stealthily farther into the darkness of the cavity. Although they were thousands walking into the cavity in a file, there was absolute silence. In fact, they were barely breathing. Surprisingly, the length of the cavity seemed neverending. They walked in the dark for hours and hours without any idea of what lay ahead or what to expect to find inside.258 259

Just when Hanuman was about to reconsider his decision, his outstretched hands hit upon a wall. It was a dead end! All this for nothing! Wanting to double-check before passing down the news, Hanuman groped in the dark to find another opening or passage. As he moved his palms along the length and breadth of the wall in front of him, he felt a smoother surface. Hanuman stepped ahead, following that even, slick feeling under his palms. He realized that it was a curve. There has to be a passage from the side of the wall. Hanuman was convinced that if he pulled himself along the wall, he might uncover something. By then, the vanaras had realized that Hanuman had hit upon something, because his movements had changed and so had his direction. Instead of moving straight, he was now moving perpendicularly. They silently followed him in a line.

With each step they took, the passage became narrower and had more frequent zigzags. Suddenly, Hanuman spotted something and stopped abruptly, colliding with the unsuspecting vanara behind and causing a domino effect of rear end crashes. Although, this unsettled and chafed everyone, Hanuman was smiling. As he absorbed the sight ahead of him, he gasped audibly. This unlikely and loud gasp from Hanuman silenced the irate and bickering stream of vanaras behind him. “Jai Shri Rama!” Through that silence rang these three words that Hanuman had uttered.

Hanuman then walked ahead, with the monkey troop following him. As each one took that last turn and came to the opening Hanuman had just entered, each let out a gasp. What they saw was beyond their wildest imagination.

The tiny cave had opened up into an entire city! By the look of it, this had to be the most opulent and sophisticated city any of them had ever seen. The whole landscape of the city had a golden sheen, as if the sun’s rays were radiating throughout the city. Even the trees had a golden hue. Although there was so much glitter and radiance in this place, not one ray of light had reached the dark cave. The cave was fashioned in a way to conceal this magnificent mountain city from the world. The monkeys couldn’t believe that an entire city of such magnificence could be constructed inside a mountain, forever hidden from the world.

Trees such as the tamala, punnaga, vanjula, dhava, champaka, naaga and karnikara dotted the city. These trees had gold trunks studded with valuable gems, and golden flowers and golden fruits hung from their branches. All sorts of melodious birds circled around those trees. The crystal clear waters of the lakes had lotuses with broad golden petals that shone like the sun. Shimmering fish frolicked in the glistening waters. On the banks of those lakes stood magnificent mansions of the kind the monkeys had never seen before. The domes of each of those palaces were jaw-droppingly huge. The mansions had golden windows with lacework made of pearls. Honeybees buzzed around, gathering golden pollen from golden lotuses.

All across the city, near the lakes, below the flower trees and in the gardens were diverse seats and opulent beds strategically arranged to provide comforts in a natural setting. Next to these seats were utensils made of gold, silver and bell metal, in which flavored drinks were served. There was lots of honey to go around and more. Exotic fabrics of various embroidered material and animal skin were available freely. Piles of eatables, fruits, roots, bulbs, stems, sandalwood sticks and aloes were available in unlimited quantities. Although the sun’s rays could not penetrate the city, the city shone as the sun itself.

Some monkeys began suggesting that they had all actually died of hunger and thirst and reached heaven after death. When they looked at each other, they noticed that their eyes still blinked, which meant they couldn’t be in the heavens, where people didn’t blink. Moreover, they all could recall all the incidents in this life, which meant that they weren’t reborn either.260

Although the city was opulent, it was empty and devoid of any life force. Everything was well maintained, tidy, but, surprisingly, there wasn’t a single soul around. They wondered who maintained this place without the number of personnel a city like that would need to sustain such magnificence. They didn’t come across any such person. Presuming this could well be the abode of Ravana, they set about combing the city for Sita. After scouring the length and breadth of the city with no luck, the monkeys concluded that this had to be an illusory city. If this were indeed an illusory city, there had to be illusory people. Hanuman was convinced that they were missing some vital point. He began to look more closely and more objectively. Then all of a sudden, he realized that they had forgotten to mark the entry point. They were so sucked in by the beauty of the golden glittering city that they lost track of the entry point. There was no way to return now. The only way out was to find someone to guide them out of this illusory maze.261

Just as they were wondering about this, they spotted something that was completely out of sync with these heavenly surroundings – someone seated under a tree right in the middle of a luxurious palace courtyard.

An emaciated woman, dressed in black garments of coarse material and wrapped in deerskin, sat in a yogic posture becoming of an advanced ascetic. As everything else in the city, she was effulgent like the sun. Her partially visible face was as bright as the full moon. Ascetic luminescence radiated from every inch of her body. Her frail frame was proof that she had been practicing severe austerities for years on end. Dust had accumulated all over her. Her eyes were semi-shut, focusing on the tip of her nose. Only her eyes were visible on her cloth-covered face. Her lotus-bud-like hands were placed straight on her knees. There was absolutely no trace of any sort of attachment in her. The mode of goodness prevailed inside and out with no signs of passion or ignorance in her persona. Matted locks of curly hair cascaded down her delicate shoulders to the ground behind her. She exuded motherly affection and seemed to be an abode of compassion.

For a split moment, a thought flashed through Hanuman’s mind. Could this be the divine Mother Sita they were madly searching for? He then carefully compared her looks to Rama’s description of her. Instantly, he ruled out that possibility and moved on to his next task: Who is this ascetic woman? Could she be of any help in the mission?262

With a soft and soothing voice Hanuman spoke, “O divine mother! May we know who you are and where we are? We entered a blind cavity, but how did we end up in this illusory city? Whose is this spectacular city?”

She had seen it all through her yogic vision. Although fully focussed in her yogic trance, she wasn’t unconscious of the world around her. She was aware of the intrusion right from the moment the monkeys entered her abode, and had been waiting for them to find her once through with the razzle-dazzle of the city. She knew that if one wasn’t looking for substance, one wouldn’t be able to find her at all. Most people, mesmerized and carried away by the city’s pleasures, never really spotted her, although she was always there. If these monkeys had been able to spot her within such short a span of entering the illusory city, it only meant that they had been searching for substance and not shadows. It only meant that they were on the lookout for something more valuable than the city could offer. It only meant that they were steeped in a mission much higher and beyond the illusory distractions that could trap only those that were already sidetracked.263

When Hanuman saw her opening her eyes and glance at them with great compassion, he decided to question further. “We entered the cavity in a famished state, desperately in need of food and water. Little did we realize that we were walking into a totally different world. We are eager to know who created this artificial reality. Is this a manifestation of your austerities and penances? Is it the dominion of some powerful demon? We entered this place halfconfused and half-convinced, not knowing what to expect, or how to behave. Kindly enlighten us with knowledge about this magical city and yourself.”

With a smile on her face, the old but extremely beautiful ascetic glanced lovingly at the troop of monkeys standing around her. She spoke with a soothing and magical voice. “Before I answer your questions, I want an answer to mine. I want to know who you are and what mission you are on?”

Hanuman went on to explain everything about how they were all in the service of Lord Rama whose wife Sita had been abducted by the demon king Ravana. He also described how King Sugriva had divided the monkey army into four parts and sent each group to each compass direction in search of Sita. He narrated their particular predicament at present. Their thirty-day deadline was running out on them and they still had so much area to cover. More importantly, there was still no hint about Sita’s whereabouts. They were mentally and physically dead – beaten by their failures, hunger and thirst. The cavity had appeared exactly when the distraught vanarasena had given up all hope. They had ventured with the hope that they would find Sita and replenishments, but instead all they found was an illusory world where everything, including water, was an illusion. Hanuman explained that now they weren’t even sure if the birds they saw flying out of the cave were real.264

On hearing the plight of the monkeys and the importance of their mission, the yogini began to shed tears. She was moved by the vanaras’ dedication to Lord Rama despite His sad plight. She wanted to help them in her own small way, so she clapped her hands. The next instant, an unlimited quantity of the choicest of fruits, tuber and beverages appeared. The monkeys were overjoyed with the abundance of food. In front of them were the tastiest of fruits from every corner of the earth. Unable to contain their hunger and thirst anymore, the monkeys pounced on the food. They overate and over-drank more than they ever had in their lives. They stopped only when no more food or drink could pass through their throats.

The mystical yogini sat smiling, looking at the innocent monkeys relishing the meal she had arranged. Once they were satiated, she started to speak. Only when they heard her voice did they become aware of their boorish behavior during the eating spree. But what could anyone do when tormented by hunger and thirst?265

“My name is Swayamprabha and I am the daughter of Merusvarni.” She introduced herself to the vanaras and began her story. “This place is nothing but a storehouse of bad memories, a lesson for those in pursuit of self-establishment. Three people lived here whose aim in life was supremacy, but they lost their lives to two others running the same race. This is the story of how a father killed a father and the son killed the sons.”266

As soon as Hanuman heard these words, it struck a chord. These words seemed as familiar as the story itself.

Swayamprabha continued her narration. “This is Hemapura.”

As soon as he heard that word, everything fell into place for Hanuman. He began to recollect how Sugriva’s life was connected so intimately to this place. It looked like the higher powers wanted the story to reach its climax at this very place.

“This magical city was created by the master of illusion, Mayasura. He made this beautiful city as an offering to his most beloved wife Hema, an apsara from the heavenly realms. I came here with Hema on the day of her marriage. The happy couple lived here for many years and had three children –two sons, Dundubhi and Mayavi, and an extremely beautiful daughter, Mandodari. Mandodari was married off to the very demon that has kidnapped Rama’s Sita. Being envious of Mayasura’s intellectual capacity and architectural abilities, Indra eliminated him by trickery. Indra was upset about two things that Mayasura did: he brought an apsara from the heavens without his sanction, and he became the master architect of the three worlds through trickery. Vali, the son of Indra and brother of your king Sugriva, killed Dundubhi and Mayavi in a ruthless massacre. Vali was upset with them because they had challenged him for a duel.267

“The city lost its charm with the death of her sons and husband. Finding no reason to continue living in this city filled with the memories of her loved ones, Hema decided to return to the heavenly realms. She handed over the possession and charge of this splendid city to me and left for the heavens. When she left, Indra appeared and informed me that I could return to the heavens the day a troop of monkeys come searching for Sita. Once I served them, I could get passage back to heaven. From that moment forth, I have been waiting for this day. Wealth, enjoyment, luxuries, comforts, had lost their meaning as soon as I witnessed the downfall of my mistress Hema in this very place. The only thing of any consequence to me now is the desire to attain perfection in my life by serving Lord Rama through his representatives. Now tell me how can I serve you, apart from what I have already done?”

Hanuman was very impressed with the humility of this saintly woman. He was even more impressed with the detached spirit with which she lived amid such opulence. She was detached from all things and personified detachment itself. He understood that she had the solutions to their predicament. After thanking her for her kindness in relieving them of the misery of hunger and thirst, he requested her to relieve them from the misery of losing their direction and goal. He told her that they were worried about missing Sugriva’s deadline for their return. They needed to resume their search so that they could get back on time and escape Sugriva’s wrath. After making his request, he said, “Please ask for something you feel we could do for you in reciprocation for all your kindness.”268

Swayamprabha smiled at Hanuman’s mention about the deadline. They were in for a surprise very soon. She replied with wise words. “O vanara-hero, service should always be selfless. Anything done with a desire to gain something in return ceases to be selfless service. I do not expect anything from you in return. There is no physical way to get out of this illusory city. The way you entered is not the way out; that route is now sealed forever. There is only one way to exit Hemapura now: the yogic way!”269

The monkeys were surprised by her perplexing proposition. They had no clue about the method she was suggesting. Noticing their confusion, Swayamprabha asked all of them to close their eyes. She explained to them that they had to have complete faith in her abilities and not even attempt to see what she was about to do. It was impossible to exit this place with open eyes. She warned them that anyone who disobeyed her instructions would forever remained sealed in this cavity.

With all those warnings, no monkey had even the remotest thought of wanting to see what was going to take place. They all closed their eyelids and covered their eyes with their hands to ensure the double sealing of vision. Swayamprabha murmured Vedic incantations, and each monkey felt himself being lifted into the air and moving at breakneck speed. Of course, they couldn’t decide if this was real or if it was their imagination. This lasted for a few minutes until they heard her voice ring in their ears.

“Open your eyes now!”

When they opened their eyes, they were shocked beyond belief to find themselves atop a mountain facing the southern ocean with clear blue sky above them, deep blue ocean below and a small dark cavity behind them. What was most bewildering was that the season was so different from when they had entered the cave. When they calculated the time spent, they came to the mind-numbing conclusion that the few hours that they felt they had spent within that cavity was in fact almost a month!

That explained the smile on Swayamprabha’s face when Hanuman talked about missing the deadline. That deadline was long gone. Now that there was no possibility of returning on time, they might as well complete the task without bothering about deadlines. Were they to return with some good news after the time limit, Sugriva might spare them. As the vanara leaders began contemplating their future actions, Swayamprabha sought their leave and disappeared into that small cavity, never to be seen again.

Darkness to Light

Swayamprabha in this episode acts like a guru who brings the disciples from darkness to light, from death to life and from illusion to reality. The cave represents the unreal, illusory world in which the monkeys were hungry and desperate. She saves them at the nick of time and brings them back on track. Although she helps them come out of the cave, she does not find Sita for them. That is up to them to manage. The role of a guru is to show direction and not spoonfeed the disciple with everything that needs to be done.

The golden city is also symbolic of the distractions and temptations around us that leave the mind restless and lost. Swayamprabha’s asking the vanaras to close their eyes and completely trust her is symbolic of how meditation and total faith in a higher force can lead us away from all temptations and distractions, calm our minds to take the right decisions in life and eventually helps us find our way.

angad’s fast

Days passed as the vanara sena wandered aimlessly wandering about the shores of the southern ocean. The hot sands burned their feet during the day and the cold winds wrecked their bones at night. Although Swayamprabha had been kind enough to get them out of that complicated maze, she hadn’t been able to help them beyond that. With not a soul in the vicinity, the monkeys had lost their bearings. Dissent began to raise its ugly head within the army. Negativity originated from the very person who was supposed to be the fountain of positivity, hope and direction.270

Angad had become too volatile with the variations in temperature, plans, failures, hunger, thirst, unpredictability, uncertainty and hopelessness that they had all been experiencing simultaneously. A very intense fear had begun to grip him from within –the same fear that he felt when he lost his father, that of insecurity and uncertainty. When he couldn’t contain his fears any longer, he began to blurt out his thoughts, causing mayhem among the already confused vanara soldiers.

“My dear friends, I have been appointed as your leader; therefore, it is imperative I expose you to this reality. Our king Sugriva is not only a taskmaster but also extremely cruel to those who do not follow his directives. All of you know well that we are behind schedule by a good one month; we have no clues about Sita’s whereabouts yet and we have no sense of direction now. The only thing that is certain at this point is that once we return with news of failure, Sugriva will execute all of us. If death is certain, then I want a dignified death. I don’t want to die a miserable, humiliating death in my home with my family and friends watching. I would rather undertake a fast until death on the shores of this holy ocean than be painfully tortured to death. I prefer committing suicide instead. My father handed me over to Rama and not Sugriva, so I want to remain loyal to Rama forever. But I won’t be able to face Him without any news of Sita. Sugriva may be the king, but he is my father’s enemy first. If he sees me transgressing his instructions and returning a failure, he wouldn’t spare this chance at eliminating the last trace of his enemy’s lineage and using my failure as a pretext to justify his decision. I don’t want to be victimized in this way. At the prime of my youth, I do not want to experience this torture and pain. It’s better that I die painlessly by fasting. All of you may go back to Kishkinda, I will patiently sit here awaiting my death. If anyone wants to accompany me on this dignified journey, you are welcome.”271

Hearing their leader speak thus, commotion spread through the vanara ranks. How were they supposed to react to this proposal? Some began to voice their thoughts. They all sat in concentric circles on the sandy beach. The sun was setting slowly, as was the morale of the vanarasena. One of the few empathetic to their prince, spoke first. “Sugriva is definitely hot-tempered, and Rama is definitely interested in the welfare of His beloved wife. Returning to Kishkinda right now will only result in death. Sugriva will eliminate us to show his concern for Rama’s suffering. But it is true that if we don’t report soon enough, Sugriva will send his men to find us and terminate the defaulters. A dignified death as suggested by Prince Angad is the only option left for us now.”

Just then, Taru got up and suggested something diametrically opposite. “Enough of this desperation! Why should we die either way? Why don’t we think of a way to live instead? Let’s find a way back into that cavity and live a life of joy in that illusory city built by Mayasura. Neither the fear of Sugriva nor the torment of hunger can disturb us as long as we are there. No one will ever find us there either.”272

The vanara soldiers began to hoot and shout in approval of Taru’s idea. None of them wanted to die. Seeing the chaos that prevailed in the army, Hanuman realized he had to do something to bring order back in the army. He stood up and looked at all the vanaras that were around him – forlorn faces, emaciated bodies, tongues hanging dry and drooping eyes. He realized that they were delirious and weren’t speaking in their right mind.

“O Prince Angad, we consider you the most intelligent like your mother Tara and a most capable warrior like your father Vali. Please understand that the monkeys would not want to die unnecessarily. Leaders such as Jambavan, Nila and Suhotra are committed to the cause of Sugriva and would not tolerate any deviation from their master’s plans. Failure and death in the hands of their master is preferable to them over treason and treachery. Do not think the cavity, the mystical Hemapura city or any other place in the world is safe enough from the arrows of Lakshmana. Rama need not even interfere in this case. Lakshmana’s missiles alone are enough to destroy the deepest of hideouts. Don’t you remember how that single arrow of Rama had penetrated seven sala trees and travelled through the mountains and the earth itself? But we need not worry about punishment. Sugriva is a kind king. He will surely understand our attempt and the predicament that has led to our failure. After being together through such odds for two whole months, why should we split now? Why should we lose hope? Why shouldn’t we have faith in Lord Rama and the power of his blessings? I strongly believe that the solution to our problems lies in remembering the Lord intensely.”273

Hanuman’s inspiring words invigorated the monkey heroes. They decided not to give up so easily. Just then, one of the monkeys stood up and requested Hanuman to narrate Lord Rama’s story in detail. Because they had nothing else to do except wait for some miracle to happen, they thought it best to use that time hearing about their master. All these days they had heard about Rama being a great person, but they had never had a chance to understand how great. With a smile on his face, Hanuman began narrating the story of Lord Rama, right from the beginning.

Hours passed by as the monkeys listened to the Ramayana from Hanuman with rapt attention. The energy levels of the monkeys were dwindling as the sky turned dark. They were unaware that hovering way above them, a flock of vultures was waiting to devour their soon-to-be meal of monkey carcasses.

The story had reached its climax, and this was by far the most exciting, yet gut-wrenching part of the story. “…Jatayu decided to fight Ravana although he knew that he had no chance against this mammoth demon. He sacrificed his life trying to protect Mother Sita. We must pursue our search and fall like Jatayu who gave up his life for the sake of protecting Her. Life is worth living only when lived like Jatayu – for the sake of others.”

As soon as Hanuman spoke these words, they heard a deafening, unfamiliar noise.

SCHRRREEEEEECCHHHHHHH!

## Chapter 9

Hanuman Reboots

sampati’s wings

A drop of water fell on his cheek. On looking carefully, he realized it was a teardrop. Why would a demon cry? Or was it perspiration, not tears? The matter seemed very different from what they had originally perceived it to be. He decided to investigate it himself before coming to any conclusions. However, it turned out that he didn’t have to do anything at all! The next moment, all his doubts were allayed.

Hanuman walked closer to the foothill of that mountain and strained his neck to look up. High up on the mountain, on the edge of a cliff was the largest, most ferocious-looking vulture he had ever seen. Had he not observed it carefully, he would have assumed that this was also the meanest vulture in the universe. But Hanuman’s eyes revealed a different story. From where he stood, Hanuman could clearly see the creature’s eyes. Tears were streaming down from those big moist eyes. Carried gently by the soft breeze that came from the ocean, these saline tears were drizzling everywhere.274

The vulture rested its neck on the edge of the cliff and hung his head out of it. His huge body rested on the cliff dangerously. Hanuman began to climb the mountain to have a clearer conversation with the vulture. As he climbed a few steps, he heard a great moan from way above. The vulture was surely in great pain of some sort. Is it physical? Hanuman couldn’t at all be sure.

Meanwhile, the monkeys headed by Angad had begun to prepare for a gruesome battle with this gigantic vulture. Going by its mammoth size, they knew that just power alone wouldn’t be enough to counter this bird. They would have to complement their strength with intelligent war tactics. Seeing the monkeys rallying around, trying to wage an attack on him, the vulture groaned heavily. The sound of that moan was so intense that everyone froze with fear. This vulture was definitely in pain. The vanaras dropped their weapons and stood still with wide-eyed wonder and gaping mouths, looking at this bawling vulture.275

The vulture began to look toward the setting sun. Painful memories emerged in front of its eyes. Everything seemed to have occurred just yesterday. Although it had all been years earlier, he had relived those painful memories every single day from that fateful day forth. He could still hear the tremble in his own voice when he yelled. He could still see the horror that his brother was rushing into. He could still feel his skin burning as he tried to protect his younger brother. His life had changed in just a few moments. That one single adventure his brother had dived into had separated them for life and perpetually crippled him. Whenever he thought of his younger brother and that unfortunate event, a myriad of emotions erupted in his mind – sometimes anger, sometimes guilt, sometimes helplessness, sometimes the pain of separation, sometimes worry, and sometimes fear, but always and invariably lots of love.276

Other than his huge size and frightening looks, nothing made this vulture seem dangerous or threatening to Hanuman. In fact, his facial expressions revealed a very docile and genuinely loving being. Hanuman decided to begin a dialogue with him to try to understand what he was going through. Maybe by helping him, they would manage to find an unforeseen hint into what they were looking for. “You seem to be in pain. Is there anything we can do to help you?” Not wanting to sound too intimidating or too unconcerned, Hanuman asked him aloud from where he was standing.

“Did I hear correctly? Did you mention that Jatayu is dea…?” He choked while uttering the word dead, and then couldn’t complete his sentence. Without saying a word, Hanuman nodded his head in affirmation. The bird began to wail pitifully and said, “He was my brother, Jatayu. I am Sampati. We are both sons of Syeni and Aruna, the charioteer of the sun god. Destiny separated us from each other after a terrible mishap many years ago. In an attempt to save my brother from the scorching heat of the sun, I burned my wings. The heavy freefall from the sky after that left me with a crushed spirit and no strength in my legs. So today I can neither walk nor fly. I can only crawl painfully when I have to move. I live in a cave on these mountains under the care of my son Suparshva. When I saw so many weary looking monkeys, I felt that you would serve as an easy meal for days to come. I was thus keeping a keen eye, making sure that the other vultures who hovered over you, waiting for you to die, kept away from my catch. Somehow, as I kept waiting, I heard you mention my brother several times. After so many painful years of separation, this is the first I heard anyone speak of my brother. At first, I was deliriously happy believing that he had survived that mishap. I felt happy knowing that my sacrifice hadn’t gone in vain. But my happiness lasted only until you began to discuss his death.”277

Sampati broke down. By this time, Hanuman had climbed all the way up to the cliff and was sitting right by Sampati’s side, caressing his head. Sampati expressed his desire to come by the ocean side and offer water oblations for the benefit of his brother’s soul. Hanuman requested a division of the monkey army to come and help the bird down. They somehow managed to move the heavy crippled bird down the mountain all the way to the ocean. After offering his heartfelt oblations, Sampati sat down on the beach side along with the monkeys discussing his story. He was touched to hear that Lord Rama had himself conducted the last rites for his brother just as a son would for his departed father. Sampati was overwhelmed with mixed feelings, and he was fighting his emotions. The monkeys had been sensitive enough not to broach any other subject until he had cried his heart out. After a long time, Sampati stabilized and began to talk.278

He suddenly became very nostalgic. “You know what? He had predicted that this would happen centuries ago!” They couldn’t figure out whom he was talking about and what had he predicted. They decided to just wait and watch where this was leading. “He knew everything about everything. Eight thousand years ago, he had predicted that one day a group of monkeys would come looking for a woman. I clearly remember that he had said something to the effect that that would be the turning point in my life. Now I realize that subconsciously I had been waiting for today to happen. Although so often I have felt like ending my life, the prophecy of that great sage inspired me to continue living for this day.”

The monkeys were still clueless about who had made the prophecy. All they knew was that something unique was going to unfold soon, something that could turn the direction of their destinies for sure. Sampati continued his recounting his memories. “His name was Nishakara maharishi. He was a true friend to all. He did not discriminate in giving his love be it to an animal, bird or human. Everyone was a recipient of his kindness. When I fell from the skies with my charred wings and mangled feet, I fortunately fell near his ashram. I remained unconscious for six days. Since my brother and I would visit his ashram very often to seek his blessings in our youth, I knew where I was when I awoke.. I dragged myself to the ashram, and when the maharishi saw me in that half-dead condition, he nursed me back to health. His kindness gave me new hope to live just as it did for the lions, tigers, elephants, snakes, wild boars and all kinds of animals that surrounded him. In fact, when he walked down this mountain to the ocean to bathe, these animals would follow his trail like seasoned disciples. Only when he would go into a meditative trance for days would the animals return to their regular routine. As soon as they came to know that he had returned to external consciousness, they would all rush back to his side. We all hankered so much for his association. He especially considered me his child.279

“One day he revealed to me that it was time for him to depart to the higher realms and sought my leave. Before taking up permanent samadhi, he revealed to me that all of you would come here searching for Sita. He had told me that if I could somehow help you in some way, then I will be liberated from my suffering. He always told me that he could have easily cured me with his powers, but he wanted me to heal because of the power of my own good deeds. Of course, I now want to help you not with the intention that I will get freedom from my suffering, but with the desire that I will follow the footsteps of my late brother. I don’t want to let the cause for which he laid down his life be unfulfilled. I will surely help you locate Sita. Now that I think of it, it seems that I have been waiting for the last eight thousand years simply to be a part of this story. I will relate two more incidents that deeply connect me with your story.”280

The way he put it made the monkeys curious to know more. If all this was ordained, perhaps a solution, too, was predestined. On seeing the monkeys’ excitement building, Sampati continued narrating his account. “Many years ago, when I was lying on this very mountain, I heard a very painful shriek. I turned around just in time to see a huge plane come toward me on which was a powerful looking rakshasa held a beautiful yaksha woman tightly in his grip. She was desperately trying to get away from him. In the next moment, I raised myself up and using my body as a wall, I obstructed the movement of the plane. The impact was so great that the rakshasa lost his balance and fell off the plane. He ran toward me with a raised sword, threatening to annihilate me. With a swift kick of my weak legs, I tore open his belly. His sword flew off his hand into the bushes, and he remained in a helpless state in front of me. With my claws and beak, I fought with him although I had very limited movement in my limbs. Seeing my handicapped physical situation and my enormous power, he seized the moment and proposed a compromise. By then, I had also realized that although I had managed to stall him, I had no real strength to win against him. So I accepted his proposal. He released that woman, but in turn took a commitment from me that I would never impede him in his future ventures. That was when he introduced himself to be Lankeshwar Ravana. He assured me of protection from the rakashasa forces and left on his airplane that had repaired itself after the accident.

“Of course that was a story from my younger days. But now, because of old age, I am almost like an invalid with hardly any strength to move about. I am entirely dependent on my son, Suparshva, who caters to all my daily needs. I am sure you are aware that the gandharvas have more lust, snakes have more anger, animals have more fear and we birds have more hunger. A couple of months ago, one day my son came empty-handed and that too extremely late. My hunger got the better of me, and I flew into a rage. After I blasted my son, I calmed down and asked him how he could be so late and yet not get any food. He then told me of his tryst with Ravana in an airplane and the kidnapped woman. The thought that my son had confronted Ravana sent a shudder down my old spine.”281

The monkeys were amazed to hear that three people in the same family had similar interactions with Ravana under almost the same circumstances. Apparently, these vultures were around each time Ravana abducted a woman and whisked her away in his plane. The little story that Sampati narrated triggered their curiosity so much that they urged him to speak in detail.282

Sampati went on to narrate his son’s experience with Ravana. “My son told me that he was flying as usual to the Mahendra mountain, a sure spot to find a meal. He had hidden himself behind a cave that opened to the edge of a cliff on that mountain and was scanning the whole area to prey on a big sea animal or bird. After a while, he heard a woman wailing from the skies. When he looked up, he saw Ravana mounted on his pushpak vimana holding an extremely beautiful damsel who was in great distress. It seemed as if she was crying constantly. She kept calling out to Rama and Lakshmana for help. Seeing Her helplessness, my son pounced on the plane, stood on its wings and looked at Ravana menacingly. But Ravana did something uncharacteristic of himself – he pleaded with my son to let him go. My son became confused – a powerful demon begging? In that moment of indecisiveness and confusion, my son unmounted the airplane and let go. As soon as he saw his chance, Ravana dashed south over the ocean. My son then told me that while he was still weighing the righteousness of his actions. Celestials approached him and congratulated him for his quick and wise decision of not confronting Ravana, else the demon would have slayed him. I didn’t know whether to congratulate my son for remaining alive or to blast him for being a coward and letting that wild rogue abduct an innocent woman. After his encounters with two old vultures, Jatayu and I, Ravana probably didn’t want to enter into a conflict with a younger, bigger and more vigorous vulture and hence acted humbly. My son stupidly mistook his humility for genuineness and made the greatest blunder of his life. I have never chastised him so severely as I did that day. But do not worry, I will surely help you locate Sita.”283

The vanaras were so disappointed by Suparshva’s stupidity that they lost all interest in even seeing him, leave alone meeting him. Just then, something miraculous happened. Right in front of their eyes, new feathers began to emerge on the body of Sampati and a new pair of wings began to sprout. The vanaras looked on in total disbelief as Sampati began to flutter around with his new wings. Even the dishevelled weak legs of Sampati began to shape up and become rugged with strength. In a few moments, Sampati was standing on his own feet easily able to handle the weight of his huge body. He flapped his wings heavily, raising a dust storm on the beach. The monkeys bent over covering their faces with their hands. Soon the giant vulture was soaring high in the sky. In just a few seconds, he flew up so high that he seemed like a small speck in the vast sky.

He flew back down as quickly as he had flown up. Yet again, the monkeys bent down to save themselves from the sand raised by his flapping wings. As Sampati settled down among them, he seemed to have some important information to give them. “O great vanaras, I have some news for you! About hundred yojanas from here, right in the middle of the ocean lies a lavish island called Lanka. It was built by the celestial designer Vishwakarma himself. People also call it the golden city as all the multistoried buildings are built symmetrically and have a golden tinge. Even the doors of the buildings are made of solid gold. From the sky, the city seemed to dazzle like the sun. That’s where Ravana has housed Sita. In fact, I even saw her weeping in Ravana’s palace. Fierce-looking rakshasa women keep a watch on Her all the time. That island in which Ravana has held Sita is highly guarded and is impossible to enter without being spotted. I recommend only one of you to venture to that place and ascertain the exact presence of Sita. Once found, convey Rama’s message to her and quietly return without any misadventure.”

Such a precise and confident confirmation from Sampati seemed too unbelievable. He just flew into the sky for a few minutes and came back with such accurate information about a place that was hundred yojanas away. All this seemed too far-fetched and almost fabricated. When Sampati saw the monkeys’ doubt-ridden faces, he understood that this warranted a proper explanation. He said, “Different birds fly at different levels. The lowest level is that of house sparrows, kulinga and other songbirds that subsist on food grains. The second level is that of birds like crows and doves that eat leftovers and that of fruit-eaters like parrots. The third level is that of wading birds like bhaasa, krouncha, kurara, cranes and herons. The fourth is that of hawks and the fifth is that of vultures. The sixth flight level is that of the majestic swans who are known for their beauty, vigor and splendor. But the uppermost flight level is that of the sons of Vinata, namely Garuda and Aruna. Because Jatayu and I descend from the lineage of Aruna, we too possess the ability to soar easily to that height.”

In addition to that, he explained to them that they also had another ability called chakshushmatividya or the power of incredible vision. By the dint of this special knowledge, Sampati revealed that he could see up to a distance of hundred yojanas with crystal clarity.” After this explanation, it wasn’t difficult for the vanaras to comprehend his confidence about Sita’s presence in Lanka.284

This was an awkward moment. How was he supposed to leave just like that? Sampati wasn’t at all comfortable telling them that it was time for him to pursue his destiny and that they could pursue theirs. Hadn’t they been a part of his restoration process? Wasn’t he supposed to express his gratitude by helping them on their mission? Wasn’t this his mission too? Didn’t he want to take revenge on Ravana, who was his own brother’s killer? All these were true, but Sampati knew of a much higher truth: behind it all was a higher plan in action, a plan that had a role for everyone and that his role ended here. He wasn’t expected to interfere in the plan beyond this. “Good-bye, my friends.” Sampati whispered a fond adieu to his new friends and flew into the darkness of the night.

renewed vigor

On a fresh day, with fresh energy, the vanara chiefs gathered to set off and accomplish a fresh task. Each of them was rejuvenated physically and mentally. They now understood that their exhaustion and weakness were more the consequence of not having a direction and being torn amid confusion. At least this morning, they had clarity about what they had to do. All they needed now was clarity on how to go about it.

Taking the lead, Angad pumped up his army with words of encouragement. He was trying to make up for all the negativity he had been feeding them consciously or unconsciously. He spoke to his team with utmost confidence. “Remember, this mission is nearing completion. We now know where Sita is. We just have to reach Her and deliver Rama’s message to Her. Once through with that, we could return to Rama, who will then decide the future course of action. Right now, our only need is to identify a hero among ourselves who can cross the hundred yojana-long ocean and reach Lanka. If one among us claims to be able to accomplish this task, then our mission will almost be complete.”285

The question was not about whether they wanted to do it, but if they could. Faced with a new problem, the monkeys began to offer their bid. It was definitely a gamble – the gamble of life. All the monkeys in the army sincerely tried to display their strength and present their best skills in this match.286

Angad asked each of the vanaras for their best performance. Gaja was the first to make a claim. “I could do no more than ten yojanas.” Hearing this, Gavaaksha gained confidence in his abilities and said, “I can do about twenty.” Sharabha then added some more yojanas to that and said, “I could easily do up to thirty.” Gandhamadana chipped in his upper limit. “Fifty yojanas,” Mainda said, “Easily sixty!” Dvivida said, “I’ll reach seventy.” Sushena said he could attempt eighty. After this, there was silence. Noticing the coldness, Jambavan spoke up. He said that in his younger days, he could traverse the universe unobstructed, but at this stage in his life, he had to seek help even for his basic needs. Quickly disqualifying himself, Jambavan looked at Angad and said, “If there is anyone I feel is most qualified for such a dangerous mission, it is you, Angad.” As soon as Jambavan said that, Angad unconsciously took a step back. Now that all eyes turned toward him, Angad knew he had to say something.

Finally, Angad, the son of the powerful hero Vali said, “I could cover the entire stretch of hundred yojana myself.” The entire army erupted in excitement. Here was a brave warrior confident of his abilities. However, the din died as soon as he uttered, “But...” Now what? The monkeys waited with baited breath until Angad continued after his pause.“…although I can cover the hundred yojanas at a stretch and reach Lanka, I surely wouldn’t have the strength to return.”287

Hearing his half-hearted answer, all the monkeys were disappointed and yet again began to feel hopeless. Silence prevailed. All of them had put forth their concerns but couldn’t arrive at a sensible conclusion. Dejected, they all lowered their heads in shame and started brooding over their inabilities. Jambavan realized that each was thinking only from an individual perspective not analyzing the strength of others. Jambavan began analyzing the strengths and weaknesses of each monkey in all the sections. When he studied them carefully, he realized many were very capable of undertaking this journey, but some unexplained fear of the rakshasas was preventing them from truly recognizing their true potential.288

As Jambavan turned around looking at each of the vanara heroes, he realized that one among them hadn’t had the chance to express his capacity at all. In fact, he had been sitting aloof from the whole troop. When Jambavan thought more deeply about the strengths of the silent Hanuman, he realized that this was the only person who could accomplish the task with ease. In fact right at the beginning of the expedition, Rama had handed over his ring to Hanuman and even described Sita to him in detail. For sure, this was ordained. However, Jambavan realized that Hanuman’s silence was not so much because he felt incapable but because he had no cognizance of his real capability. A childhood mistake had shrunken his abilities for life. Jambavan began to recount to the troops how Hanuman went through a transformation from being a prankster to a silent child.

The Meaning of Hanuman’s Silence

The acharyas explain what Hanuman’s exact thoughts were at this point in time. He was thinking about Angad asking all the monkey heroes to jump over the ocean when in fact Rama had given the ring to him to offer it to Sita along with His message. Hanuman’s thoughts then were that it didn’t matter to him whether anyone managed to jump over the ocean or not, he had to go anyway. If someone did manage the jump, he had to go along with him. If no one managed the jump, he had to jump alone. Either way, he had to go. This was all he was contemplating at this time.

“Early in his childhood, all demigods in the universe had blessed Hanuman with diverse strengths and abilities. Filled with this kind of power, the small child had no idea how to utilize it constructively, and neither did he have a guide who could channel his powers. He naturally used every ounce of his energy to cause destruction and despair. What was a source of entertainment for him became a cause of anxiety for others. Every day, Hanuman would be at the riverbank looking for new ways to harass people. The sages, hardened by austerities, were his soft targets. While most children stayed away from these sages fearing their supernatural powers, Hanuman felt naturally attracted to them. Of course, there was nothing constructive he offered them or gained from them. He did all this for fun. He would constantly look for some activity to occupy his restless, wild mind.289

“When the sages bathed, Hanuman would make big holes in their clothes. He would break their kamandalus by hitting them against tree trunks. He would then wait for them to come out so that he could watch their disturbed faces and derive sadistic pleasure. If they took longer to come out, he would enter the waters and pull their legs from underneath. The sages would panic, thinking it to be a crocodile attack. Sometimes, if he saw them standing next to the riverbank and offering prayers to the sun with closed eyes, he would jump onto their shoulders and pull their long beards. His favorite trouble-game would take place after the sages came out of the river freshly bathed. He would be waiting for them patiently. He would quickly fill his mouth with water and squirt it out at them. Being thus contaminated, the sages would be forced to bathe again. This loop was repeated several times with a single sage, sometimes for hours together.

“Finally, fed up of being harassed by this maniac at the riverbank, the sages decided to abandon their regular spots, and each found a different water source for baths. After several days of drought in his fun, Hanuman understood what the intention of the sages was. He decided to venture into their hermitages for his daily quota of fun and entertainment.

“One day, the sages were engaged in a serious discussion on the scriptures when Hanuman entered their ashram. He was seated on a tree a little away from the meeting place. Suddenly, his eyes fell on a bunch of succulent fruits hanging on the tree under which these sages were seated. He jumped on to the tree, but the branch on which he landed snapped. Just in time, he managed to jump off and land on another branch, which broke off, too. Hanuman managed to save himself from a fall each time, but he couldn’t prevent the two broken branches from crashing right into the assembly of sages. Although no one was hurt, the sages were furious with this mischief-maker.

“There were many such incidents, but one crossed the sages’ collective threshold of tolerance. Once a little before dawn-break, Hanuman ventured into the forest in search of enjoyment. He spotted a tiger fighting an elephant. He walked up to them, and to their shock, held the elephant by its tusks, pulling it away from the tiger. He then held the tiger by its tail with his other hand and began to drag the two stunned beasts. After dragging them quite a distance, he tied them to two poles on either side of the door of an ascetic’s hermitage. This done, Hanuman hid himself to watch the fun.

“Soon, the ascetic, Trinabindu, opened his door to go out and freshen himself up. Because the sun hadn’t risen yet, it was still quite dark. He was used to the routine, however, and knew how to navigate his way through the darkness. As he stepped out of the doorway with his water-pot, he was startled and frightened by the sight and roar of a tiger standing right at the entrance. He shrieked, dropped his pot, rushed back into the hut and locked the door. He usually suffered from constipation, but today’s incident had frightened him such that he felt an urgent need to release. Knowing that a tiger was tied to a post, he gathered enough courage and attempted an exit, making sure he had stepped as far away from the tiger as possible. But in doing so, he had shifted dangerously close to the wild elephant, only to suffer another bout of shock. He screamed again and ran indoors.

“By this time, Trinabindu was writhing in agony from the turmoil within his body and the catastrophe outside his house. He had no idea who had played this wild prank. He was boiling with rage, when he heard peals of laughter nearby. He ran up to his window and saw Hanuman rolling on the ground, holding his stomach and laughing.290

“In extreme rage and with the desire to prevent the restless monkey from offending other revered sages, Trinabindu picked up his kamandalu, dipped his fingers inside it, scooped up some water and hurled it at Hanuman. He had pronounced a curse: ‘You impudent child, your power and speed have made you very proud. Because no one knows how to channel this power and speed for you, you are misusing it and causing others harm. I curse that you will forget all your powers until you are reminded of them by someone.’

“Hanuman became quiet as soon as the sage finished declaring the curse. He began to look around, unable to comprehend how he had reached the forest and what he was doing lying on the ground. He began to contemplate the purpose of his life and how to make productive use of his time.291

“Hanuman continued to remain contemplative right through his childhood and youth. As Jambavan looked at this charismatic personality, he realized that he had an important role to play in the life of Hanuman. It was time to remind Hanuman of his unlimited prowess. He had a purpose to achieve now, a mission to fulfill and a master to dedicate his life to. Now that would be appropriate channelization of that power.”292

Jambavan sat next to Hanuman and began to urge Hanuman to remember what he had forgotten. “Hanuman, your broken hanu is a reminder of the numerous blessings the gods had bestowed upon you when you were a child. Remember the supernatural abilities, the greatness, the brain, the brawn, the knowledge and the immense strength you have been blessed with. Remember none compares with you in valor except Garuda. Remember you were given great powers, so you must know that greater responsibility rests on you. Power and responsibility go hand in hand. As you stand on the shores of responsibility, your powers are bound to return. Get up and take the leap across the ocean at supersonic speed. The entire vanarasena is anxious to witness your prowess. The army is dispirited and needs your show of power to regain inspiration. Arise, O vanara hero, Hanuman! Arise, O pavanaputra Hanuman!”293

As Jambavan spoke, Hanuman began recollecting his lost and forgotten powers. He could feel an immense and invigorating rise in confidence. Soon, he began to grow in size. Within moments, Hanuman was a few hundred feet tall. The other monkeys looked up in amazement at the sudden transformation of their friend. Hanuman roared. The tress began to tremble, forcing birds to take off in different directions. The monkeys began to cheer him. Hanuman then began lashing his tail on the ground, creating tremors.

Hanuman began to run toward Mount Mahendra, covering the distance in a few strides. He climbed the mountain in easy steps and reached the peak in a flash. Standing right at its summit, he yelled, “Victory to Lord Rama! Jai Shri Rama! I am the son of the wind god and equal to him in strength and speed. There is nothing I can’t do. I can touch the sun during the noon heat. I can dry up the oceans. I can pulverize the mountains with my fists. I can snatch the elixir from the hands of Brahma and the thunderbolt from the hands of Indra. Nothing can stop me from achieving success in my Lord’s mission! I will find Sita today itself and offer Lord Rama’s ring to Her and convey His message.”

Standing atop the highest peak of the Mahendra mountains, Hanuman looked southward toward where Lanka lay. He prepared for the big leap. He gathered all his energy in his feet and put immense pressure on the mountain on which he stood. Streams of gold, silver and other precious minerals flowed out in different directions from the compressed mountain. Sages began running out of the caves, and animals began to move away from the pressure points.

The super-hero was born again. A new saga in his life was about to begin – a saga comparable to none in the past, present or future.

## Author Profile

Shubha Vilas, a spiritual seeker and a motivational speaker, holds a degree in engineering and law with a specialization in patent law.

In essence, he is an author, motivational speaker, lifestyle coach and storyteller par excellence. His keen interest in finding his ultimate goal in life led him to channelize his energies into deep studies and interpretations of the Vedic Scriptures.

He believes that a good teacher, no matter how knowledgeable, always sees the process of learning along with teaching as an inherent aspect of personal and spiritual growth. He teaches the importance of being governed by dharmic principles, meting out spiritual lifestyle tips and wisdom that can be applied to modernday situations.

His leadership seminars are popular among top-level management in many corporate houses. He addresses their crucial needs through thought-provoking discussions.

Apart from the corporate sector, his target audience is also the youth. He travels across the globe delivering talks to these young minds across many educational institutes, counseling and guiding them in the area of developing strong leadership skills, overcoming failures, and managing stress.

Compelled by his deep sense of responsibility towards society, he started writing books that would benefit the readers in all aspects by understanding ancient literature in its true essence.

He took on the monumental task of writing a book on the Ramayana that would bring the epic out of the closet and into the world, to show the lessons hidden in the tales. And thus, he embarked on the ambitious project of a six-volume series titled Ramayana – The Game of Life. The books combine the riveting story of the Ramayana with life-changing lessons to help you live contentedly.

His other bestsellers include Chronicles of Hanuman, which brings out the best and unheard stories from Hanuman’s life, and Openeyed Meditations, a delightful compilation of his thoughts wherein he meditates with his eyes wide open, deriving practical wisdom for life’s dilemmas through insights from the Ramayana and Mahabharata.
